GDB, sanitizers, Valgrind & rr — from the memory model up

Welcome

What you'll learn: how this guide is laid out, who it is for, and how to rebuild every lab and every terminal block in it on your own machine.

Who this is for

You work on a multi-threaded C daemon. Sometimes it crashes. Sometimes it hangs. Sometimes it just counts wrong. This guide teaches you to find out why, with GDB, the sanitizers, Valgrind and rr.

Two kinds of reader, one path

  • Starting from zero? Read Part I in order. Skip every box labelled Deep dive on the first pass. Each foundation chapter ends with a box that names the tool it explains.
  • Already debug cores for a living? Skim Part I for the Deep dives, then go straight to the bug ladder in Part III. Each rung links back to the foundation it relies on.
  • Holding a crash right now? Open The Map or the Decision Matrix. Find your symptom and jump to its chapter.

How the guide is built

  • Part I · Foundations explains how memory works: the process map, stack frames, the allocator, how tools instrument a program, the memory model, and locks.
  • Part II · Tool primers shows how to drive each tool, in about one screen each. The why already lives in Part I.
  • Part III · The bug ladder climbs from a NULL dereference to a refcount use-after-free that crashes far from its cause. Every rung is solved twice: once when it reproduces, and once when you have only a core.
  • Part IV · Reference holds the cheat sheet, the flag matrix, the glossary and the complete lab kit.
The platform is Linux on x86-64, with glibc and GCC or Clang. Most ideas carry over to other platforms. Exact offsets, registers and abort messages do not.

Every terminal block is real

Nothing in a terminal block was typed by hand. Each one is the output of a lab that ran in a pinned container. A script then copied it into this page.

What that buys you

  • A capture can't drift from its source without the build noticing.
  • The numbered callouts under each block point at lines you can see, not lines someone remembered.
  • You can rerun any block yourself and compare.
Your addresses and PIDs will differ. ASLR moves the heap, the stack and every library on each run. PIDs, thread IDs (LWPs) and the random tcache key change too. Under GDB the addresses repeat, because GDB turns ASLR off: 0x5555… and 0x7fff… look the same on most machines. The callouts teach the shape of the output. Don't expect to match the digits.

The toolchain

This is what the capture container reported. Every block in the guide came from this toolchain.

gcc (Ubuntu 13.3.0-6ubuntu2~24.04.1) 13.3.0
Ubuntu clang version 18.1.3 (1ubuntu1)
GNU gdb (Ubuntu 15.1-1ubuntu1~24.04.1) 15.1
valgrind-3.22.0
rr version 5.7.0 
ldd (Ubuntu GLIBC 2.39-0ubuntu8.12) 2.39
x86_64
ulimit -c: 0
rr-smoke(sys_admin)
  1. Line 1: GCC 13 builds every variant except MSan.
  2. Line 2: Clang 18 builds the MSan variant. GCC has no MemorySanitizer.
  3. Line 3: GDB 15 drives every live session and reads every core.
  4. Lines 4–5: Valgrind 3.22 and rr 5.7.
  5. Line 6: glibc 2.39. The allocator chapter's chunk layout, tcache key and abort messages are glibc 2.39's.
  6. Line 8: ulimit -c is 0, so no core file is written from this container. GDB writes the cores the labs use. Ch 8 explains why.
  7. Line 9: rr worked only once the container had --cap-add SYS_ADMIN. On that host, CAP_PERFMON alone was not enough. Try the weakest option first on yours.

Running the labs

Each lab is one C file that plants one bug. All of them share one header, pktd.h. Every lab builds with one make line.

Build the image, start a shell, run a lab

docker build -t mdg-lab - < Dockerfile
⋯
docker run -it --rm --hostname pktd-lab -v "$PWD":/lab -w /lab \
  --ulimit core=0 --cap-add SYS_PTRACE --security-opt seccomp=unconfined \
  mdg-lab bash
⋯
make 06-uaf.asan && ./06-uaf.asan
  • Run these from labs/memory-debugging/. That directory is mounted at /lab, so paths in captures read /lab/….
  • Add --cap-add PERFMON, --cap-add SYS_ADMIN or --privileged only when you want rr.
  • make <lab>.<variant> picks a build: plain, asan, tsan, msan, pattern, stripped or reftrace. The Makefile header lists the exact flags.
  • make core-<lab> runs the lab under GDB and has GDB write the core at the fault.
Dockerfile — full source
# mdg-lab: the memory-debugging lab toolchain. Build: docker build -t mdg-lab - < Dockerfile
# The base is pinned by digest so every capture comes from the same userland.
FROM ubuntu:24.04@sha256:73b999393d07f2bf65a40373778a44dc9a6d54ad7718d29247d8ff9413c4d6a8

ARG DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive
RUN apt-get update \
 && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
      build-essential clang-18 libclang-rt-18-dev gdb valgrind rr heaptrack \
      libc6-dbg binutils file procps \
 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/* \
 && update-alternatives --install /usr/bin/clang clang /usr/bin/clang-18 100

WORKDIR /lab
Makefile — full source
# Memory-debugging labs. Build one lab in one variant:  make <lab>.<variant>
#   e.g.  make 06-uaf.asan && ./06-uaf.asan
#
#   plain     gcc -O0 -g -fno-omit-frame-pointer -pthread
#   asan      plain + -fsanitize=address,undefined
#   tsan      gcc -O1 -g -fsanitize=thread -pthread
#   msan      clang -O1 -g -fsanitize=memory -fsanitize-memory-track-origins -pthread
#   pattern   plain + -ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern  (GCC fills with 0xFE bytes)
#   stripped  plain, debug info split into <lab>.debug, stripped, gnu-debuglink added
#   reftrace  plain + -DPKTD_REFTRACE -rdynamic  (boss lab only)
#
# Get a core without touching the host's core_pattern (GDB writes it):
#   make core-<lab> [V=<variant>] [ARGS=<args>]   ->  cores/<lab>.<V>[-<ARGS>].core
# It fails if the program exits normally instead of faulting.

.DEFAULT_GOAL := help

CC    := gcc
PLAIN := -O0 -g -fno-omit-frame-pointer -pthread
HDRS  := $(wildcard pktd.h)
V     ?= plain
ARGS  ?=

%.plain: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -o $@ $<

%.asan: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -fsanitize=address,undefined -o $@ $<

%.tsan: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) -O1 -g -fsanitize=thread -pthread -o $@ $<

%.msan: %.c $(HDRS)
	clang -O1 -g -fsanitize=memory -fsanitize-memory-track-origins -pthread -o $@ $<

%.pattern: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern -o $@ $<

%.stripped: %.plain
	cp $< $@
	objcopy --only-keep-debug $@ $*.debug
	strip $@
	objcopy --add-gnu-debuglink=$*.debug $@

%.reftrace: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -DPKTD_REFTRACE -rdynamic -o $@ $<

CORE = cores/$*.$(V)$(if $(ARGS),-$(ARGS)).core

core-%: %.$(V)
	@mkdir -p cores
	@rm -f $(CORE)
	-gdb -batch -ex "run $(ARGS)" -ex "generate-core-file $(CORE)" ./$<
	@test -f $(CORE) || { echo "core-$*: ./$< did not fault, no core written" >&2; exit 1; }

# Keep chained intermediates (e.g. the .plain behind core-<lab>).
.SECONDARY:

help:
	@echo "usage: make <lab>.<variant>     variants: plain asan tsan msan pattern stripped reftrace"
	@echo "       make core-<lab> [V=<variant>] [ARGS=<args>]   GDB writes cores/<lab>.<V>[-<ARGS>].core"
	@echo "       make clean                 remove every built binary, .debug file and core"

clean:
	rm -rf cores *.plain *.asan *.tsan *.msan *.pattern *.stripped *.reftrace *.debug

.PHONY: help clean
pktd.h — full source
/* pktd.h — the shared model for every lab: a toy packet daemon, "pktd".
 *
 * pktd keeps peer sessions in a fixed table and counts packets. Four workers,
 * one control thread and one stats thread share it. Lock order is always
 * table_lock -> stats_lock.
 *
 * Everything is defined here (static inline, plus the few globals), so each
 * lab is a single .c file and a single compile line.
 *
 * Build with -DPKTD_REFTRACE (make <lab>.reftrace) to turn on the refcount
 * tracer: every get/put is logged to a per-id side ring, the final put poisons
 * the session instead of freeing it, and session_check() aborts with that
 * history the first time anyone touches a dead session.
 */
#ifndef PKTD_H
#define PKTD_H

#ifndef _GNU_SOURCE
#define _GNU_SOURCE
#endif
#include <assert.h>
#include <pthread.h>
#include <stddef.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <time.h>
#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
#include <execinfo.h>
#include <unistd.h>
#endif

#define SESSION_MAGIC 0x5E55102Bu   /* live session */
#define SESSION_DEAD  0xDEADBEEFu   /* poisoned by the final put (PKTD_REFTRACE) */
#define MAX_SESSIONS  64

struct peer {
    uint32_t addr;                  /* IPv4, host byte order */
    uint16_t port;
};

struct session {                    /* 56 bytes */
    uint32_t magic;                 /* 0x00 */
    int refcnt;                     /* 0x04 */
    uint32_t id;                    /* 0x08 */
    uint32_t flags;                 /* 0x0c */
    uint64_t created_ns;            /* 0x10 */
    const struct peer *peer;        /* 0x18 */
    char name[24];                  /* 0x20 */
};

struct pktbuf {                     /* 56 bytes */
    uint32_t len;
    uint32_t port;
    uint64_t ts;
    char data[40];
};

_Static_assert(sizeof(struct session) == 56, "struct session is 56 bytes");
_Static_assert(sizeof(struct pktbuf) == sizeof(struct session), "pktbuf and session share a size class");
_Static_assert(offsetof(struct session, created_ns) == 0x10, "created_ns at 0x10");
_Static_assert(offsetof(struct session, peer) == 0x18, "peer pointer at 0x18");

/* Shared state. The locks are declared extern like a real header would; all
 * of it is defined right here because every lab is one translation unit. */
extern pthread_mutex_t table_lock, stats_lock;

pthread_mutex_t table_lock = PTHREAD_MUTEX_INITIALIZER;
pthread_mutex_t stats_lock = PTHREAD_MUTEX_INITIALIZER;
struct session *session_table[MAX_SESSIONS];    /* under table_lock */
struct { uint64_t pkts, drops; } stats;          /* under stats_lock */

static inline uint64_t now_ns(void)
{
    struct timespec ts;
    clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC, &ts);
    return (uint64_t)ts.tv_sec * 1000000000u + (uint64_t)ts.tv_nsec;
}

#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
/* ---- refcount tracer: a side table, outside struct session ---- */
#define REF_RING 16

struct ref_event {
    char op;                        /* 'N' new, 'G' get, 'P' put */
    int refcnt_after;
    void *caller;
    pid_t tid;
};

struct ref_ring {
    unsigned head;                  /* total events; slot = head % REF_RING */
    struct ref_event ev[REF_RING];
};

static struct ref_ring rings[MAX_SESSIONS];

static inline void ref_record(uint32_t id, char op, int after, void *caller)
{
    struct ref_ring *r = &rings[id % MAX_SESSIONS];
    unsigned slot = __atomic_fetch_add(&r->head, 1, __ATOMIC_RELAXED) % REF_RING;
    r->ev[slot] = (struct ref_event){ op, after, caller, gettid() };
}

static inline void ref_dump(uint32_t id)
{
    struct ref_ring *r = &rings[id % MAX_SESSIONS];
    unsigned n = r->head < REF_RING ? r->head : REF_RING;
    for (unsigned i = r->head - n; i != r->head; i++) {
        struct ref_event *e = &r->ev[i % REF_RING];
        fprintf(stderr, "  #%-2u %c refcnt=%d tid=%d  ", i, e->op, e->refcnt_after, (int)e->tid);
        fflush(stderr);
        backtrace_symbols_fd(&e->caller, 1, 2);
    }
}
#define REF_RECORD(s, op, after) ref_record((s)->id, (op), (after), __builtin_return_address(0))
#else
#define REF_RECORD(s, op, after) ((void)0)
#endif

/* ---- session lifetime ---- */

/* Labs build at -O0, so these stay real calls: GDB sees their frames and the
 * tracer's __builtin_return_address(0) is the caller of get/put. */
static inline struct session *session_new(uint32_t id, const struct peer *p)
{
    struct session *s = malloc(sizeof *s);
    if (!s)
        return NULL;
    s->magic = SESSION_MAGIC;
    s->refcnt = 1;
    s->id = id;
    s->flags = 0;
    s->created_ns = now_ns();
    s->peer = p;
    snprintf(s->name, sizeof s->name, "sess-%u", id);
    REF_RECORD(s, 'N', 1);
    return s;
}

static inline void session_get(struct session *s)
{
    int after = __atomic_add_fetch(&s->refcnt, 1, __ATOMIC_RELAXED);
    REF_RECORD(s, 'G', after);
    (void)after;
}

static inline void session_put(struct session *s)
{
    int after = __atomic_sub_fetch(&s->refcnt, 1, __ATOMIC_ACQ_REL);
    REF_RECORD(s, 'P', after);
    assert(after >= 0);
    if (after != 0)
        return;
#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
    /* Poison, don't free: keep refcnt and id, smash created_ns/peer/name
     * (0x10..0x37), and leave the chunk quarantined forever. */
    s->magic = SESSION_DEAD;
    memset((char *)s + offsetof(struct session, created_ns), 0x5A,
           sizeof *s - offsetof(struct session, created_ns));
#else
    free(s);
#endif
}

#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
static inline void session_check(struct session *s)
{
    if (s->magic != SESSION_DEAD)
        return;
    fprintf(stderr, "session %u: use after final put\n", s->id);
    ref_dump(s->id);
    abort();
}
#else
static inline void session_check(struct session *s) { (void)s; }
#endif

/* Print every live entry. session_check() runs before any field is read. */
static inline void table_dump(void)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_SESSIONS; i++) {
        struct session *s = session_table[i];
        if (!s)
            continue;
        session_check(s);
        printf("[%2d] id=%u refcnt=%d name=%s peer=%u.%u.%u.%u:%u\n", i, s->id,
               s->refcnt, s->name, s->peer->addr >> 24, (s->peer->addr >> 16) & 0xff,
               (s->peer->addr >> 8) & 0xff, s->peer->addr & 0xff, s->peer->port);
    }
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
}

#endif /* PKTD_H */
The rest of the kit is in the Lab Kit: the README, the capture scripts, the expectations, and every GDB script not shown in a chapter. With it, the guide works as a complete offline kit.
→ The Map → Ch 1. Process Memory Layout → Lab Kit

The Map

What you'll learn: every bug class in the guide on one screen, which tool finds it on each path, and the toy daemon all the labs share.

Two paths

Every bug in Part III is solved twice. Which path you're on depends on one question: can you make it happen again?

  • Path A · It reproduces. You can rerun the program. Rebuild it with a sanitizer, run it under Valgrind, or record it with rr. The tool stops at the cause.
  • Path B · Only a core. It crashed once, maybe in the field, and you have a core file. You read memory, registers and allocator state. You reason backwards from the symptom to a hypothesis.

Bug class × tool × path

ChBug classTypical symptomPath A: it reproducesPath B: only a core
14NULL dereferenceSIGSEGV at a small address, or rip=0GDB: bt, info registersx/i $pc, ptype /o to map the offset to a field, the caller from [$rsp]
15Uninitialized pointer / stack garbageWrong values; crashes that move aroundMemcheck --track-origins, MSan, pattern initGDB on the frame's locals: spot garbage or pattern bytes
16Heap overflow, off-by-oneSIGABRT inside free or malloc, laterASan redzonesThe corrupted header of the neighbouring chunk
17Stack smashing*** stack smashing detected ***, a garbled btASan stack redzonesThe canary abort; unwinding by hand
18Double freefree(): double free detected in tcache 2ASanThe glibc abort string; the tcache key
19Use-after-freeSIGSEGV on a pointer that looks almost rightASan (alloc, free and use stacks), MemcheckA safe-linked tcache pointer where a field should be
20Leak and memory growthRSS climbs for hoursLSan, Memcheck, Massif, heaptrackgcore the live process; look at what fills the heap
21Data raceCounts are wrong, differently on each runTSan, Helgrind, DRDTurn the hypothesis into a stress test or an rr chaos run (Ch 24)
22Lock-order deadlockHangs, no crashThe TSan or Helgrind lock-order reportgcore, thread apply all bt, mutex __owner
23Refcount use-after-free (boss)A crash in unrelated code, much laterASan, a watchpoint on refcnt, rr reverse executionThe object doesn't match its type: find who owns the chunk now
Part II teaches each tool once: GDB live, GDB on a core, ASan, TSan, Valgrind and rr. The Decision Matrix runs the other way, from a symptom to the first tool for each path.

Meet pktd

pktd is a toy packet daemon, invented for this guide. It is illustrative and generic; no code in it comes from a real product. Each lab plants one bug in a small slice of it.

AspectCanon
Threads1 main/control thread, 4 worker threads, 1 stats thread
Shared statesession_table (an array of struct session *) under table_lock; a global stats struct under stats_lock
Objectsstruct session and struct pktbuf, both 56 bytes. session->peer is a pointer at offset 0x18
Ref APIsession_get() adds a reference; session_put() drops one and frees the session at 0
Lock orderAlways table_lock → stats_lock. Bug 9 breaks this on purpose

The two objects

struct peer {
    uint32_t addr;                  /* IPv4, host byte order */
    uint16_t port;
};

struct session {                    /* 56 bytes */
    uint32_t magic;                 /* 0x00 */
    int refcnt;                     /* 0x04 */
    uint32_t id;                    /* 0x08 */
    uint32_t flags;                 /* 0x0c */
    uint64_t created_ns;            /* 0x10 */
    const struct peer *peer;        /* 0x18 */
    char name[24];                  /* 0x20 */
};

struct pktbuf {                     /* 56 bytes */
    uint32_t len;
    uint32_t port;
    uint64_t ts;
    char data[40];
};

_Static_assert(sizeof(struct session) == 56, "struct session is 56 bytes");
_Static_assert(sizeof(struct pktbuf) == sizeof(struct session), "pktbuf and session share a size class");
_Static_assert(offsetof(struct session, created_ns) == 0x10, "created_ns at 0x10");
_Static_assert(offsetof(struct session, peer) == 0x18, "peer pointer at 0x18");
  • The sizes are equal on purpose. A freed session and a new pktbuf land in the same allocator size class. That is what makes the boss level possible.
  • The _Static_asserts pin the layout, so the offsets you see in a core are the offsets in this header.
  • magic at offset 0 is the cheapest type check there is. When it's wrong, the object is not a live session.
→ Welcome → Ch 1. Process Memory Layout → Decision Matrix

1. Process Memory Layout

What you'll learn: which region of a process every address belongs to, how to read /proc/<pid>/maps, and why a stack overflow is a SIGSEGV on a guard page.

1.1 One address space, many regions

Every process sees its own virtual address space. The kernel maps regions into it. Each region has its own permissions. When you touch an address that isn't mapped, or break its permissions, you get SIGSEGV.

RegionHoldsPermsTypical x86-64 address
textMachine code of the programr-x0x55…/0x56… (PIE, randomized)
rodataString literals, const tablesr--Just above text
RELROGOT and other pointers fixed at startup, then made read-onlyr--Just below data
data / bssInitialized globals / zero-initialized globalsrw-End of the binary's mappings
[heap]Small malloc chunks of the main arenarw-A random gap above the binary; grows up with brk
mmap areaShared libraries, large malloc blocks, other threads' arenas, thread stacksvaries0x7f…, growing down
[stack]The main thread's stackrw-0x7ffc…–0x7fff…; grows down
[vvar] / [vdso]Kernel data and code for fast clock_gettime and friendsr-- / r-xNear the stack
  • User space on x86-64 is the lower 128 TiB: 0x0 to 0x00007fffffffffff.
  • Nothing is mapped near 0. The kernel refuses to map below vm.mmap_min_addr (64 KiB by default). That's why a NULL dereference faults even at offset 0x18.

1.2 Watch it: one object from each region

f1-layout prints one address from each region. Then it prints its own /proc/self/maps, so you can find each address in its mapping.

text    main           0x557adaa4e410
rodata  banner         0x557adaa4f022
bss     session_table  0x557adaa510c0
bss     started        0x557adaa512d0
heap    session        0x557afe4342a0
stack   peer           0x7ffe469b8738
libc    printf         0x7f0a8d942100

557adaa4d000-557adaa4e000 r--p 00000000 fe:01 7898510                    /lab/f1-layout.plain
557adaa4e000-557adaa4f000 r-xp 00001000 fe:01 7898510                    /lab/f1-layout.plain
557adaa4f000-557adaa50000 r--p 00002000 fe:01 7898510                    /lab/f1-layout.plain
557adaa50000-557adaa51000 r--p 00002000 fe:01 7898510                    /lab/f1-layout.plain
557adaa51000-557adaa52000 rw-p 00003000 fe:01 7898510                    /lab/f1-layout.plain
557afe434000-557afe455000 rw-p 00000000 00:00 0                          [heap]
7f0a8d8df000-7f0a8d8e2000 rw-p 00000000 00:00 0 
7f0a8d8e2000-7f0a8d90a000 r--p 00000000 fe:01 8282950                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
7f0a8d90a000-7f0a8da93000 r-xp 00028000 fe:01 8282950                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
7f0a8da93000-7f0a8dae2000 r--p 001b1000 fe:01 8282950                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
7f0a8dae2000-7f0a8dae6000 r--p 001ff000 fe:01 8282950                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
7f0a8dae6000-7f0a8dae8000 rw-p 00203000 fe:01 8282950                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
7f0a8dae8000-7f0a8daf5000 rw-p 00000000 00:00 0 
7f0a8daf9000-7f0a8dafb000 rw-p 00000000 00:00 0 
7f0a8dafb000-7f0a8dafc000 r--p 00000000 fe:01 8282807                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2
7f0a8dafc000-7f0a8db27000 r-xp 00001000 fe:01 8282807                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2
7f0a8db27000-7f0a8db31000 r--p 0002c000 fe:01 8282807                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2
7f0a8db31000-7f0a8db33000 r--p 00036000 fe:01 8282807                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2
7f0a8db33000-7f0a8db35000 rw-p 00038000 fe:01 8282807                    /usr/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2
7ffe46998000-7ffe469b9000 rw-p 00000000 00:00 0                          [stack]
7ffe469dd000-7ffe469e1000 r--p 00000000 00:00 0                          [vvar]
7ffe469e1000-7ffe469e3000 r-xp 00000000 00:00 0                          [vdso]
  1. Line 1: main falls inside line 10, the only r-xp mapping of the binary. That's text.
  2. Line 2: the static const banner falls inside line 11, read-only. That's rodata.
  3. Lines 3–4: both globals fall inside line 13, the binary's rw-p mapping. They're zero-initialized, so they live in bss, at the end of that mapping.
  4. Line 5: the session sits in [heap] (line 14), 0x2a0 bytes past its start. The first 0x290 bytes of the heap hold the main thread's tcache (Ch 3).
  5. Line 6: the local peer sits in [stack] (line 28).
  6. Line 7: printf lives in libc's r-xp mapping (line 17), far up in the mmap area.
  7. Line 12: a second r--p mapping of the binary. This is RELRO: pointers the loader filled in at startup, then made read-only.
  8. Lines 9–13: one file mapped five times, at file offsets 0, 0x1000, 0x2000 (twice) and 0x3000. Each mapping gets the permissions its contents need.
  9. Every address here changes on your next run. That's ASLR. The offsets inside each mapping don't change.
f1-layout.c — full source
/* f1-layout.c — where things live in a process.
 * Prints the address of one object from each region, then copies this
 * process's own /proc/self/maps to stdout so you can find each address
 * in its mapping.
 *
 *   make f1-layout.plain && ./f1-layout.plain
 */
#include "pktd.h"

static const char banner[] = "pktd";        /* .rodata */
static int started;                          /* .bss    */

int main(void)
{
    struct peer peer = { 0x0a000001, 179 };  /* stack */
    struct session *s = session_new(1, &peer); /* heap  */
    started = 1;

    printf("text    main           %p\n", (void *)main);
    printf("rodata  banner         %p\n", (void *)banner);
    printf("bss     session_table  %p\n", (void *)session_table);
    printf("bss     started        %p\n", (void *)&started);
    printf("heap    session        %p\n", (void *)s);
    printf("stack   peer           %p\n", (void *)&peer);
    printf("libc    printf         %p\n", (void *)printf);
    printf("\n");

    FILE *f = fopen("/proc/self/maps", "r");
    if (!f) {
        perror("/proc/self/maps");
        return 1;
    }
    char line[512];
    while (fgets(line, sizeof line, f))
        fputs(line, stdout);
    fclose(f);

    session_put(s);
    return 0;
}

1.3 Thread stacks and guard pages

f1-layout has one thread. A daemon like pktd has six. Each extra thread needs its own stack.

How glibc builds a thread stack

  • pthread_create maps the stack with mmap. By default it is as large as the RLIMIT_STACK soft limit, commonly 8 MiB.
  • Below the stack, glibc leaves a guard page: one page with no permissions at all. In the maps file it shows as ---p, directly under an anonymous rw-p mapping.
  • Thread stacks grow down, toward their guard page.

The main thread is different

  • Its [stack] mapping grows on demand, up to RLIMIT_STACK.
  • The kernel keeps a gap below it (stack_guard_gap, 256 pages by default) that no other mapping may take.
Stack overflow is a SIGSEGV on the guard. Deep recursion or a huge local array walks the stack pointer into the guard page. The next push faults. The fault address sits just below the thread's stack. That is how you tell a stack overflow from a wild pointer.

1.4 Where does a bad pointer fall?

The first question for any faulting address: which region is it in, if any? The value alone often tells you the bug class.

Faulting addressWhat it usually means
0x0 to a few KiBNULL plus a field offset. Map the offset to a field (Ch 14)
Just below a thread's stackStack overflow into the guard page
Inside [heap] or a heap-like mmap regionMapped, so no fault on read. If the data is wrong, suspect use-after-free
Repeated bytes: 0xfefefefe…, 0xaaaaaaaa…A pattern fill: uninitialized memory (Ch 15)
Looks like ASCII, e.g. 0x504f4e4d4c4b4a49Text where a pointer should be. The object was overwritten or reused (Ch 23)
Bits 63–47 not all the sameNon-canonical. The CPU raises a general-protection fault instead of a page fault, so the kernel can't report the address
Deep dive

Reading a maps line

  • The columns are: address range, permissions, file offset, device, inode, path.
  • The fourth permission letter is p (private, copy-on-write) or s (shared).
  • No path means an anonymous mapping. Large malloc blocks, thread stacks and other threads' arenas all look like this.
  • /proc/<pid>/smaps adds per-mapping RSS, dirty pages and swap. That's where you go when memory grows (Ch 20).

ASLR and PIE

  • The binary is position-independent (PIE), so the kernel can load it at a random base. The heap, the mmap base and the stack are randomized separately.
  • GDB disables ASLR for programs it starts (set disable-randomization on is the default). That's why addresses under GDB start with 0x5555… for the binary and 0x7fff… for the stack, and repeat from run to run.
  • To turn a run-time address into a code location, subtract the mapping's base and add its file offset. Tools that print (binary+0x1184) have already done this for you.

brk heap versus mmap

  • The main arena grows [heap] with brk. Requests at or above the mmap threshold (128 KiB by default, adjusted at run time) get their own mmap.
  • Other threads' arenas are separate mmap regions. In the maps file they look like anonymous mappings, not [heap].

GDB's info proc mappings prints this same table for a live process, every region included (Ch 7, GDB on a Live Process). On a core it reads only the file list the core carries (NT_FILE): the binary and its libraries, with no [heap], [stack] or anonymous regions. There, info files or maint info sections lists the core's own load segments, and info symbol names an address inside a loaded object (Ch 9, GDB on a Core). Classify a faulting address against them first. A stack overflow then shows up as a SIGSEGV just below a thread's stack, on its guard page.

→ Ch 2. x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames → Ch 7. GDB on a Live Process → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 14. NULL Dereference

2. x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames

What you'll learn: where arguments, return addresses and saved registers live on x86-64, how to read a frame byte by byte, and why a smashed stack gives a garbled backtrace.

2.1 The System V calling convention

Linux on x86-64 follows the System V AMD64 ABI. It fixes which registers carry what across a call. GDB, the unwinder and every crash report rely on it.

RoleRegistersWhat it means for you
Integer and pointer arguments 1–6rdi, rsi, rdx, rcx, r8, r9At a function's first instruction, $rdi is its first argument. Argument 7 onward goes on the stack
Return valuerax (and rdx for a second word)Step out with finish and GDB shows it
Callee-savedrbx, rbp, r12–r15Preserved across calls, so the unwinder can recover them in outer frames
Caller-saved (scratch)rax, rcx, rdx, rsi, rdi, r8–r11Clobbered by any call. Don't trust their values in an outer frame
Stack pointerrsp16-byte aligned just before each call, so at function entry rsp+8 is a multiple of 16

The red zone

  • The 128 bytes below rsp belong to the current function. Signal handlers and the kernel won't touch them.
  • A leaf function, one that calls nothing, can keep its locals there without moving rsp at all. You'll see exactly that below.

2.2 A frame, byte by byte

call pushes the return address. The callee's prologue pushes the caller's rbp and points rbp at it. Everything else hangs off rbp.

AddressHoldsIn checksum() below
rbp+0x10 and upThe caller's frame. Stack arguments 7+ start here when there are anyparse_header's spilled pb
rbp+0x8Return address (saved rip), pushed by call0x…51fe, inside parse_header
rbp+0x0Caller's saved rbp, pushed by the prologue0x7fffffffebb0
rbp−0x8The stack canary, in functions that have one, just below the saved rbpNo canary here (no array), so sum sits there
belowLocals and spilled argumentsi −0x4, sum −0x8, data −0x18, len −0x1c
below rsp, 128 bytesRed zonersp == rbp, so every local above is in it
  • With frame pointers, frames form a linked list. [rbp] is the caller's rbp; [rbp+8] is where the caller resumes.
  • GDB calls the address just above the return address the frame address (the CFA): the caller's rsp before the call.

2.3 Watch it: three frames deep

main calls rx_packet, which calls parse_header, which calls checksum. The script stops in checksum and looks at its frame.

Breakpoint 1, checksum (data=0x7fffffffebe0 "BGP!", len=4) at f2-frames.c:11
11	    uint32_t sum = 0;
#0  checksum (data=0x7fffffffebe0 "BGP!", len=4) at f2-frames.c:11
#1  0x00005555555551fe in parse_header (pb=0x7fffffffebd0) at f2-frames.c:19
#2  0x000055555555527a in rx_packet (port=179) at f2-frames.c:27
#3  0x00005555555552a6 in main () at f2-frames.c:32
Stack level 0, frame at 0x7fffffffeb98:
 rip = 0x555555555198 in checksum (f2-frames.c:11); saved rip = 0x5555555551fe
 called by frame at 0x7fffffffebc0
 source language c.
 Arglist at 0x7fffffffeb88, args: data=0x7fffffffebe0 "BGP!", len=4
 Locals at 0x7fffffffeb88, Previous frame's sp is 0x7fffffffeb98
 Saved registers:
  rbp at 0x7fffffffeb88, rip at 0x7fffffffeb90
0x7fffffffeb88:	0x00007fffffffebb0	0x00005555555551fe
0x7fffffffeb98:	0x00007fffffffebd0	0x0000000000008000
Dump of assembler code for function checksum:
   0x0000555555555189 <+0>:	endbr64
   0x000055555555518d <+4>:	push   %rbp
   0x000055555555518e <+5>:	mov    %rsp,%rbp
   0x0000555555555191 <+8>:	mov    %rdi,-0x18(%rbp)
   0x0000555555555195 <+12>:	mov    %esi,-0x1c(%rbp)
=> 0x0000555555555198 <+15>:	movl   $0x0,-0x8(%rbp)
⋯
   0x00005555555551d4 <+75>:	pop    %rbp
   0x00005555555551d5 <+76>:	ret
  1. Line 1: GDB names both arguments. data is pb + 0x10, the offset of data in struct pktbuf.
  2. Lines 3–6: the backtrace. Each outer frame's address is a return address: the instruction just after its call.
  3. Line 7: the frame address (CFA) is …eb98: rsp in parse_header just before the call.
  4. Line 8: saved rip = 0x…51fe, the same value as frame #1 on line 4.
  5. Line 14: the saved rbp is at CFA−16 (…eb88), the return address at CFA−8 (…eb90).
  6. Line 15: x/4gx $rsp starts at …eb88. The two words are the caller's rbp and the return address, in that order.
  7. Line 16: the next word up is in parse_header's frame. It is pb, spilled at its rbp−0x18, and it matches frame #1 on line 4.
  8. Line 18: endbr64 is a landing pad for indirect branches (Intel CET). This distro's GCC adds it by default.
  9. Lines 19–20: the prologue. Push the caller's rbp, then rbp = rsp.
  10. Lines 21–22: the arguments arrive in rdi and esi and are stored below rbp. No sub rsp: checksum is a leaf, so its locals live in the red zone.
  11. Line 23: the arrow is $pc. GDB stopped after the prologue, at the first line of the body.
  12. Lines 25–26: the epilogue. Pop the caller's rbp; ret pops the return address into rip.
f2-frames.c — full source
/* f2-frames.c — a three-deep call chain to look at stack frames.
 * main -> rx_packet -> parse_header -> checksum. Stop in checksum and walk
 * back up: each frame holds its saved rbp and the return address (saved rip).
 *
 *   make f2-frames.plain && gdb -x gdb/f2-frames.gdb ./f2-frames.plain
 */
#include "pktd.h"

static uint16_t checksum(const char *data, uint32_t len)
{
    uint32_t sum = 0;
    for (uint32_t i = 0; i < len; i++)
        sum += (unsigned char)data[i];
    return (uint16_t)(sum + (sum >> 16));
}

static int parse_header(struct pktbuf *pb)
{
    uint16_t csum = checksum(pb->data, pb->len);
    return csum != 0;
}

static int rx_packet(uint32_t port)
{
    struct pktbuf pb = { .len = 4, .port = port, .ts = 0 };
    memcpy(pb.data, "BGP!", 4);
    return parse_header(&pb);
}

int main(void)
{
    int ok = rx_packet(179);
    printf("rx_packet: %s\n", ok ? "ok" : "bad");
    return 0;
}
gdb/f2-frames.gdb — full source
# f2-frames.gdb — stop in the innermost frame and look at the stack.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/f2-frames.gdb ./f2-frames.plain
set pagination off
break checksum
run
bt
info frame
x/4gx $rsp
disassemble
continue

2.4 Frame pointers, unwind tables and canaries

Frame pointer or not

  • The labs build with -fno-omit-frame-pointer, so the rbp chain is always there.
  • At -O1 and above, GCC on x86-64 omits it by default and uses rbp as a normal register.
  • GDB doesn't need the chain. It unwinds from DWARF call-frame information (.eh_frame), which says, for every instruction, where the CFA and the saved registers are.
  • Profilers and some quick unwinders do rely on the chain. That's why many shops keep frame pointers on in production builds.

Canaries

  • -fstack-protector-strong (on by default in many distro compilers) guards functions that have local arrays or take a local's address.
  • The prologue copies a secret from %fs:0x28, the thread's TCB, into the slot below the saved rbp. The epilogue compares it before ret.
  • On a mismatch it calls __stack_chk_fail, which prints *** stack smashing detected ***: terminated and aborts.
  • The check runs at return, not at the overflow. By then the saved rbp and return address above the canary are usually trashed too.
Deep dive

Unwinding by hand

  • With frame pointers: x/2gx $rbp gives the caller's rbp and the return address. Repeat with the new rbp. info symbol <addr> names each return address.
  • Without them, or when they're smashed: dump the stack with x/64a $rsp. The a format prints each word with a symbol when it has one. Words that point into text are candidate return addresses.
  • Check each candidate. A real return address follows a call. Disassemble a few bytes before it (x/2i <addr>-5 for the common 5-byte direct call) and look for one.
  • Stale words lie. Old frames leave return addresses behind on the stack. Trust a candidate only if the chain above it also makes sense.

Why a smashed stack garbles bt

  • An overflow of a local buffer writes upward, toward the canary, then the saved rbp, then the return address.
  • The unwinder reads those slots to find the caller. Overwritten, they point at nothing, or at the attacker's bytes, so frame #1 onward is noise.
  • Frame #0 is usually still right. So are the words above the smashed region: older frames survive.

_FORTIFY_SOURCE

  • When optimizing, many distro compilers also define _FORTIFY_SOURCE. Calls like memcpy and strcpy into objects of known size become checked variants that abort with *** buffer overflow detected ***.
  • It has no effect at -O0, which is how the labs build.

This frame layout is why stack smashing (Ch 17, Stack Smashing) aborts at the function's return and leaves a garbled backtrace: the overflow runs up through the canary, the saved rbp and the return address the unwinder needs. Knowing the slots, you can unwind by hand from $rsp and recover the frames above the damage.

→ Ch 1. Process Memory Layout → Ch 7. GDB on a Live Process → Ch 17. Stack Smashing → Ch 14. NULL Dereference

3. The glibc Allocator

What you'll learn: what a malloc chunk looks like in memory, what free writes into it, how safe-linking hides tcache pointers, and what glibc's abort messages are telling you.

3.1 Chunks

malloc carves memory into chunks. Each chunk starts with a 16-byte header. The pointer you get back points just past it.

OffsetIn useFree
chunk+0x00prev_size: belongs to the previous chunk's user data while that chunk is in useprev_size: the previous chunk's size, valid only when that chunk is free
chunk+0x08size, a multiple of 16, with three flag bits in the low nibble: P=0x1 PREV_INUSE (the previous chunk is in use), M=0x2 IS_MMAPPED, A=0x4 NON_MAIN_ARENA
chunk+0x10User data starts here: the pointer malloc returnstcache and fastbins: next, safe-linked. Other bins: fd
chunk+0x18User datatcache: key (the random tcache_key). Other bins: bk
chunk+0x20User dataLarge bins only: fd_nextsize, bk_nextsize

Sizes

  • Chunk size = request + 8, rounded up to 16, at least 0x20. The 8 extra bytes are the size field; the next chunk's prev_size lends the tail.
  • A 0x48-byte request gets a 0x50 chunk. A 56-byte struct session or struct pktbuf gets a 0x40 chunk: same size class, same bins.
  • The size field of a normal in-use chunk usually reads 0x…1: the P bit of its in-use neighbour below.

3.2 Watch it: a chunk before and after free

f3-chunks makes three 0x48-byte allocations, fills the middle one with 'P' (0x50) bytes, and frees it. The script dumps that chunk from its header on, before and after the free.

--- before free(p): prev_size, size|flags, then user data ---
0x5555555592e0:	0x0000000000000000	0x0000000000000051
0x5555555592f0:	0x5050505050505050	0x5050505050505050
0x555555559300:	0x5050505050505050	0x5050505050505050
0x555555559310:	0x5050505050505050	0x5050505050505050
⋯
--- after free(p): user data now holds tcache next + key ---
0x5555555592e0:	0x0000000000000000	0x0000000000000051
0x5555555592f0:	0x0000000555555559	0xf59be862f15c5061
0x555555559300:	0x5050505050505050	0x5050505050505050
0x555555559310:	0x5050505050505050	0x5050505050505050
next (mangled) = 0x555555559
next (decoded) = 0x0   (= (&next >> 12) ^ mangled)
tcache key     = 0xf59be862f15c5061
a=0x5555555592a0 p=0x5555555592f0 c=0x555555559340
  1. Line 2: the header. prev_size is 0, unused while the chunk before is live. size is 0x51: a 0x50 chunk with P set.
  2. Lines 3–5: user data, all 'P'.
  3. Line 8: after free, the header is unchanged. Freeing into the tcache doesn't touch it, and doesn't clear the P bit in the next chunk either. To the rest of malloc, the chunk still looks in use.
  4. Line 9: the first 16 bytes of user data are overwritten: the safe-linked next pointer, then the tcache key.
  5. Lines 10–11: the rest of the data is untouched. Freed memory keeps its old contents. In a core, that's evidence.
  6. Lines 12–13: next decodes to 0. The bin was empty when this chunk went in, so it's the only entry.
  7. Line 14: the tcache key is random per process. Yours will differ.
  8. Line 15: the three chunks sit 0x50 apart. The first is 0x2a0 past the heap start, taken as …9000. That start is inferred, because this capture doesn't print the heap mapping, but Ch 1's capture shows the same 0x2a0 offset from a printed [heap] start. The main thread's tcache structure fills the first 0x290 bytes.
f3-chunks.c — full source
/* f3-chunks.c — what a malloc chunk looks like, before and after free.
 * Three 0x48-byte requests, each rounded up to a 0x50-byte chunk. Freeing
 * the middle one puts it in the tcache: its first word becomes the
 * safe-linked "next" pointer and its second word the tcache key.
 *
 *   make f3-chunks.plain && gdb -x gdb/f3-chunks.gdb ./f3-chunks.plain
 */
#include "pktd.h"

/* Empty marker functions so GDB can stop at a named point. */
__attribute__((noinline)) void before_free(void) { __asm__ volatile(""); }
__attribute__((noinline)) void after_free(void)  { __asm__ volatile(""); }

int main(void)
{
    char *a = malloc(0x48);
    char *p = malloc(0x48);
    char *c = malloc(0x48);
    memset(p, 'P', 0x48);

    before_free();
    free(p);
    after_free();

    printf("a=%p p=%p c=%p\n", (void *)a, (void *)p, (void *)c);
    free(a);
    free(c);
    return 0;
}
gdb/f3-chunks.gdb — full source
# f3-chunks.gdb — dump the middle chunk's header and first words around free(p).
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/f3-chunks.gdb ./f3-chunks.plain
set pagination off
break before_free
break after_free
run
up
echo \n--- before free(p): prev_size, size|flags, then user data ---\n
x/8gx p-16
continue
up
echo \n--- after free(p): user data now holds tcache next + key ---\n
x/8gx p-16
printf "next (mangled) = %#lx\n", *(unsigned long *)p
printf "next (decoded) = 0x%lx   (= (&next >> 12) ^ mangled)\n", ((unsigned long)p >> 12) ^ *(unsigned long *)p
printf "tcache key     = %#lx\n", *(unsigned long *)(p + 8)
continue

3.3 Safe-linking

Since glibc 2.32, the singly linked lists (tcache and fastbins) don't store next as a plain pointer. They store it mangled with the address of the field that holds it.

stored  = (pos >> 12) ^ next      /* pos = address of the next field itself */
next    = (pos >> 12) ^ stored

Decoding the capture

  • For a tcache entry, pos is the user pointer. Here p = 0x5555555592f0, so pos >> 12 = 0x555555559.
  • The stored word (line 12) is 0x555555559. XOR them: 0x0. So next is NULL.
  • In GDB: p/x ((unsigned long)p >> 12) ^ *(unsigned long *)p. That's line 15 of the script.
A field that equals its own address shifted right by 12. When the first word of an object reads as (&object >> 12), the object has been freed into a tcache or fastbin that was empty. The value looks like a small, plausible pointer, and dereferencing it faults. You'll meet exactly this in a use-after-free core (Ch 19).
  • Why it exists: an attacker who overwrites next now has to know a heap address. It also catches some corruption, because glibc checks that a decoded pointer is 16-byte aligned.
  • Doubly linked bins (fd/bk) are not mangled. They are protected by consistency checks instead.

3.4 Bins, tcache, arenas, top

Free chunks are kept in lists called bins. malloc looks in them before carving new memory. For a small request the order is: tcache, fastbin, the exact small bin, a scan of the unsorted bin, the large bins, then the top chunk.

BinScopeChunk sizesBehaviour
tcachePer thread0x20–0x410, one bin per 16 bytes (64 bins)LIFO, no lock, 7 chunks per bin by default. First stop for both free and malloc
fastbinsPer arenaUp to 0x80 by defaultLIFO, singly linked, not merged with neighbours right away
unsortedPer arenaAnyOne list. Freed chunks land here after merging, and are sorted on a later malloc
small binsPer arenaBelow 0x400One exact size per bin, doubly linked
large binsPer arena0x400 and upRanges of sizes, kept sorted

Where new memory comes from

  • Top chunk: the free space at the end of an arena's heap. When no bin fits, malloc splits the top. When the top is too small, the heap grows.
  • mmap'd chunks: requests at or above the mmap threshold (128 KiB by default, adjusted at run time) get their own mapping, with the M bit set. free unmaps them.

Arenas

  • The main arena uses the brk heap. The first thread to call malloc uses it.
  • Each new thread that calls malloc gets its own arena, with its own mmap'd heap, until there are 8 × the CPU count (on 64-bit). After that, threads share. Contention only matters once that limit is reached.
  • So a worker thread's chunks usually come from a non-main arena and carry the A bit, whether or not anything ever contended. Their sizes read 0x…5 instead of 0x…1.
  • The tcache sits in front of all of this, one per thread. A chunk freed by thread T goes into T's tcache, whichever thread allocated it.
LIFO plus per-thread is why the boss bug is deterministic. A thread frees a 0x40 chunk and its next 0x40 malloc gets the same chunk back (Ch 23).
Deep dive

Decoding glibc's abort messages

glibc checks its own structures on many malloc and free paths. When a check fails, it prints one line and calls abort(). The message names the check, not the bug. Here's how to translate the common ones (glibc 2.39).

MessageCheck that failedUsual cause
free(): double free detected in tcache 2The chunk's key word equals tcache_key, and the chunk is already in its tcache binDouble free (Ch 18)
free(): invalid pointerThe pointer is misaligned, or its size field would wrap past the top of the address spaceFreeing a non-heap pointer, or an overflow that rewrote this chunk's size (Ch 16)
free(): invalid sizeThe size is below the minimum or not a multiple of 16Header overwritten
free(): invalid next size (fast) / (normal)The next chunk's size is implausibleAn overflow off the end of this chunk
double free or corruption (!prev)The next chunk's P bit says this chunk is already freeDouble free past the tcache, or overflow into the next header
double free or corruption (fasttop)The chunk is already first in its fastbinDouble free when the tcache bin is full or disabled
malloc(): unaligned tcache chunk detectedA tcache next decodes to a misaligned addressA write into a freed chunk: use-after-free (Ch 19)
malloc(): corrupted top sizeThe top chunk's size is larger than the arena's memoryAn overflow off the last chunk in the heap
corrupted size vs. prev_sizeA chunk's size disagrees with the next chunk's prev_sizeHeader overwritten
corrupted double-linked listfd->bk or bk->fd doesn't point backA write into a freed chunk in a small or large bin
munmap_chunk(): invalid pointerA chunk with the M bit set has an inconsistent address or sizeFreeing an interior pointer, or a smashed header
  • The abort happens in whichever thread next hits the check. That thread is often innocent.
  • In a core, frame malloc_printerr (str=…) carries the message even when stderr is lost.

glibc's own debugging knobs

  • MALLOC_PERTURB_=N fills allocated memory with the byte N ^ 0xff and freed memory with N, which turns some silent use of stale or uninitialized data into visible garbage.
  • But not on the tcache path. In glibc 2.39, a free that lands in the tcache returns before the perturb fill, and a malloc served from the tcache never perturbs. For tcache-sized chunks, such as pktd's 0x40 objects, MALLOC_PERTURB_ does nothing while the bin has room.
  • So pair it with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0, which disables the tcache. Frees then also hit the older fastbin and bin checks.
  • MALLOC_CHECK_ and mtrace still exist, but since glibc 2.34 they only work with LD_PRELOAD=libc_malloc_debug.so.

This layout is what you read in a core with x/8gx ptr-16 (Ch 9, GDB on a Core). A neighbour's smashed size field explains a heap overflow's late abort (Ch 16). The tcache key is how glibc spots a double free (Ch 18). A safe-linked next sitting where a field should be gives away a use-after-free (Ch 19).

→ Ch 1. Process Memory Layout → Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 18. Double Free → Ch 23. Boss Level

4. Instrumentation Models

What you'll learn: the three ways a tool can watch your program (compile-time instrumentation, dynamic binary translation, record and replay), what each costs, and which ones can't be combined.

4.1 Three ways to watch a program

A debugger only sees the moment it stops. To catch a bug at its cause, a tool has to see every memory access, or be able to replay them. There are three ways to get there.

  • Compile-time (sanitizers): the compiler inserts a check before loads and stores. A runtime library replaces malloc and keeps the bookkeeping. You must rebuild.
  • Dynamic binary translation (Valgrind): your unmodified binary runs on a synthetic CPU. Every instruction is translated and instrumented on the fly. No rebuild; much slower.
  • Recording (rr): no checks at all. rr records everything nondeterministic, so the same execution can be replayed, forwards and backwards, under GDB.

4.2 ASan: shadow memory, redzones, quarantine

AddressSanitizer keeps one shadow byte for every 8 bytes of application memory. The shadow byte says how many of those 8 bytes you may touch.

shadow(addr) = (addr >> 3) + 0x7fff8000          /* x86-64 Linux */
/* e.g. 0x502000000010  ->  shadow byte at 0xa047fff8002 */

After s = malloc(56), then free(s)

App bytesShadowMeans
s−16 … s−1fa faHeap redzone in front of the block. ASan's own chunk header lives here
s … s+5500 × 7All 56 bytes addressable
s+56 …fa …Redzone after the block. One byte past the end is caught
(if it were malloc(60)) s+56 … s+6304Partial: only the first 4 of these 8 bytes are addressable
s … s+55 after freefd × 7Freed. The block sits in quarantine; any access is a use-after-free

The check

  • Before an 8-byte access, the shadow byte must be 0.
  • Before a 1-, 2- or 4-byte access at a, with shadow byte k: OK if k == 0, or if (a & 7) + size − 1 < k. Anything else is a report.
  • Negative shadow values (fa, fd, f1…) are never addressable. The value tells the report what kind of memory you hit.

Redzones and quarantine

  • Redzones: poisoned padding around every heap block. The compiler adds them around stack arrays and globals too. An overflow lands in poison instead of a neighbour.
  • Quarantine: free poisons the block and holds it in a FIFO queue, 256 MB by default on 64-bit. It isn't reused until it leaves the queue, so a stale pointer still hits fd long after the free.
  • ASan's malloc records the allocating and freeing stacks. That's how a report can show you all three: alloc, free, use.

4.3 The other sanitizers

  • UBSan checks language rules, not memory: signed overflow, misaligned pointers, bad shifts. It's cheap, so the labs build it into the asan variant.
  • LSan runs at exit. It stops the process, scans globals, stacks, registers and TLS for pointers into the heap, and reports blocks nothing points to. It's built into ASan.
  • MSan (Clang only) keeps one shadow bit per application bit, marking it initialized or not. Copying uninitialized data is fine. Branching on it, dereferencing it or passing it to a syscall is a report. Every linked library must be instrumented, or you get false reports.
  • TSan keeps shadow cells recording the last few accesses to each 8-byte word: which thread, its logical time, read or write. Each thread and each lock carries a vector clock. Two accesses conflict when neither happens-before the other (Ch 5).

4.4 Valgrind and rr

Valgrind: dynamic binary translation

  • Valgrind loads your program itself. It translates each block of machine code into its own intermediate form (VEX IR). The tool (Memcheck, Helgrind, DRD, Massif) adds instrumentation. Then it compiles the block back to host code and caches it.
  • Memcheck tracks A bits (is this byte addressable?) and V bits (is this bit defined?) for all memory and registers.
  • It replaces malloc and free with its own. Freed blocks wait in a queue (--freelist-vol, 20 MB by default) before reuse.
  • It runs one thread at a time. Races still show in Helgrind and DRD, because they reason about ordering, not timing.

rr: record once, replay exactly

  • rr runs every thread of the process on a single core, one at a time. Scheduling becomes the only source of interleaving, and rr records it.
  • It records the results of system calls and the timing of signals and context switches. Replay feeds the same results back, so every run of the trace is the same run.
  • Reverse execution works by restoring a checkpoint and replaying forward to just before where you were.
  • rr counts retired conditional branches with the same hardware performance counters perf reads, which is why it needs perf-counter access (perf_event_paranoid, CAP_PERFMON or more, and a CPU or VM that exposes the counters).

4.5 Cost and compatibility

ToolModelRebuild?Rough slowdownFindsDoesn't mix with
ASan (+UBSan, LSan)Compile-timeYes: -fsanitize=address~2×Heap, stack and global overflows, use-after-free, double free, leaksTSan, MSan, Valgrind
MSanCompile-time, Clang onlyYes, every library~3×Use of uninitialized memoryASan, TSan, Valgrind
TSanCompile-timeYes: -fsanitize=thread5–15×, 5–10× memoryData races, lock-order inversionsASan, MSan, Valgrind
MemcheckDBTNo (keep -g)~20–30×Invalid access, uninitialized use, leaksSanitized binaries
Helgrind / DRDDBTNoTens of ×Races, lock misuse; Helgrind also lock orderSanitized binaries
rrRecord / replayNoLow on one thread; parallel work is serialized. Needs perf countersNothing by itself: it makes the run repeatable and reversible—
The slowdown figures are the tools' own documented ballparks. Your workload will vary. Measure before you put a sanitizer in a soak test.
Deep dive

Why they can't be combined

  • ASan, TSan and MSan each reserve huge fixed ranges of the address space for their shadow, and each wants its own malloc and its own interceptors. Two of them in one process collide.
  • Valgrind also replaces malloc and manages the whole address space itself. A sanitized binary under Valgrind gets two allocators and a shadow layout Valgrind didn't expect. Run Valgrind on the plain build.
  • So build one variant per tool. The labs' make <lab>.<variant> does exactly that.

Older and newer relatives

  • Electric Fence put each allocation right against an inaccessible page, so the first byte past the end faulted. It costs at least one page per allocation. ASan's redzones are the cheap, byte-precise version of the same idea.
  • HWASan, mainly on AArch64, tags each pointer's top byte and each 16-byte granule of memory, and compares them on access. It uses much less memory than ASan, and it detects bugs probabilistically rather than exactly.

What each model can't see

  • Sanitizers only check code that was compiled with them. A bug inside an uninstrumented .so is invisible, except where the runtime intercepts a libc call.
  • Valgrind sees every instruction, but it can't see a stack or global overflow that stays inside the mapped region: it has no redzones there.
  • rr changes timing (one core), so some races become rarer under recording. Its chaos mode randomizes scheduling to push them back out (Ch 24).

Each Part II primer drives one of these models. Shadow memory, redzones and quarantine are why ASan stops at the bad access (Ch 10, The ASan Family). Vector clocks are why TSan flags a race that didn't bite; its shadow cells and history are how it names both accesses (Ch 11, TSan). Translation is why Valgrind needs no rebuild (Ch 12, Valgrind). Recording is why rr can run backwards from a crash (Ch 13, rr).

→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 12. Valgrind → Ch 13. rr → Build-Flag Matrix

5. The C Memory Model

What you'll learn: the difference between a data race and a race condition, what happens-before means, how the compiler and CPU reorder memory accesses, and why volatile doesn't fix any of it.

5.1 Data race versus race condition

The two terms sound alike. They mean different things, and tools find only one of them.

Data raceRace condition
DefinitionTwo threads access the same memory location, at least one writes, at least one access is not atomic, and neither happens-before the otherThe result depends on the timing of threads, and some timings are wrong
In C11Undefined behaviour, whatever the outcomeA logic bug; the program is well-defined
ExampleFour workers do stats.pkts++ with no lockCheck a session exists, unlock, then lock again and use it: it may be gone
Found byTSan, Helgrind, DRDReasoning, stress tests, rr chaos mode
  • Code made only of atomics and locks can still have a race condition.
  • A data race doesn't have to corrupt anything in your test to be a bug. It's undefined behaviour the moment it happens.

5.2 Happens-before

Happens-before is the order the language guarantees. If A happens-before B, B sees A's effects. If neither happens-before the other, they're concurrent, whatever the clock said.

Where it comes from

  • Within a thread: program order (sequenced-before).
  • Between threads, a synchronizes-with edge:
    • an unlock of a mutex, then a later lock of the same mutex;
    • a release store, then an acquire load that reads the value it stored;
    • pthread_create, then the start of the new thread;
    • a thread's end, then the pthread_join that waits for it.
  • Transitivity: chain those edges together.

5.3 Reordering: compiler and CPU

Your code doesn't run in the order you wrote it. Both the compiler and the CPU move memory accesses, as long as a single thread can't tell.

The compiler

  • It keeps values in registers and skips re-reading memory.
  • It hoists loads out of loops, sinks stores, merges or splits them.
  • It assumes no other thread writes a plain variable, because that would be a data race.
static bool stop;                      /* illustrative, not from a lab */
static unsigned long spins;
void *worker(void *arg) { while (!stop) spins++; return NULL; }
/* At -O2 the compiler may read stop once and loop forever. */

The CPU

  • x86-64 is strongly ordered (TSO). The one reordering it allows: a later load can complete before an earlier store, because stores wait in a store buffer.
  • AArch64 and POWER allow far more. Code that "works" on x86 can break when ported.
  • Only atomics with the right memory order (or locks, which use them) constrain both the compiler and the CPU.

5.4 Why volatile doesn't help

volatile was made for memory-mapped hardware registers and signal handlers, not for threads.

  • No atomicity. volatile_counter++ is still a load, an add and a store. Two threads still lose updates.
  • No ordering with other memory. The compiler won't reorder volatile accesses with each other, but it may move ordinary loads and stores across them. The CPU isn't constrained at all.
  • Still a data race. In C11, concurrent conflicting volatile accesses are undefined behaviour, and TSan reports them.
  • It does stop the hoisting in 5.3, which is why it seems to work. The fix is _Atomic or a lock.
"Benign" races aren't. A racy flag or statistic may look harmless. The compiler is still allowed to tear it, re-read it, or cache it forever. Make it atomic, with memory_order_relaxed if ordering doesn't matter. It costs almost nothing on x86 and it makes the intent explicit.
Deep dive

What x86 actually guarantees

  • Aligned loads and stores up to 8 bytes are atomic in hardware. Plain ++ still compiles to a separate load and store, or to an add to memory without lock, and neither is atomic across cores.
  • Every lock-prefixed read-modify-write (lock xadd, lock cmpxchg) is atomic and acts as a full barrier.
  • A seq_cst store needs StoreLoad ordering, so compilers emit xchg (or mov plus mfence). Acquire loads and release stores are plain movs; on x86 they only restrain the compiler.

Why TSan catches races that didn't bite

  • TSan doesn't look for wrong results. It checks each access against the vector clocks: is there a happens-before path from the last conflicting access?
  • If not, it reports, even when the run happened to produce the right number. One run where the two accesses were merely close in time is enough.
  • The reverse holds too: if your test never executes the racing pair, TSan can't see it. Coverage still matters.

Happens-before is what TSan computes (Ch 11, TSan). Each thread and each lock carries a vector clock. Locks, atomics, create and join merge clocks along the edges in 5.2. Two accesses with no path between them are a race, so TSan reports a race that never corrupted anything in your run.

→ Ch 6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting → Ch 11. TSan → Ch 21. Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag

6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting

What you'll learn: what an atomic read-modify-write is, which memory orders you actually need, what is inside a glibc mutex, why lock order matters, and how to write a refcount that can't free under you.

6.1 Atomic read-modify-write

A read-modify-write (RMW) reads a value, changes it and writes it back as one indivisible step. No other thread can slip in between.

  • On x86-64 it's one lock-prefixed instruction: lock xadd for fetch-and-add, lock cmpxchg for compare-and-swap.
  • In C11: atomic_fetch_add, atomic_compare_exchange_strong, and friends.
  • GCC and Clang also offer __atomic_* builtins on plain types. pktd.h uses them for the refcount:
static inline void session_get(struct session *s)
{
    int after = __atomic_add_fetch(&s->refcnt, 1, __ATOMIC_RELAXED);
    REF_RECORD(s, 'G', after);
    (void)after;
}

static inline void session_put(struct session *s)
{
    int after = __atomic_sub_fetch(&s->refcnt, 1, __ATOMIC_ACQ_REL);
    REF_RECORD(s, 'P', after);
    assert(after >= 0);
    if (after != 0)
        return;
#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
    /* Poison, don't free: keep refcnt and id, smash created_ns/peer/name
     * (0x10..0x37), and leave the chunk quarantined forever. */
    s->magic = SESSION_DEAD;
    memset((char *)s + offsetof(struct session, created_ns), 0x5A,
           sizeof *s - offsetof(struct session, created_ns));
#else
    free(s);
#endif
}
  • session_get increments and session_put decrements, each as one RMW. Two threads can never both see the same old value.
  • session_put frees on the result of its own RMW. Exactly one thread sees 0, so exactly one thread frees.
  • Reading refcnt and then deciding is not the same thing. Another thread can change it in between.

6.2 Memory orders, only as deep as you need

OrderGuaranteesUse it for
relaxedAtomicity only. No ordering with other memoryCounters and statistics; taking a reference when you already hold one
release (store)Earlier reads and writes can't move after itPublishing: fill an object, then store the pointer
acquire (load)Later reads and writes can't move before itConsuming: load the pointer, then read the object
acq_rel (RMW)Both at onceThe final refcount decrement
seq_cstacq_rel plus one total order of all seq_cst operations. The defaultWhen in doubt

Why the refcount uses two orders

  • Get is relaxed. You can only take a reference to an object you can already reach safely, so it can't die during the increment.
  • Put is acq_rel. The release half publishes this thread's writes to the object. The acquire half, in whichever thread reaches 0, makes every other holder's writes visible before free. Without it, a late write could land in freed memory.

6.3 Inside a glibc mutex

A pthread_mutex_t is 40 bytes on x86-64. In a core, its fields tell you who holds it and whether anyone waits.

OffsetFieldMeaning
0x00__lockThe futex word: 0 unlocked, 1 locked, 2 locked with (possible) waiters
0x04__countRecursion depth for recursive mutexes
0x08__ownerThread ID (LWP) of the holder; 0 when unlocked
0x0c__nusersNumber of current users
0x10__kindType: normal, recursive, error-checking, robust…
0x14__spins, __elisionAdaptive-spin and lock-elision state
0x18__listLinks for robust mutexes
  • Uncontended lock and unlock are one atomic instruction each, with no syscall.
  • A thread that finds the mutex taken sets __lock to 2 and sleeps in futex(FUTEX_WAIT). The unlocker sees the 2 and wakes a waiter.
  • In GDB: p table_lock.__data.__owner gives an LWP. Match it against info threads to find the holder's stack.

6.4 Lock ordering

A deadlock needs a cycle: each thread holds one lock and waits for a lock another thread holds. The rule that prevents it is simple. Pick one global order and always take locks in that order. pktd's order is table_lock → stats_lock.

Lock-order detectors

  • TSan and Helgrind record an edge A → B whenever a thread takes B while holding A.
  • A cycle in that graph is a potential deadlock. They report it even if the run never hung, because the bad interleaving only has to be possible.
  • So you can catch a deadlock in a test that passes. The labs' inversion mode does exactly this (Ch 22).

6.5 Refcount patterns

The rules

  • Take a reference before you let go of what kept the object alive. Look the session up under table_lock, session_get it, then unlock.
  • Every get is paired with exactly one put, on every path, including error and timeout paths.
  • Never put a reference you didn't get. The count drops early, another thread's correct put frees the object, and the crash lands somewhere else entirely. That's the boss bug (Ch 23).
  • Never get from zero. An object at refcount 0 is already being freed. If lookups can race with the final put, use increment-if-not-zero with a CAS loop.

Make the next bug cheap to find

  • A magic field at offset 0, checked on use.
  • Poison on the final put, in debug builds.
  • An underflow assert: pktd.h asserts after >= 0.
  • A debug ring that logs every get and put with its caller. pktd.h's PKTD_REFTRACE build does that.
Deep dive

The futex protocol behind __lock

  • Lock: CAS 0 → 1. If it succeeds, you own it, with no syscall. If not, exchange the word to 2 and, unless the old value was 0, call futex(FUTEX_WAIT, 2). Repeat.
  • Unlock: exchange the word to 0. If the old value was 2, call futex(FUTEX_WAKE, 1).
  • That's why a hung thread's backtrace shows futex_wait (… expected=2, futex_word=<the mutex>). The futex_word names the lock it's waiting for.

When __owner can mislead

  • It's written after the lock is acquired and cleared before it's released, so it can briefly lag the futex word.
  • A holder that exited without unlocking leaves its old LWP behind. No thread in info threads has it. Robust mutexes exist for exactly that case.
  • Read it together with __lock, and with the waiters' backtraces.

Atomics are not free

  • A locked instruction makes the core own the cache line exclusively. Many threads hammering one counter bounce that line between cores.
  • Per-thread counters summed on read are often faster than one shared atomic, and still race-free.

The lock-order graph is what TSan's lock-order-inversion report shows (Ch 11, TSan), and what Helgrind checks (Ch 12, Valgrind). __owner is how you read a deadlock from a gcore of a hung process (Ch 22, Lock-Order Deadlock). The refcount rules are what the boss bug breaks (Ch 23, Boss Level).

→ Ch 5. The C Memory Model → Ch 11. TSan → Ch 22. Lock-Order Deadlock → Ch 23. Boss Level

7. GDB on a Live Process

What you'll learn: how to stop a running daemon where you want, catch every write to one field from any thread, look at threads, frames and raw memory, and keep the other threads still while you step.

Why it works: GDB reads frames with the layout from Ch 2, x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames, and classifies addresses with the map from Ch 1, Process Memory Layout. This chapter is only about driving it.

7.1 Start, attach, stop

Getting GDB onto the process

  • gdb ./pktd, then run [args]: GDB starts it, with ASLR off by default.
  • gdb -p <pid>: attach to a daemon that's already running. It needs ptrace permission: the same user plus kernel.yama.ptrace_scope 0, or CAP_SYS_PTRACE. That's why the lab container adds --cap-add SYS_PTRACE.
  • gdb -q -batch -nx -x script.gdb ./prog: run a command file and exit. Every GDB capture in this guide was made this way.

Breakpoints

CommandDoes
break file.c:42, break funcStop when any thread reaches that line or function
break func if s->id == 7Stop only when the condition holds. GDB still stops each time to evaluate it, so it's slow on hot paths
tbreak funcStop once, then delete itself
break func thread 3Stop only in GDB thread 3
commands N … endRun commands each time breakpoint N hits. End with continue to log without stopping
info breakpoints, delete N, disable NList and manage them. Watchpoints share the same list
The boss lab has a hook for exactly this: control_ready(), an empty noinline function the control thread calls once sessions 1–8 exist. break control_ready stops there on every run.

7.2 Watchpoints: hardware and software

A watchpoint stops the program when a value changes. It answers the question a breakpoint can't: who wrote this?

Hardware watchpoints

  • x86-64 has four debug address registers, DR0–DR3. Each watches one aligned 1-, 2-, 4- or 8-byte region. So at most four hardware watchpoints of up to 8 bytes each, and fewer if a watched region needs several registers.
  • The CPU traps after the writing instruction runs. GDB then prints Old value and New value, and the location is the instruction after the store.
  • GDB sets the registers in every thread, including threads created later. A write from any thread is caught, at full speed.
  • Ask for more than the hardware has and GDB may accept the watchpoint, then fail at continue with Could not insert hardware watchpoint.

Software watchpoints

  • When it can't use hardware (or after set can-use-hw-watchpoints 0), GDB single-steps the program and compares the value after every step. The manual calls it hundreds of times slower.
  • In a multi-threaded program it only watches the value in a single thread. For a race between threads, that's useless.

watch versus watch -l

  • watch s->refcnt watches the expression. When s's frame returns, the watchpoint goes out of scope and is deleted.
  • watch -l s->refcnt (-location) evaluates the address once and watches that memory, whatever frame or thread you're in. For a field on the heap, that's almost always what you want.
  • rwatch (read) and awatch (read or write) exist too. They only work as hardware watchpoints.

7.3 Watch it: who touched session 7's refcount?

The boss lab drops a session's last reference too early and crashes later, somewhere else. The script stops at control_ready, sets one hardware watchpoint on session 7's refcnt, and logs every write with a short backtrace.

break control_ready
run
watch -l session_table[7]->refcnt
set $prev = session_table[7]->refcnt
commands
printf "HIT %d->%d handle_timeout=%d\n", $prev, session_table[7]->refcnt, $_caller_is("handle_timeout")
set $prev = session_table[7]->refcnt
bt 3
continue
end
continue

  1. Lines 1–3: stopped at the hook. The watchpoint is a hardware one: refcnt is a 4-byte int, one debug register.
  2. Lines 5–11: thread 4 writes 1 → 2 inside session_get. Each hit prints Old value, New value and the location just after the store. That thread didn't exist yet when the watchpoint was set, and the write is still caught: GDB arms new threads too.
  3. Lines 13–22: thread 5 writes 2 → 1 in session_put. The commands block's $_caller_is test (line 19) and bt 3 (lines 20–22) show who called it: handle_timeout.
  4. Lines 24–30: thread 4 writes 1 → 0.
  5. Lines 32–35: the crash, in a different thread and function. Two more writes to the same 4 bytes happen in between, from inside free and from a new allocation; Ch 23 explains them.
10-refcount-uaf.c — full source
/* 10-refcount-uaf.c — boss level: a refcount bug whose crash lands far away.
 *
 * Worker 3's error path, handle_timeout(), drops a reference it never took.
 * Worker 2's own, correct put then takes session 7 to zero, and free() puts
 * the chunk in worker 2's tcache. Worker 2's next malloc is a struct pktbuf:
 * same 56-byte size class, same thread, so tcache hands back the same chunk.
 * Later the control thread's table_dump() reads session_table[7]->peer, which
 * is now packet bytes "IJKLMNOP" = 0x504f4e4d4c4b4a49. That address is
 * non-canonical, so the read is a SIGSEGV in code that did nothing wrong.
 *
 * Barriers put the six threads through the same phases on every run:
 *   0  control   creates sessions 1-8 (7 -> peers[2], refcnt 1); control_ready()
 *   1  worker 2  session_get(7)                       refcnt 1 -> 2
 *   2  worker 3  handle_timeout(7): a put, no get     refcnt 2 -> 1   (the bug)
 *   3  worker 2  session_put(7)                       refcnt 1 -> 0, free()
 *                malloc() + fill a pktbuf             gets the same chunk
 *   4  control   table_dump()                         SIGSEGV on session 7
 *
 *   ./10-refcount-uaf.plain              SIGSEGV in table_dump, every run
 *   make core-10-refcount-uaf            GDB writes cores/10-refcount-uaf.plain.core
 *   ./10-refcount-uaf.asan               heap-use-after-free, with the free's stack
 *   ./10-refcount-uaf.reftrace           session_check() prints the get/put history
 *
 * worker_main, handle_timeout and control_main are not static, so the
 * reftrace build (-rdynamic) can name them in its history.
 */
#include "pktd.h"

#define WORKERS 4
#define THREADS (WORKERS + 2)       /* + control + stats */

static pthread_barrier_t phase;
static const struct peer peers[4] = {
    { 0x0a000001, 179 }, { 0x0a000002, 179 }, { 0x0a000003, 179 }, { 0x0a000004, 179 },
};

/* A hook for debuggers: sessions 1-8 exist and no worker has touched them. */
__attribute__((noinline)) void control_ready(void) { __asm__ volatile("" ::: "memory"); }

static struct session *lookup(uint32_t id)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    struct session *s = session_table[id];
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
    return s;
}

/* Error path: the peer timed out, so count a drop and let the session go.
 * BUG: this thread never called session_get(), so it drops a reference
 * that belongs to someone else. */
void handle_timeout(struct session *s)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    stats.drops++;
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&stats_lock);
    session_put(s);
}

void *worker_main(void *arg)
{
    int me = (int)(intptr_t)arg;    /* 1..4 */
    struct session *s = NULL;
    struct pktbuf *pb = NULL;

    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);   /* 0: sessions exist */
    if (me == 2) {
        s = lookup(7);
        session_get(s);             /* 1: our reference, 1 -> 2 */
    }
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    if (me == 3)
        handle_timeout(lookup(7));  /* 2: 2 -> 1 */
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    if (me == 2) {
        session_put(s);             /* 3: our put, correct, but 1 -> 0: free() */
        pb = malloc(sizeof *pb);    /* the next packet: same thread, same bin */
        pb->len = sizeof pb->data;
        pb->port = 179;
        pb->ts = now_ns();
        memcpy(pb->data, "ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklm", sizeof pb->data);
    }
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);   /* 4: control dumps the table */
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);   /* done */
    free(pb);
    return NULL;
}

void *control_main(void *arg)
{
    (void)arg;
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    for (uint32_t id = 1; id <= 8; id++)
        session_table[id] = session_new(id, &peers[(id - 1) % 4]);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
    control_ready();
    for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++)     /* let the workers run phases 0-3 */
        pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    table_dump();                   /* 4: crashes on session 7 */
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    return NULL;
}

static void *stats_main(void *arg)
{
    (void)arg;
    for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++)
        pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    printf("stats: pkts=%lu drops=%lu\n", (unsigned long)stats.pkts, (unsigned long)stats.drops);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&stats_lock);
    return NULL;
}

int main(void)
{
    pthread_t tid[THREADS];

    setvbuf(stdout, NULL, _IOLBF, 0);   /* each table line is out before the crash */
    pthread_barrier_init(&phase, NULL, THREADS);
    pthread_create(&tid[0], NULL, control_main, NULL);
    for (int i = 1; i <= WORKERS; i++)
        pthread_create(&tid[i], NULL, worker_main, (void *)(intptr_t)i);
    pthread_create(&tid[THREADS - 1], NULL, stats_main, NULL);
    for (int i = 0; i < THREADS; i++)
        pthread_join(tid[i], NULL);
    puts("pktd: clean exit");
    return 0;
}
gdb/10-watch.gdb — full source
# 10-watch.gdb — catch every write to session 7's refcnt, whichever thread makes it.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/10-watch.gdb ./10-refcount-uaf.plain
# -l watches the address, not the expression, so the watchpoint survives
# frames returning and threads switching. Each hit prints one summary line
# (old->new, and whether the caller is handle_timeout), then a short backtrace.
set pagination off
break control_ready
run
watch -l session_table[7]->refcnt
set $prev = session_table[7]->refcnt
commands
printf "HIT %d->%d handle_timeout=%d\n", $prev, session_table[7]->refcnt, $_caller_is("handle_timeout")
set $prev = session_table[7]->refcnt
bt 3
continue
end
continue

7.4 Threads, frames and raw memory

CommandDoes
info threadsOne line per thread: GDB's number, LWP, where it is. * marks the current one
thread 4Make thread 4 current. bt, frame and p now look at it
thread apply all btBacktrace of every thread. The first thing to run on a hang (Ch 22)
bt full, bt 3Add locals to each frame; or show only the innermost 3
frame 2, up, down, frame function table_dumpSelect a frame by number, by direction or by function name
info frameThe frame's CFA, saved rip and saved registers
x/8gx ptr, x/s, x/10i $pc, x/16a $rspExamine memory: count, format (x hex, d decimal, s string, i instruction, a address with symbol), size (b/h/w/g)
ptype /o struct sessionThe type with every field's offset and size
info proc mappingsThe process map, to classify an address (Ch 1)

Here are four of them on f2-frames from Ch 2: the breakpoint, bt, info frame and x/4gx $rsp.

Breakpoint 1 at 0x1198: file f2-frames.c, line 11.
⋯
Breakpoint 1, checksum (data=0x7fffffffebe0 "BGP!", len=4) at f2-frames.c:11

#0  checksum (data=0x7fffffffebe0 "BGP!", len=4) at f2-frames.c:11

#2  0x000055555555527a in rx_packet (port=179) at f2-frames.c:27
#3  0x00005555555552a6 in main () at f2-frames.c:32

0x7fffffffeb88:	0x00007fffffffebb0	0x00005555555551fe
