  1. Line 1: break checksum resolved to an offset in the PIE binary. It becomes an absolute address once the program is loaded.
  2. Lines 5–8: bt. Outer frames show a return address, inside the caller.
  3. Lines 9–16: info frame. The CFA, the saved rip, and where the saved registers sit on the stack.
  4. Lines 17–18: x/4gx $rsp, four 8-byte words. The first two are the saved rbp and the return address. Ch 2 walks every word.

7.5 All-stop and scheduler-locking

By default GDB runs in all-stop mode. When any thread stops, GDB stops all of them. When you resume, all of them resume. That includes step and next.

  • So while you next over one line in worker 2, the other five threads run too. One of them may hit a breakpoint, and GDB switches you to it mid-step.
  • set scheduler-locking controls which threads run when you resume:
ModeOther threadsUse it when
offAlways runNormal execution
stepHeld during step/next; run on continueStepping through one thread's logic without being yanked away
onNever runForcing one interleaving by hand. If the current thread waits on a lock another thread holds, it hangs
replay (default)Like on while replaying GDB's own record log, off otherwiseLeave it. Under rr it matters less: rr serializes the threads itself
Stopping changes timing. A breakpoint holds every thread for as long as you look. A race may vanish, or a timeout may fire that never fires in production. If the bug depends on timing, record it with rr (Ch 13) and debug the recording instead.
Deep dive

Non-stop mode

  • set non-stop on, set before run or attach, stops only the thread that hit an event. The others keep running.
  • Use it when the daemon must keep serving, for example to keep a watchdog fed, while you inspect one thread.
  • continue -a resumes all stopped threads; interrupt -a stops all of them.

Signals

  • GDB intercepts signals before the program sees them. info signals shows what it does with each.
  • Daemons that use SIGUSR1 or SIGPIPE internally will keep stopping. handle SIGPIPE nostop noprint pass lets them through.
  • At a fault, p $_siginfo._sifields._sigfault.si_addr gives the faulting address. For a non-canonical pointer it reads 0: the CPU raised a general-protection fault, not a page fault (Ch 1).

Watching more than 8 bytes

  • One debug register covers at most 8 aligned bytes. A whole 56-byte struct session would need seven, so it doesn't fit.
  • Watch the fields that matter (magic, refcnt, peer) as separate watch -l points, within the four-register budget.
→ Ch 2. x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 13. rr → Ch 23. Boss Level

8. Getting a Usable Core & Symbols

What you'll learn: how a crash turns into a core file, where the kernel sends it, how to take one from a live process, and how to give GDB the exact symbols for a stripped binary.

Why it works: a core is a snapshot of the mappings in Ch 1, Process Memory Layout, plus each thread's registers. Symbols turn its raw addresses back into the frames of Ch 2.

8.1 From crash to core file

When a process dies of a signal whose default action is to dump core (SIGSEGV, SIGABRT, SIGBUS, SIGFPE, SIGILL…), the kernel may write a core. Two settings decide whether, and where.

ulimit -c: the process's core limit

  • It's RLIMIT_CORE, inherited from the parent. Many shells and service managers start it at 0: no core.
  • ulimit -c unlimited in the shell that starts the program. For a systemd service, LimitCORE=infinity in its unit.
  • The limit binds file cores. A piped core_pattern (below) still runs its handler, which is told the limit and decides for itself.
  • Non-dumpable processes get no core at all, file or pipe. A daemon that drops privileges (setuid and friends) becomes non-dumpable unless fs.suid_dumpable allows it. The fix in the daemon is prctl(PR_SET_DUMPABLE, 1) after the drop.

core_pattern: where it goes

  • /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern is one setting for the whole host.
  • A plain path is a file template. %e is the executable name, %p the PID, %t the time. A generic example: /var/tmp/core.%e.%p.%t.
  • A value starting with | pipes the core to a handler program instead: systemd-coredump, apport and the like. Then no file appears next to your program.
  • Check yours with cat /proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern before you go looking for a file.

If the host uses systemd-coredump

coredumpctl lists, inspects and extracts what it stored.

$ coredumpctl list pktd
TIME                          PID  UID  GID SIG     COREFILE EXE            SIZE
Tue 2026-11-17 09:14:02 UTC  4127  998  998 SIGSEGV present  /usr/sbin/pktd 1.8M
$ coredumpctl info 4127          # signal, command line, a short backtrace
$ coredumpctl dump 4127 -o pktd.core
$ coredumpctl debug 4127         # opens GDB on the binary and the core

8.2 Taking a core yourself

  • From a live process: gcore -o pktd.core <pid> writes pktd.core.<pid>. It attaches, dumps and detaches. The process stops only while the dump is written, then carries on. This is how you capture a hang without killing it (Ch 22).
  • From inside GDB: generate-core-file <file> (alias gcore) dumps the process GDB is debugging, right where it stopped.

Why the labs let GDB write the core

  • A container can't change core_pattern. The setting belongs to the host kernel and isn't namespaced. On a host that pipes cores to a handler, a crash inside a container goes to the host's handler, not to your working directory.
  • So the lab container runs with --ulimit core=0. With a file pattern, the kernel writes nothing when the limit is 0. With a pipe, the kernel ignores the limit: it still starts the handler and passes the limit to it (%c). systemd-coredump honours 0 and keeps no core; other handlers may not.
  • make core-<lab> runs the lab under GDB and writes the core at the fault. GDB reads the stopped process through ptrace and writes the file itself, so the core limit doesn't apply. The same target works on your own box and on a shared host.
CORE = cores/$*.$(V)$(if $(ARGS),-$(ARGS)).core

core-%: %.$(V)
	@mkdir -p cores
	@rm -f $(CORE)
	-gdb -batch -ex "run $(ARGS)" -ex "generate-core-file $(CORE)" ./$<
	@test -f $(CORE) || { echo "core-$*: ./$< did not fault, no core written" >&2; exit 1; }

8.3 Build IDs and split debug info

A core holds addresses. To name them, GDB needs the exact binary that crashed, and its debug info. The build ID is how it knows it has the right one.

  • The linker writes a hash of the binary's contents into a .note.gnu.build-id note. Rebuild with any change and it changes.
  • By default the kernel dumps the first page of each mapped ELF file (coredump_filter bit 4). That page holds the build-ID note, so a core records the build ID of every binary and library it had loaded.
01-null.stripped: ELF 64-bit LSB pie executable, x86-64, version 1 (SYSV), dynamically linked, interpreter /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2, BuildID[sha1]=94110480f02a93766dd2776b7ae3d679657e4790, for GNU/Linux 3.2.0, stripped
    Build ID: 94110480f02a93766dd2776b7ae3d679657e4790
  1. Line 1: file shows the build ID and says stripped: no symbol table, no DWARF.
  2. Line 2: readelf -n prints the same note. This hash is what you match against a core and against a debug file.

Split debug info

Ship the stripped binary; keep the debug info. The labs' stripped variant does it in three steps:

%.stripped: %.plain
	cp $< $@
	objcopy --only-keep-debug $@ $*.debug
	strip $@
	objcopy --add-gnu-debuglink=$*.debug $@
  • --only-keep-debug copies the DWARF and symbol table into <lab>.debug.
  • strip removes them from the binary. Only .dynsym, the symbols the dynamic linker needs, remains.
  • --add-gnu-debuglink adds a .gnu_debuglink section: the debug file's name plus a CRC of its contents.

Where GDB looks

  • By build ID: <debug-file-directory>/.build-id/ab/cdef….debug, where ab is the first byte of the ID. The default directory is /usr/lib/debug. Distro -dbg/-dbgsym packages install there.
  • By debuglink: the named file in the binary's own directory, then in its .debug/ subdirectory, then under the debug directory.

The rr capture in Ch 13 shows a build-ID lookup, for the dynamic loader:

Reading symbols from /lib64/ld-linux-x86-64.so.2...
Reading symbols from /usr/lib/debug/.build-id/23/1ab65dd867a89dce29cb958fd5ca50abb1aa34.debug...
  1. Line 2: GDB found ld-linux's debug info under /usr/lib/debug/.build-id/23/…, the directory named by the first byte of its build ID.

8.4 Watch it: a stripped core, before and after symbols

The boss lab's stripped build crashes and GDB writes a core. The binary is copied to a directory with no .debug next to it, and GDB's debug directory points nowhere, so it finds no symbols at all. Then symbol-file loads the split debug file by hand.

Core was generated by `/lab/10-refcount-uaf.stripped'.

  1. Lines 1–2: the core names the program and the signal. That much needs no symbols.
  2. Lines 5–8: every frame is ??. The faulting rip is known; its function isn't.
  3. Line 12: the one name you do get is pthread_barrier_wait. It's an exported libc function, so it's in libc's .dynsym, which strip never removes. Internal functions stay ??.
  4. Lines 14–15: after symbol-file 10-refcount-uaf.debug, the same addresses resolve: table_dump at pktd.h:191, called from control_main.
  5. Lines 16–17: libc's frames are still ??. Its debug file would come from the build-ID directory, and that lookup was disabled for this capture.
  6. Lines 19–21: thread apply all bt 2 now names every thread running program code: here the stats thread, parked in a barrier.
  7. Lines 23–25: the main thread waits in libc internals, so it stays unnamed without libc's debug info.
gdb/t2-stripped.gdb — full source
# t2-stripped.gdb — a core from a stripped binary, before and after its symbols.
#   make core-10-refcount-uaf V=stripped
#   mkdir -p cores/nodebug && cp 10-refcount-uaf.stripped cores/nodebug/
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -iex 'set debug-file-directory /nonexistent' -x gdb/t2-stripped.gdb \
#       cores/nodebug/10-refcount-uaf.stripped cores/10-refcount-uaf.stripped.core
# The copy in cores/nodebug/ has no .debug next to it, so GDB finds no symbols.
set pagination off
bt
info threads
symbol-file 10-refcount-uaf.debug
bt
thread apply all bt 2
quit

8.5 debuginfod: symbols on demand

  • A debuginfod server hands out debug files, executables and sources, looked up by build ID over HTTP.
  • Point clients at one with DEBUGINFOD_URLS="https://…". Several distros run public servers for their own packages, and some set the variable for you.
  • GDB (12 and later) asks once per session before it downloads. set debuginfod enabled on skips the question.
  • From a shell: debuginfod-find debuginfo <build-id> fetches one file into the local cache.
  • For your own daemon, run a debuginfod server over your build artifacts. A core from any build then resolves itself, as long as you kept that build's debug files.
Keep the debug file for every build you ship. Without the matching build ID you have addresses and nothing else. A debug file from a different build, even of the same commit, can name the wrong functions.
Deep dive

Matching a core to its binary

  • Start with Core was generated by … and the build IDs. eu-unstrip -n --core=core (elfutils) lists every module in a core with its build ID.
  • GDB checks the executable's build ID against the core. On a mismatch it warns that the core file may not match the executable. Believe it: frames and locals will be wrong.
  • A core from another machine needs that machine's libraries too. Copy the binary and its exact libraries into a directory tree and set sysroot /that/dir before loading the core.

coredump_filter

  • /proc/<pid>/coredump_filter picks which mappings go into a core. The default, 0x33, dumps private and shared anonymous memory, ELF header pages and private huge pages.
  • File-backed code isn't dumped. The core expects you to supply the same binaries.
  • GDB's gcore honours the same filter (set use-coredump-filter, on by default).

Cores and sanitizers

  • ASan disables cores by default on 64-bit, because its shadow reserves terabytes of address space. ASAN_OPTIONS=disable_coredump=0:abort_on_error=1 gets you one (Ch 10).
→ Ch 1. Process Memory Layout → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 22. Lock-Order Deadlock → Lab Kit

9. GDB on a Core

What you'll learn: what a core does and doesn't hold, how to read threads, registers and frames from it, and how to script GDB in Python so it walks pktd's session table for you.

Why it works: a core is raw memory. You read it with the chunk layout from Ch 3, The glibc Allocator and the address map from Ch 1, Process Memory Layout.

9.1 What is in a core, and what isn't

A Linux core is an ELF file of type CORE: a set of notes, then memory.

In the coreNot in the core
Each thread's general registers and signal state (NT_PRSTATUS), and its FP/vector registersThe program's code and libraries: you supply the same files
Process info: command name and state (NT_PRPSINFO)Symbols and debug info (Ch 8)
The signal that killed it, with the fault address (NT_SIGINFO)History: how the process got here
The auxiliary vector (NT_AUXV): AT_ENTRY and AT_PHDR let GDB work out where a PIE binary was loadedOther processes' memory, even ones the daemon talks to
The list of mapped files (NT_FILE), which info proc mappings readsKernel-side state: what an fd points at, socket buffers, anything about a waiting thread beyond its registers
Writable memory: stacks, heap, data, bss, anonymous mappingsAnything already returned to the kernel
Freed heap memory that malloc kept is still in the core, old contents and all. That's evidence (Ch 3).

9.2 Opening a core

gdb ./binary core. Here is the boss lab's core, with bt as the first command.

[New LWP 105]
[New LWP 102]
[New LWP 106]
[New LWP 107]
[New LWP 108]
[New LWP 109]
[New LWP 110]
[Thread debugging using libthread_db enabled]
Using host libthread_db library "/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libthread_db.so.1".
Core was generated by `/lab/10-refcount-uaf.plain'.
Program terminated with signal SIGSEGV, Segmentation fault.
#0  table_dump () at /lab/pktd.h:191
191	               (s->peer->addr >> 8) & 0xff, s->peer->addr & 0xff, s->peer->port);
[Current thread is 1 (Thread 0x7ffff7da26c0 (LWP 105))]
#0  table_dump () at /lab/pktd.h:191
#1  0x0000555555555825 in control_main (arg=0x0) at 10-refcount-uaf.c:98
#2  0x00007ffff7e42b64 in start_thread (arg=<optimized out>) at ./nptl/pthread_create.c:447
#3  0x00007ffff7ecfe8c in clone3 () at ../sysdeps/unix/sysv/linux/x86_64/clone3.S:78
  1. Lines 1–7: one LWP per thread in the core.
  2. Line 10: the command line that crashed. Check it's the binary you passed.
  3. Line 11: the fatal signal. SIGSEGV is a bad access; SIGABRT means something called abort(), usually glibc or an assert.
  4. Line 14: the current thread is the one that took the signal. Here it's thread 1, LWP 105: the control thread, listed first. The main thread, LWP 102, is listed second.
  5. Lines 15–18: its backtrace. Frame #0 is where it died, not necessarily where the bug is.

The first commands to run

CommandTells you
bt, thread apply all btWhere each thread was
info registers, p $rip, x/i $pcThe faulting instruction and its operands
p $_siginfo._sifields._sigfault.si_addrThe address that faulted (0 for a non-canonical one)
frame N, info locals, p *sThe variables in one frame
info proc mappingsWhich file-backed mapping (binary, library) a pointer falls in. On a core it has no heap or stack lines (NT_FILE only)
info files, maint info sections, info symbolThe core's own load segments, heap and stacks included; the object and symbol an address belongs to
x/8gx ptr-16A heap object with its chunk header
info sharedlibraryWhich libraries were loaded, and whether their symbols were found
  • Nothing runs in a core. continue, step and calls to the program's functions (p strlen(s)) don't work. GDB can only read.
  • Registers in outer frames are what the unwinder recovered. Callee-saved ones are reliable; scratch ones aren't (Ch 2).

9.3 Teach GDB your data structures

Real daemons keep their state in tables, lists and pools. Walking them by hand with p and x is slow and error-prone. GDB embeds Python, so write the walk once.

pktd-gdb.py adds one command, pktd-sessions. Its core:

class PktdSessions(gdb.Command):
    """List pktd's session_table: slot, address, state, refcnt, id, name, peer."""

    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__("pktd-sessions", gdb.COMMAND_DATA)

    def invoke(self, arg, from_tty):
        table = gdb.parse_and_eval("session_table")
        n = table.type.range()[1] + 1
        used = 0
        for i in range(n):
            s = table[i]
            if int(s) == 0:
                continue
            used += 1
            head = "[%2d] %#x" % (i, int(s))
            try:
                d = s.dereference()
                magic = int(d["magic"]) & 0xFFFFFFFF
                refcnt = int(d["refcnt"])
                sid = int(d["id"])
            except gdb.MemoryError:
                gdb.write("%s  UNREADABLE\n" % head)
                continue
            if magic == SESSION_MAGIC:
                name = d["name"].string(length=24, errors="replace").split("\0")[0]
                gdb.write("%s  live  refcnt=%d id=%u name=%s peer=%s\n"
                          % (head, refcnt, sid, name, _peer(d)))
            elif magic == SESSION_DEAD:
                gdb.write("%s  DEAD  refcnt=%d id=%u  (poisoned by final put)\n"
                          % (head, refcnt, sid))
            else:
                gdb.write("%s  BAD MAGIC %#010x refcnt=%d id=%u  (freed or corrupt?)\n"
                          % (head, magic, refcnt, sid))
        gdb.write("%d of %d slots in use\n" % (used, n))
  • A gdb.Command subclass becomes a new command. invoke runs when you type it.
  • gdb.parse_and_eval("session_table") returns a gdb.Value with the program's real type. Index it, take fields with ["magic"], follow pointers with .dereference().
  • Reading through a bad pointer raises gdb.MemoryError. Catch it, or one corrupt entry stops the whole walk.
  • Check magic before trusting any other field. In a core, the object behind a pointer may be freed or reused.
  • It only reads memory, so it works the same on a live process and on a core.

9.4 Watch it: live, then on the core

First on the live boss lab, stopped at control_ready, before any worker has touched a session:

Thread 2 "10-refcount-uaf" hit Breakpoint 1, control_ready () at 10-refcount-uaf.c:38

[ 3] 0x7ffff0000bf0  live  refcnt=1 id=3 name=sess-3 peer=10.0.0.3:179
[ 4] 0x7ffff0000c30  live  refcnt=1 id=4 name=sess-4 peer=10.0.0.4:179
[ 5] 0x7ffff0000c70  live  refcnt=1 id=5 name=sess-5 peer=10.0.0.1:179
[ 6] 0x7ffff0000cb0  live  refcnt=1 id=6 name=sess-6 peer=10.0.0.2:179
[ 7] 0x7ffff0000cf0  BAD MAGIC 0x00000028 refcnt=179 id=3798382428  (freed or corrupt?)
[ 8] 0x7ffff0000d30  live  refcnt=1 id=8 name=sess-8 peer=10.0.0.4:179
8 of 64 slots in use
  1. Line 7: slot 7, same address, fails the magic check. refcnt=179 and a huge id are not session values. Something else owns this chunk now.
  2. The other slots still pass. The helper flagged the one bad entry and kept going, instead of faulting on its peer the way table_dump did.
  3. Whose bytes these are, and how they got there, is the boss level (Ch 23).
pktd-gdb.py — full source
# pktd-gdb.py — GDB helper for pktd: the "pktd-sessions" command.
#   (gdb) source pktd-gdb.py
#   (gdb) pktd-sessions
# Walks session_table[] and prints one line per non-NULL slot. It checks the
# magic before trusting any other field, so a freed, poisoned or garbage
# entry is labelled rather than dereferenced blindly. Works live or on a core.
import gdb

SESSION_MAGIC = 0x5E55102B
SESSION_DEAD = 0xDEADBEEF


def _peer(s):
    p = s["peer"]
    if int(p) == 0:
        return "NULL"
    try:
        addr = int(p.dereference()["addr"])
        port = int(p.dereference()["port"])
    except gdb.MemoryError:
        return "%#x (unreadable)" % int(p)
    return "%d.%d.%d.%d:%d" % (addr >> 24, (addr >> 16) & 0xFF, (addr >> 8) & 0xFF, addr & 0xFF, port)


class PktdSessions(gdb.Command):
    """List pktd's session_table: slot, address, state, refcnt, id, name, peer."""

    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__("pktd-sessions", gdb.COMMAND_DATA)

    def invoke(self, arg, from_tty):
        table = gdb.parse_and_eval("session_table")
        n = table.type.range()[1] + 1
        used = 0
        for i in range(n):
            s = table[i]
            if int(s) == 0:
                continue
            used += 1
            head = "[%2d] %#x" % (i, int(s))
            try:
                d = s.dereference()
                magic = int(d["magic"]) & 0xFFFFFFFF
                refcnt = int(d["refcnt"])
                sid = int(d["id"])
            except gdb.MemoryError:
                gdb.write("%s  UNREADABLE\n" % head)
                continue
            if magic == SESSION_MAGIC:
                name = d["name"].string(length=24, errors="replace").split("\0")[0]
                gdb.write("%s  live  refcnt=%d id=%u name=%s peer=%s\n"
                          % (head, refcnt, sid, name, _peer(d)))
            elif magic == SESSION_DEAD:
                gdb.write("%s  DEAD  refcnt=%d id=%u  (poisoned by final put)\n"
                          % (head, refcnt, sid))
            else:
                gdb.write("%s  BAD MAGIC %#010x refcnt=%d id=%u  (freed or corrupt?)\n"
                          % (head, magic, refcnt, sid))
        gdb.write("%d of %d slots in use\n" % (used, n))


PktdSessions()
gdb/pktd-sessions.gdb — full source
# pktd-sessions.gdb — stop pktd once its session table is built, then list it.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/pktd-sessions.gdb ./10-refcount-uaf.plain
# control_ready() runs just after the control thread creates sessions 1-8,
# before any worker touches them, so every slot should read "live refcnt=1".
# On a core, skip the break: "source pktd-gdb.py" then "pktd-sessions".
set pagination off
source pktd-gdb.py
break control_ready
run
pktd-sessions
kill
quit
Deep dive

Loading helpers automatically

  • source pktd-gdb.py by hand, or put that line in a .gdbinit.
  • GDB refuses to auto-load scripts from directories it doesn't trust. add-auto-load-safe-path /path/to/tree in ~/.gdbinit allows them.
  • A file named <objfile>-gdb.py next to the binary, or under the debug directory, is loaded with that binary. Ship it with your debug files.

Pretty-printers

  • A command prints on request. A pretty-printer changes how p shows a type everywhere: in bt full, info locals and frame lines.
  • Register one with gdb.printing.RegexpCollectionPrettyPrinter. A struct session printer could flag a bad magic and show the peer as a dotted quad.
  • Keep printers defensive: they also run on freed and corrupt objects.

Cores of hung processes

  • A gcore of a hang has no fatal signal. Start with thread apply all bt and look for threads blocked in futex_wait or __lll_lock_wait.
  • Each mutex's __data.__owner names the LWP that holds it (Ch 6).
→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 8. Getting a Usable Core & Symbols → Ch 19. Simple Use-After-Free → Ch 23. Boss Level

10. The ASan Family

What you'll learn: what ASan, UBSan, LSan and MSan each catch, how to read an ASan report's three stacks, and which ASAN_OPTIONS change what you get.

Why it works: shadow memory, redzones and quarantine are explained in Ch 4, Instrumentation Models. This chapter is about building with them and reading their output.

10.1 Four sanitizers, four jobs

SanitizerBuild withCatchesMisses
ASan-fsanitize=addressHeap, stack and global overflows; use-after-free; double and invalid free; use-after-scopeUninitialized reads; races; an access that jumps past a redzone into another live object; reuse after a block leaves quarantine
UBSan-fsanitize=undefinedSigned overflow, bad shifts, misaligned or NULL access, out-of-bounds index into a known-size array, division by zeroHeap memory errors
LSanBuilt into ASan, or -fsanitize=leakBlocks nothing points to, at exitMemory still referenced but never used: growth, not a leak (Ch 20)
MSanClang only: -fsanitize=memoryBranching on, dereferencing or passing uninitialized bits (Ch 15)Everything else. Every library must be instrumented, or you get false reports

Building

  • Add -g for file and line, and -fno-omit-frame-pointer: ASan records alloc and free stacks with a fast frame-pointer unwinder.
  • -O1 is a good default for speed. The labs use -O0 so locals stay visible.
  • The labs' asan variant builds ASan and UBSan together. Those two combine; ASan, TSan and MSan don't (Ch 4).
  • Reports need symbolizing. GCC's runtimes (as in these labs' asan and tsan builds) symbolize in-process with a bundled libbacktrace, so the GCC captures set no option. Clang's runtimes look for llvm-symbolizer and don't fall back to addr2line by default. The lab image has no llvm-symbolizer, so the Clang MSan lab passes MSAN_OPTIONS=external_symbolizer_path=/usr/bin/addr2line (Ch 15).

10.2 Watch it: a use-after-free, three stacks

06-uaf: a policy check frees an rx job, and the rx path reads it anyway.

/* Policy says drop: release the job. The caller is not told. */
static int rx_policy(struct rx_job *job)
{
    if (job->port == 179 && job->buf->len > 4096) {
        free(job);
        return -1;
    }
    return 0;
}

static uint32_t rx_process(struct rx_job *job)
{
    return job->buf->len;           /* BUG: job may already be freed */
}

==28==ABORTING
  1. Line 2: the bug class and the bad address. ==28== is the PID.
  2. Line 3: what the access was: an 8-byte read, in thread T0 (ASan's numbering; T0 is the main thread).
  3. Lines 4–5: stack 1, the use. rx_process at line 49 loads job->buf.
  4. Line 7: which block it hit: offset 0 of a 40-byte region. Offset 0 is buf.
  5. Lines 8–11: stack 2, the free. rx_policy freed it at line 41. This is usually where the fix goes.
  6. Lines 13–16: stack 3, the allocation. rx_enqueue created it at line 27.
  7. Line 18: the one-line summary. Grep logs for SUMMARY: AddressSanitizer.
  8. Line 21: the shadow row. fa fa is the 16-byte left redzone, [fd] marks the 8 bytes accessed, and five fd bytes cover all 40 freed bytes.
  9. Lines 24–28: the legend for the shadow values used here.
  10. Line 30: ASan stops at the first error by default.

The plain build of the same lab faults inside rx_process on a pointer the allocator wrote (Ch 19). ASan reports the read itself, because the block sits poisoned in quarantine.

06-uaf.c — full source
/* 06-uaf.c — a policy drop frees an rx_job, but the rx path keeps using it.
 *
 * struct rx_job is 40 bytes: a 0x30 chunk, a size class nothing else in this
 * program allocates, and only one job is ever allocated. So its tcache bin is
 * empty when rx_policy() frees it, and glibc's safe-linked 'next' pointer,
 * (pos >> 12) ^ NULL, lands on job->buf. The plain build then loads
 * buf->len from an unmapped address (job >> 12) and faults every time.
 *
 *   make 06-uaf.asan && ./06-uaf.asan
 *   valgrind ./06-uaf.plain
 *   make core-06-uaf
 */
#include "pktd.h"

struct rx_job {                     /* 40 bytes */
    struct pktbuf *buf;             /* 0x00  tcache 'next' after free */
    uint64_t enq_ns;                /* 0x08  tcache 'key' after free */
    uint64_t seq;                   /* 0x10 */
    uint32_t port;                  /* 0x18 */
    uint32_t prio;                  /* 0x1c */
    uint64_t flags;                 /* 0x20 */
};
_Static_assert(sizeof(struct rx_job) == 40, "rx_job is 40 bytes (0x30 chunk)");

static struct rx_job *rx_enqueue(struct pktbuf *b, uint64_t seq)
{
    struct rx_job *job = malloc(sizeof *job);
    job->buf = b;
    job->enq_ns = now_ns();
    job->seq = seq;
    job->port = b->port;
    job->prio = 0;
    job->flags = 0;
    return job;
}

/* Policy says drop: release the job. The caller is not told. */
static int rx_policy(struct rx_job *job)
{
    if (job->port == 179 && job->buf->len > 4096) {
        free(job);
        return -1;
    }
    return 0;
}

static uint32_t rx_process(struct rx_job *job)
{
    return job->buf->len;           /* BUG: job may already be freed */
}

int main(void)
{
    struct pktbuf *b = malloc(sizeof *b);
    b->len = 9000;                  /* oversized BGP frame: policy drops it */
    b->port = 179;
    b->ts = now_ns();

    struct rx_job *job = rx_enqueue(b, 1);
    if (rx_policy(job) < 0)
        fprintf(stderr, "rx: job 1 dropped by policy\n");
    printf("rx: job 1, %u bytes\n", rx_process(job));   /* BUG: ignores the drop */
    free(b);
    return 0;
}

10.3 ASAN_OPTIONS that matter

Set them as ASAN_OPTIONS=opt1=v1:opt2=v2. ASAN_OPTIONS=help=1 prints every option with its default.

OptionDefaultWhat it changes
halt_on_error1Stop at the first report. 0 continues, but only if you built with -fsanitize-recover=address
abort_on_error0 on Linux1 calls abort() instead of _exit(1), so a debugger or core handler sees a SIGABRT
disable_coredump1 on 64-bitASan turns off cores. 0 allows them; the shadow is left out of the dump
detect_leaks1 on x86-64 LinuxRun LSan at exit. 0 skips it
quarantine_size_mb−1 (= 256 MB on 64-bit)How much freed memory is held back before reuse. Bigger catches a use-after-free after more churn
malloc_context_size30Frames kept for each alloc and free stack
fast_unwind_on_malloc1Record alloc/free stacks via frame pointers. 0 uses the slow DWARF unwinder, for code built without them
detect_stack_use_after_returnVaries by compiler versionCatch use of a returned function's locals. Costs memory
log_pathstderrWrite reports to <path>.<pid>. Essential for daemons whose stderr goes nowhere
symbolize10 prints raw addresses, to symbolize later against the exact binary
  • LSan, UBSan and MSan read LSAN_OPTIONS, UBSAN_OPTIONS and MSAN_OPTIONS.
  • UBSan prints and keeps going by default. UBSAN_OPTIONS=print_stacktrace=1:halt_on_error=1, or building with -fno-sanitize-recover=undefined, makes it stop.

10.4 LSan and MSan in practice

LSan

  • It runs once, at exit. A daemon that never exits never reports. Call __lsan_do_recoverable_leak_check() from a debug command to check while it runs.
  • It can't run under ptrace: under GDB or strace it refuses to check. In a container it may need SYS_PTRACE to stop and scan the other threads; that depends on the seccomp profile and Yama settings.
  • "Direct" leaks are blocks nothing points to. "Indirect" leaks are reachable only from leaked blocks. Fix the direct ones; the indirect ones usually go with them.

MSan

  • Add -fsanitize-memory-track-origins so a report also says where the uninitialized value was created: which stack variable or malloc.
  • Copying uninitialized bytes is fine. MSan reports when the value decides something: a branch, an address, a syscall argument.
  • C code that only calls libc works, because the runtime intercepts libc. C++ needs an instrumented standard library.
Deep dive

HWASan

  • Hardware-assisted ASan, mainly on AArch64. It uses top-byte-ignore: the CPU ignores a pointer's top byte, so the runtime can keep a tag there.
  • Each 16-byte granule of memory gets a tag too. malloc picks a random tag and sets it on both the pointer and the granules. Every access compares the two.
  • Free retags the memory. A stale pointer keeps its old tag, so a use-after-free mismatches, even after the memory is reused. No quarantine needed.
  • No redzones are needed either, so it uses much less memory than ASan.
  • The catch: with 8-bit tags, two tags collide about 1 time in 256. Detection is probabilistic. Arm's Memory Tagging Extension (MTE) does the same check in hardware, with 4-bit tags.
  • x86-64 has no top-byte-ignore, so HWASan support there is limited. For x86 daemons, ASan is the tool.

Suppressing and annotating

  • __attribute__((no_sanitize("address"))) turns off instrumentation in one function, for code that reads memory on purpose, such as a custom allocator walking its free list.
  • A custom pool allocator hides use-after-free from ASan: the memory never goes back to free. Call ASAN_POISON_MEMORY_REGION when an object returns to the pool and ASAN_UNPOISON_MEMORY_REGION when you hand it out (<sanitizer/asan_interface.h>).
→ Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 16. Heap Overflow, Off-by-One → Ch 19. Simple Use-After-Free → Build-Flag Matrix

11. TSan

What you'll learn: how to read a data-race report and a lock-order-inversion report, how TSan treats atomics and hand-rolled synchronization, and how to suppress what you can't fix yet.

Why it works: TSan computes happens-before from Ch 5, The C Memory Model and records the lock-order graph from Ch 6, Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting.

11.1 Building and running

  • Compile and link with -fsanitize=thread. Add -g, and -O1 or higher for speed. The labs' tsan variant is gcc -O1 -g -fsanitize=thread.
  • Instrument every file that touches shared state. Accesses in uninstrumented code are invisible.
  • TSan keeps running after a report, prints a count at exit, and exits with status 66 if it reported anything (TSAN_OPTIONS=exitcode=N changes it). That makes it easy to gate in CI.
The setarch note. On some newer kernels with more mmap randomization, older TSan runtimes abort at startup with an "unexpected memory mapping" error. Running the program under setarch $(uname -m) -R, which disables ASLR for that one process, works around it. The labs' toolchain didn't need it.

11.2 Watch it: a data race

08-race: four workers each add a million to stats.pkts, without stats_lock.

static void *worker(void *arg)
{
    (void)arg;
    for (int i = 0; i < PKTS_PER_WORKER; i++) {
        if (use_atomic)
            atomic_fetch_add((_Atomic uint64_t *)&stats.pkts, 1);
        else
            stats.pkts++;           /* BUG: stats_lock not held */
    }
    return NULL;
}

  1. Line 2: the report type, and the PID.
  2. Lines 3–4: the current access, the one that completed the race: a read in T2, at 08-race.c:26.
  3. Lines 6–7: the previous conflicting access: a write in T1, on the same line. TSan restores this stack from its per-thread history, after the fact.
  4. Line 9: what the memory is: the global stats, at offset 0, which is pkts.
  5. Lines 11–17: where each thread was created. T1 and T2 are TSan's numbers; tid is the OS thread ID.
  6. Lines 21–22: a second report, a write against the previous write, is trimmed. TSan prints each distinct pair of stacks once.
  7. Line 23: this run also lost updates. TSan would have reported the race even if the count had come out right.
08-race.c — full source
/* 08-race.c — four workers count packets into stats.pkts without stats_lock.
 * "++" is load, add, store: two workers interleave and one update is lost.
 * Run with "atomic" to make the increment a single atomic read-modify-write.
 *
 *   ./08-race.plain                         (lost updates, no tool)
 *   make 08-race.tsan && ./08-race.tsan     (ThreadSanitizer)
 *   valgrind --tool=helgrind ./08-race.plain
 *   valgrind --tool=drd ./08-race.plain
 *   ./08-race.tsan atomic                   (fixed: no report, exact count)
 */
#include <stdatomic.h>
#include "pktd.h"

#define WORKERS 4
#define PKTS_PER_WORKER 1000000

static int use_atomic;

static void *worker(void *arg)
{
    (void)arg;
    for (int i = 0; i < PKTS_PER_WORKER; i++) {
        if (use_atomic)
            atomic_fetch_add((_Atomic uint64_t *)&stats.pkts, 1);
        else
            stats.pkts++;           /* BUG: stats_lock not held */
    }
    return NULL;
}

int main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    pthread_t tid[WORKERS];

    use_atomic = argc > 1 && strcmp(argv[1], "atomic") == 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < WORKERS; i++)
        pthread_create(&tid[i], NULL, worker, NULL);
    for (int i = 0; i < WORKERS; i++)
        pthread_join(tid[i], NULL);

    uint64_t expected = (uint64_t)WORKERS * PKTS_PER_WORKER;
    printf("expected=%lu actual=%lu lost=%lu\n", (unsigned long)expected,
           (unsigned long)stats.pkts, (unsigned long)(expected - stats.pkts));
    return 0;
}

11.3 How TSan treats atomics

Run the same lab with atomic: the increment becomes atomic_fetch_add.

expected=4000000 actual=4000000 lost=0
exit=0
  1. Line 1: no report, and no lost update.
  2. Line 2: exit status 0, not 66.

What TSan understands

  • C11 <stdatomic.h> and the __atomic_* builtins. The compiler turns each one into a call into the TSan runtime, with its memory order.
  • Two atomic accesses never race with each other.
  • Release and acquire create happens-before edges, exactly as in Ch 5. Relaxed atomics don't. So if a relaxed flag "publishes" plain data, TSan reports the plain data as racy. It's right.
  • The lab casts a plain uint64_t to _Atomic for brevity. Real code should declare the field _Atomic, so no plain access can sneak in.

What it doesn't

  • Hand-rolled volatile flags. TSan sees plain loads and stores and reports a race. By C11's rules it's a real data race; by the author's intent it's a false positive. Either way, the fix is an atomic.
  • Inline-asm locks and barriers. A spinlock written as lock cmpxchg in asm is invisible to TSan. It sees no synchronization, so every access the lock protects looks racy: false positives. Rewrite it with __atomic builtins, or annotate it.
  • Standalone fences. atomic_thread_fence isn't modelled. GCC warns about it under -fsanitize=thread (-Wtsan).

11.4 Watch it: a lock-order inversion

09-deadlock in its inversion mode: the control thread takes table_lock → stats_lock. After a barrier, the stats thread takes stats_lock → table_lock. The barrier keeps the two apart, so it never actually hangs.

static void *control_thread(void *arg)
{
    static const struct peer p = { 0x0a000001, 179 };
    (void)arg;
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[1] = session_new(1, &p);
    if (hang)
        usleep(100000);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    stats.pkts++;
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&stats_lock);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
⋯
static void *stats_thread(void *arg)
{
    int live = 0;
    (void)arg;
    if (!hang)
        pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    if (hang)
        usleep(100000);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);    /* BUG: stats_lock -> table_lock */
==================

  Thread T1 (tid=246, running) created by main thread at:
    #0 pthread_create ../../../../src/libsanitizer/tsan/tsan_interceptors_posix.cpp:1022 (libtsan.so.2+0x5ac1a) (BuildId: 2a13a7710e361d06f7babbea53065ca2be93f738)
    #1 main /lab/09-deadlock.c:63 (09-deadlock.tsan+0x1733) (BuildId: 95a74dd70908a69d9c6eb704cf96796d6af5aa78)

  Thread T2 (tid=247, running) created by main thread at:
    #0 pthread_create ../../../../src/libsanitizer/tsan/tsan_interceptors_posix.cpp:1022 (libtsan.so.2+0x5ac1a) (BuildId: 2a13a7710e361d06f7babbea53065ca2be93f738)
    #1 main /lab/09-deadlock.c:64 (09-deadlock.tsan+0x1751) (BuildId: 95a74dd70908a69d9c6eb704cf96796d6af5aa78)

SUMMARY: ThreadSanitizer: lock-order-inversion (potential deadlock) /lab/09-deadlock.c:29 in control_thread
==================
ThreadSanitizer: reported 1 warnings
stats: pkts=1 live=1
pktd: clean exit
  1. Line 3: the cycle. M0 => M1 means "M1 was taken while holding M0".
  2. Lines 5–7: control_thread line 29 takes stats_lock (M1) while holding table_lock (M0). That's pktd's documented order.
  3. Line 9: by default you get only the stack of the second lock. second_deadlock_stack=1 adds where the first one was taken.
  4. Lines 11–13: stats_thread line 48 takes table_lock (M0) while holding stats_lock (M1). The reverse order: this is the bug.
  5. Lines 26–27: the run finished cleanly. The deadlock was only possible, and TSan reported it anyway (Ch 6).
09-deadlock.c — full source
/* 09-deadlock.c — the stats thread takes stats_lock, then table_lock: the
 * reverse of pktd's documented order (table_lock -> stats_lock).
 *
 *   inversion  control finishes table -> stats, a barrier, then stats takes
 *              stats -> table. Never hangs, but both lock edges are recorded,
 *              so TSan and Helgrind report the cycle.
 *   hang       both threads sleep while holding their first lock: a real
 *              deadlock. Use it with gcore.
 *
 *   ./09-deadlock.tsan inversion
 *   valgrind --tool=helgrind ./09-deadlock.plain inversion
 *   ./09-deadlock.plain hang &  sleep 2;  gcore -o cores/09 $!
 */
#include <unistd.h>
#include "pktd.h"

static pthread_barrier_t phase;
static int hang;

/* Control thread: add a session, then count it. table_lock -> stats_lock. */
static void *control_thread(void *arg)
{
    static const struct peer p = { 0x0a000001, 179 };
    (void)arg;
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[1] = session_new(1, &p);
    if (hang)
        usleep(100000);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    stats.pkts++;
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&stats_lock);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
    if (!hang)
        pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    return NULL;
}

/* Stats thread: snapshot the counters, then count live sessions. */
static void *stats_thread(void *arg)
{
    int live = 0;
    (void)arg;
    if (!hang)
        pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    if (hang)
        usleep(100000);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);    /* BUG: stats_lock -> table_lock */
    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_SESSIONS; i++)
        live += session_table[i] != NULL;
    printf("stats: pkts=%lu live=%d\n", (unsigned long)stats.pkts, live);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&stats_lock);
    return NULL;
}

int main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    pthread_t ctl, st;

    hang = argc > 1 && strcmp(argv[1], "hang") == 0;
    pthread_barrier_init(&phase, NULL, 2);
    pthread_create(&ctl, NULL, control_thread, NULL);
    pthread_create(&st, NULL, stats_thread, NULL);
    pthread_join(ctl, NULL);
    pthread_join(st, NULL);
    puts("pktd: clean exit");
    return 0;
}

11.5 TSAN_OPTIONS and suppressions

OptionDefaultWhat it changes
halt_on_error01 exits (code 66) at the first report; add abort_on_error=1 to get a SIGABRT a debugger can stop on
exitcode66Exit status when anything was reported
second_deadlock_stack01 prints both stacks for each edge of a lock-order cycle
suppressionsnoneA file of report patterns to ignore

A suppressions file

  • One rule per line: type:pattern. The pattern matches a function, a file or a library anywhere in the report's stacks.
  • Types include race:, deadlock:, mutex:, thread: and called_from_lib: (ignores interceptors called from that library; it does not hide every report that involves it).
  • For example race:third_party_stats_flush, or called_from_lib:libvendor.so. Run with TSAN_OPTIONS=suppressions=tsan.supp.
  • Treat each line as a bug with a ticket number. A suppression hides the report; the race is still undefined behaviour.
Deep dive

Annotating custom synchronization

  • When code synchronizes in a way TSan can't see, tell it. <sanitizer/tsan_interface.h> has hooks for custom mutexes: __tsan_mutex_create, __tsan_mutex_pre_lock/__tsan_mutex_post_lock, __tsan_mutex_pre_unlock/__tsan_mutex_post_unlock.
  • Annotate the lock, not the data. TSan then derives the happens-before edges itself, and the lock-order detector sees the lock too.

When the previous stack is missing

  • TSan keeps a bounded history per thread. If the earlier access is too old, the report can't restore its stack and says so.
  • Rerun, or shrink the window: less work between the two accesses.

Races TSan can't see

  • Only executed code is checked. A race on a path your test never runs isn't reported.
  • Logic races between correctly synchronized operations (check, unlock, re-lock, use) are race conditions, not data races (Ch 5). TSan has nothing to report. That's where stress tests and rr chaos mode come in (Ch 24).
→ Ch 5. The C Memory Model → Ch 6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting → Ch 21. Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag → Ch 22. Lock-Order Deadlock

12. Valgrind

What you'll learn: how to drive Memcheck, Helgrind, DRD and Massif on an unmodified binary, how to read their reports, and when Valgrind is a better choice than a sanitizer.

Why it works: Valgrind translates and instruments your machine code as it runs, the model in Ch 4, Instrumentation Models. No rebuild, at the price of speed.

12.1 The tools

ToolRunFinds
Memcheck (default)valgrind ./progInvalid reads and writes on the heap, use of uninitialized values, bad frees, leaks
Helgrindvalgrind --tool=helgrind ./progData races, pthread API misuse, lock-order inversions
DRDvalgrind --tool=drd ./progData races and pthread misuse, usually with less memory than Helgrind. No lock-order checking
Massifvalgrind --tool=massif ./progHeap usage over time, and which call sites hold it
  • Run them on the plain build: -g, ideally at -O0 or -O1. Never on a sanitized binary (Ch 4).
  • Useful flags: --track-origins=yes (where an uninitialized value came from), --leak-check=full, --error-exitcode=1 (for CI), --num-callers=30, --suppressions=file and --gen-suppressions=all.
  • Valgrind runs one thread at a time. A parallel daemon slows down by far more than the single-thread overhead.

12.2 Watch it: Memcheck on the plain build

The same use-after-free as Ch 10, on the binary built without any sanitizer.

  1. Lines 2–4: the bad read, in rx_process at line 49. The ==40== prefix is the PID.
  2. Line 5: inside a 40-byte block that was free'd.
  3. Lines 6–8: the free, in rx_policy. Lines 9–12: the allocation, in rx_enqueue. The same three stacks ASan gave.

  5. Lines 16–20: no leaks, one error.

12.3 Watch it: DRD on the race

==221== Thread 3:

⋯
==221== ERROR SUMMARY: 6000000 errors from 2 contexts (suppressed: 14 from 6)
  1. Lines 1–3: a load by thread 3 at line 26 conflicts with another thread. Valgrind numbers threads from 1, the main thread.
  2. Line 7: the memory is in the binary's BSS: stats.
  3. Lines 8–11: the other side is a segment of thread 2: a stretch of its code between two synchronization points. Thread 2 has finished, so its stack is gone.
  4. Line 14: no updates lost. Valgrind serialized the threads, so the race couldn't bite, and DRD still found it. It reasons about ordering, not timing.
  5. Line 16: DRD counts every occurrence: millions of errors, from just 2 contexts.

Helgrind reports the same race in a different shape, with Locks held: none for each access. It also catches the lock-order inversion from Ch 11 (Ch 21, Ch 22).

12.4 Watch it: Massif on a leak

07-leak opens and closes 2,000 sessions, but the close path never drops the table's reference.

Number of snapshots: 97
  1. Lines 3–23: heap size against time. It only goes up. A leak is a ramp; a healthy daemon's churn is a plateau.
  2. Line 24: the x axis counts instructions executed (Mi), Massif's default time unit.
  3. @ columns are detailed snapshots, which carry the allocation tree. # is the peak. The rest of ms_print's output, trimmed here, shows for each detailed snapshot which call sites hold the bytes.
07-leak.c — full source
/* 07-leak.c — peers connect and disconnect. session_close() unlinks the
 * session from session_table but never drops the table's reference, so every
 * closed session leaks. The rx buffers are temporary and freed correctly.
 *
 *   make 07-leak.asan && ./07-leak.asan                  (LeakSanitizer)
 *   valgrind --leak-check=full ./07-leak.plain
 *   valgrind --tool=massif ./07-leak.plain; ms_print massif.out.*
 *   heaptrack ./07-leak.plain
 */
#include "pktd.h"

#define CHURN 2000                  /* connect/disconnect cycles */

static void session_open(uint32_t id, const struct peer *p)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS] = session_new(id, p);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
}

static void session_rx(uint32_t id)
{
    struct pktbuf *b = malloc(sizeof *b);   /* temporary: freed below */
    b->len = 64;
    b->port = 179;
    b->ts = now_ns();
    snprintf(b->data, sizeof b->data, "keepalive %u", id);
    stats.pkts++;
    free(b);
}

static void session_close(uint32_t id)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS] = NULL;    /* BUG: no session_put() */
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
}

int main(void)
{
    static const struct peer p = { 0x0a000001, 179 };

    for (uint32_t id = 0; id < CHURN; id++) {
        session_open(id, &p);
        for (int k = 0; k < 4; k++)
            session_rx(id);
        session_close(id);
    }
    printf("pktd: %u sessions opened and closed, %lu pkts\n", CHURN,
           (unsigned long)stats.pkts);
    return 0;
}

12.5 When Valgrind beats sanitizers

SituationBetter choiceWhy
You can't rebuild: a vendor binary, a closed-source .soValgrindIt instruments whatever machine code runs
Uninitialized reads, in code that links uninstrumented librariesMemcheckMSan needs every library rebuilt; Memcheck tracks definedness through all of them
Heap usage over time, by call siteMassifSanitizers find bugs, they don't profile
Stack or global buffer overflowsASanMemcheck has no redzones there
Races under realistic parallelism and loadTSanValgrind serializes threads and runs far slower
A long soak testASan / TSanRoughly 2–15× slowdown, against Valgrind's 20× and up
Valgrind has a built-in GDB server. valgrind --vgdb-error=0 ./prog waits before the program starts. Then gdb ./prog and target remote | vgdb let you set breakpoints and inspect state at each error.
Deep dive

glibc's own checkers

Sometimes all you can change is the environment. glibc has debugging knobs that need no rebuild and no Valgrind.

  • MALLOC_CHECK_=3 switches malloc to a slower, checking variant. On an inconsistency, 3 means print a message and abort. Since glibc 2.34 it only works with LD_PRELOAD=libc_malloc_debug.so.0.
  • MALLOC_PERTURB_=N fills new memory with N ^ 0xff and freed memory with N, so stale or uninitialized data reads as an obvious pattern. As Ch 3 showed, it does nothing on the tcache path, so pair it with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0.
  • mtrace logs every allocation and free. Call mtrace() early in main, run with MALLOC_TRACE=/tmp/mt (plus the same LD_PRELOAD since 2.34), then mtrace ./prog /tmp/mt lists what was never freed.

Electric Fence

  • An old, still-useful idea: LD_PRELOAD=libefence.so.0.0 ./prog places each allocation against an inaccessible page. An overrun faults at the exact instruction, under any debugger, with no rebuild.
  • EF_PROTECT_BELOW=1 guards the start instead, for underruns. EF_PROTECT_FREE=1 never reuses freed memory, so a use-after-free faults too.
  • Blocks are aligned, so a small overrun can land in alignment padding and go unnoticed. EF_ALIGNMENT=0 closes that gap, at the price of misaligned blocks.
  • It costs at least a page per allocation. A daemon with millions of small objects runs out of memory or mappings.
→ Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 20. Leak & Memory Growth → Ch 21. Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag

13. rr

What you'll learn: how to record a run once and replay it exactly, how to run backwards from a crash with a watchpoint to the write that caused it, and how chaos mode makes rare interleavings show up.

Why it works: rr records every nondeterministic input and counts progress with hardware performance counters, the model in Ch 4, Instrumentation Models. Replay is the same run, every time.

13.1 Record, replay

CommandDoes
rr record ./prog argsRun the program and save a trace in ~/.local/share/rr/ (or $_RR_TRACE_DIR). The newest is latest-trace
rr replayReplay the latest trace under GDB, stopped at the very start
rr replay -aReplay with no debugger ("autopilot"), printing the program's output
rr replay -o <opt>Pass an option through to GDB
rr ps, rr replay -p <pid>List the processes in a trace; debug a child instead of the first process
rr packMake a trace self-contained, to move it to another machine

In the replay

  • All normal GDB commands work, plus reverse-continue (rc), reverse-step, reverse-next, reverse-finish and reverse-stepi.
  • Breakpoints and watchpoints work in both directions.
  • when prints the current event number. checkpoint and restart N jump between saved points.
  • Addresses, thread numbers and the crash are identical on every replay. A pointer you wrote down last time is still valid.
rr needs perf counters. It needs perf_event_open, a CPU or VM that exposes the counters, and permission. Under a strict perf_event_paranoid that means a capability. On the capture host (paranoid level 3), CAP_PERFMON wasn't enough and the lab container needed --cap-add SYS_ADMIN; the toolchain capture records rr-smoke(sys_admin). Use the weakest option that works on your host.

13.2 Watch it: backwards from the crash

Record the boss lab once. In the replay, run to the crash, take session 7's address from table_dump's frame, watch its refcnt, and reverse-continue.

printf "HIT %d->%d handle_timeout=%d\n", $prev, $s->refcnt, $_caller_is("handle_timeout")
set $prev = $s->refcnt
end

  2. Lines 4–7: in the replay, continue reaches the same SIGSEGV in table_dump. Replay is exact.
  3. Lines 9–10: a hardware watchpoint on $s->refcnt, then the first reverse-continue. The stray (rr) and >>> are GDB's prompts, echoed because the commands come from stdin.
  4. Lines 12–14: the first reverse-continue stops on the same SIGSEGV again. Expect that, and reverse once more.
  5. Lines 16–22: the last write before the crash. Going backwards, Old value is the value after the write and New value the one before it. rr stops on the writing statement itself, line 78.
  6. Line 23: two intermediate hits are elided here (Ch 23 walks all of them).
  7. Lines 24–32: the fifth reverse-continue reaches the 2 → 1 write from handle_timeout, the same write the forward watchpoint caught in Ch 7.

One recording, walked from the crash back to an earlier write. The forward watchpoint in Ch 7 needed the run to crash the same way again. With rr, the bug never has to happen twice.

gdb/10-rr.gdb — full source
# 10-rr.gdb — replay the recorded crash, then walk refcnt's writers backwards.
#   rr record ./10-refcount-uaf.plain
#   rr replay -o -q < gdb/10-rr.gdb
# Feed it on stdin: rr 5.7 runs "rr replay -x" files before it connects GDB
# to the replay, so a -x script finds no program to continue.
# The first reverse-continue stops on the SIGSEGV again (the fault is the
# last event); each one after that stops at the previous write to s->refcnt.
# Going backwards, "Old value" is the value after the write, "New value" the
# value before it; the HIT line prints them in that same order.
set pagination off
set width 0

frame function table_dump
set $s = s
watch -l $s->refcnt
set $prev = $s->refcnt
commands
printf "HIT %d->%d handle_timeout=%d\n", $prev, $s->refcnt, $_caller_is("handle_timeout")
set $prev = $s->refcnt
end
reverse-continue
bt 2
reverse-continue
bt 2
reverse-continue
bt 2
reverse-continue
bt 2
reverse-continue
bt 2

13.3 Chaos mode: making a rare interleaving happen

rr runs one thread at a time and switches between them on long timeslices. That makes many races rarer under recording. rr record --chaos randomizes thread priorities and timeslice lengths, so unusual schedules come up more often.

The capture records the race lab with --chaos until one run loses updates, then replays that trace.

try 1: actual=4000000 lost=0
try 2: actual=4000000 lost=0
try 3: actual=4000000 lost=0
⋯
try 29: actual=3000000 lost=1000000
lossy on try 29 of 50
--- lossy recording 29:
expected=4000000 actual=3000000 lost=1000000
--- rr replay -a /tmp/rr/t29:
⋯
expected=4000000 actual=3000000 lost=1000000
replay matches lossy recording: actual=3000000
  1. Lines 1–3: chaos recordings that came out exact. Tries 4–28 did too, and are trimmed.
  2. Lines 5–8: try 29 lost updates.
  3. Lines 9–12: rr replay -a on that trace prints the same loss. The rare run is now a file you can replay as often as you like. Four rr warnings about the dynamic loader's inode are trimmed; the replay still matched.
Chaos raises the odds; replay makes it exact. Chaos mode doesn't guarantee a hit: here it took 29 tries. Once you have one bad recording, you have the bug for good. For races this tight, a stress run on real cores is often faster at finding it. Ch 24 takes this run apart.
Deep dive

What rr can't record

  • Memory shared with a process outside the recording, and devices mapped into memory, such as a GPU or a kernel-bypass NIC. rr can't see writes it didn't cause.
  • Work that really runs in parallel is serialized onto one core. A daemon that depends on parallel throughput runs slower, and its timeouts may fire.
  • It needs perf counters, so many cloud VMs without a virtual PMU can't run it at all. Check before you depend on it.

Noise in the replay

  • rr loads its own GDB commands. Redefining restart and jump makes GDB ask for confirmation, and rr's Python helpers may print SyntaxWarning lines under newer Python. Both are harmless; the capture above trims them.
  • Feed a command file on stdin, as the lab does. The script's header explains why -x didn't work with this rr version.

rr and watchpoints

  • The same four-register budget applies, because replay runs on the real CPU.
  • A reverse watchpoint walk is the fastest way to answer "who last wrote this?" for any corruption you can see at the crash.
→ Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 7. GDB on a Live Process → Ch 23. Boss Level → Ch 24. From a One-Shot Core to a Reproducer

14. NULL Dereference

What you'll learn: how to tell a plain NULL, a NULL struct field and a NULL function pointer apart from the core alone, how to map a fault address like 0x18 to the field that was read, and how to recover the caller when pc is 0.

Builds on: why nothing is mapped near address 0 (Ch 1, Process Memory Layout), where the return address sits after a call (Ch 2, x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames), and the first commands on a core (Ch 9, GDB on a Core).

14.1 Three ways to hit NULL

01-null plants three NULL bugs in pktd. Pick one with the first argument.

  • plain: session_lookup() misses and returns NULL. The caller passes it on unchecked, and session_id() reads s->id.
  • field: the same miss, but the code reads s->peer->addr. It faults loading s->peer, at offset 0x18.
  • fnptr: the control thread dispatches an opcode whose handler slot is NULL. The CPU jumps to address 0.
static struct session *session_lookup(uint32_t id)
{
    return session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS];   /* NULL if never added */
}

static uint32_t session_id(const struct session *s)
{
    return s->id;                   /* plain: faults at address 0x8 */
}

static void rx_plain(uint32_t id)
{
    struct session *s = session_lookup(id);
    printf("rx for session %u\n", session_id(s));   /* BUG: s unchecked */
}

static void rx_field(uint32_t id)
{
    struct session *s = session_lookup(id);
    uint32_t addr = s->peer->addr;  /* BUG: loads s->peer from 0x0 + 0x18 */
    printf("peer %#x\n", addr);
}

All three crash on the line with the bug. That makes NULL the first rung: the cause and the crash are in the same place.

01-null.c — full source
/* 01-null.c — three ways pktd dereferences NULL. Pick one with argv[1]:
 *   plain  session_lookup() misses and the caller uses the NULL it got back
 *   field  same miss, but the code reads s->peer->addr: the fault is at 0x18
 *   fnptr  the control thread dispatches an opcode with no handler: pc = 0
 *
 *   make core-01-null ARGS=field
 *   gdb ./01-null.plain cores/01-null.plain-field.core
 */
#include "pktd.h"

typedef int (*ctrl_fn)(struct session *s);

static int ctrl_show(struct session *s) { return printf("%s\n", s->name); }

/* Opcode table. CTRL_CLEAR (1) was never wired up: its slot is NULL. */
static ctrl_fn ctrl_ops[] = { ctrl_show, NULL };

/* Non-static so GDB's "info symbol" names it from the return address. */
int ctrl_dispatch(unsigned op, struct session *s)
{
    return ctrl_ops[op](s);         /* BUG: no NULL check on the slot */
}

static struct session *session_lookup(uint32_t id)
{
    return session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS];   /* NULL if never added */
}

static uint32_t session_id(const struct session *s)
{
    return s->id;                   /* plain: faults at address 0x8 */
}

static void rx_plain(uint32_t id)
{
    struct session *s = session_lookup(id);
    printf("rx for session %u\n", session_id(s));   /* BUG: s unchecked */
}

static void rx_field(uint32_t id)
{
    struct session *s = session_lookup(id);
    uint32_t addr = s->peer->addr;  /* BUG: loads s->peer from 0x0 + 0x18 */
    printf("peer %#x\n", addr);
}

int main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    static const struct peer p = { 0x0a000001, 179 };
    session_table[1] = session_new(1, &p);
    const char *mode = argc > 1 ? argv[1] : "plain";

    if (strcmp(mode, "plain") == 0)
        rx_plain(7);
    else if (strcmp(mode, "field") == 0)
        rx_field(7);
    else if (strcmp(mode, "fnptr") == 0)
        ctrl_dispatch(1, session_table[1]);
    else
        fprintf(stderr, "usage: %s plain|field|fnptr\n", argv[0]);
    return 1;
}

14.2 Both paths

The same bug, the plain variant, both ways.

You can rerun it, so rebuild with sanitizers: make 01-null.asan, then ASAN_OPTIONS=detect_leaks=0 ./01-null.asan plain. The labs' asan variant includes UBSan.

  2. Lines 3–5: the access faults. ASan's signal handler reports a READ at 0x8, and adds that it points to the zero page.

No rerun: one core, from the plain build. Nothing in it says "NULL". You read it off the frames and the fault address.

Core was generated by `/lab/01-null.plain plain'.
Program terminated with signal SIGSEGV, Segmentation fault.
#0  0x0000555555555390 in session_id (s=0x0) at 01-null.c:31
31	    return s->id;                   /* plain: faults at address 0x8 */
#0  0x0000555555555390 in session_id (s=0x0) at 01-null.c:31
#1  0x00005555555553be in rx_plain (id=7) at 01-null.c:37
#2  0x000055555555548a in main (argc=2, argv=0x7fffffffecc8) at 01-null.c:54

  1. Line 2: SIGSEGV, a bad access. No message, no file name for the bug.
  2. Lines 3–4: frame #0 is session_id (s=0x0), on the line that reads s->id. id is at offset 0x8, the same address ASan reported: a NULL base plus a field offset.
  3. Lines 5–7: bt. rx_plain called it, from main.
  4. Lines 8–10: in frame 1, s is NULL too. rx_plain took it from session_lookup and passed it on without a check. That's where the fix goes.
  5. This build has -g and -O0, so GDB can show s. In an optimized or stripped build it may print <optimized out> or nothing; then 14.3's recipe, from the fault address and the instruction, still works.
Symptom → class: SIGSEGV at a small address (< 0x1000), or rip = 0 → NULL dereference

14.3 From a fault address to a field

A small fault address is a NULL base plus a displacement. The displacement is a field offset. Three readings give you the field from a core, without trusting any variable GDB prints.

The recipe

  • The fault address: p $_siginfo._sifields._sigfault.si_addr. Here, 0x18.
  • The instruction: x/i $pc. Its memory operand is disp(%base). Check the base register with info registers: 0 means NULL. The displacement should equal the fault address.
  • The type: ptype /ox struct <type>. Find the field at that offset.

Watch it: the field variant's core

Program terminated with signal SIGSEGV, Segmentation fault.
#0  0x00005555555553f8 in rx_field (id=7) at 01-null.c:43
43	    uint32_t addr = s->peer->addr;  /* BUG: loads s->peer from 0x0 + 0x18 */
⋯
=> 0x5555555553f8 <rx_field+33>:	mov    0x18(%rax),%rax
$1 = (void *) 0x18
/* offset      |    size */  type = struct session {
/* 0x0000      |  0x0004 */    uint32_t magic;
/* 0x0004      |  0x0004 */    int refcnt;
/* 0x0008      |  0x0004 */    uint32_t id;
/* 0x000c      |  0x0004 */    uint32_t flags;
/* 0x0010      |  0x0008 */    uint64_t created_ns;
/* 0x0018      |  0x0008 */    const struct peer *peer;
/* 0x0020      |  0x0018 */    char name[24];
⋯
                               /* total size (bytes):   56 */
                             }
  1. Lines 2–3: rx_field, line 43. That line dereferences twice, s->peer then ->addr. Which one faulted?
  2. Line 5: x/i $pc. The instruction loads 8 bytes from rax + 0x18.
  3. Line 6: the fault address, from $_siginfo, is 0x18. So rax was 0: the base pointer was NULL.
  4. Lines 7–17: ptype /ox struct session. /o adds each field's offset and size; x prints them in hex.
  5. Line 13: offset 0x0018 is peer. So s was NULL and the load of s->peer faulted. Had s been fine and peer NULL, the fault address would be 0x0: addr is at offset 0 of struct peer.

Offsets in struct session

Fault addressFieldIn 01-null
0x0magicAlso what a NULL struct peer * gives, reading addr
0x8idThe plain variant, in session_id
0x18peerThe field variant
0x20–0x37name[24]A printf("%s", s->name) on a NULL session faults in here, inside libc
More than one type fits? In a real daemon several structs can have some field at 0x18. The instruction settles it: its source line, or the registers loaded just before it, tell you which type the code was walking.

14.4 A NULL function pointer: pc = 0

The control thread looks up a handler by opcode. Slot 1 was never filled in.

/* Opcode table. CTRL_CLEAR (1) was never wired up: its slot is NULL. */
static ctrl_fn ctrl_ops[] = { ctrl_show, NULL };

/* Non-static so GDB's "info symbol" names it from the return address. */
int ctrl_dispatch(unsigned op, struct session *s)
{
    return ctrl_ops[op](s);         /* BUG: no NULL check on the slot */
}

The call pushes the return address and jumps to 0. Fetching the first instruction faults. Nothing ran at address 0, so nothing else was pushed.

Program terminated with signal SIGSEGV, Segmentation fault.
⋯
#0  0x0000000000000000 in ?? ()
#1  0x0000555555555356 in ctrl_dispatch (op=1, s=0x5555555592a0) at 01-null.c:21
#2  0x00005555555554e0 in main (argc=2, argv=0x7fffffffecc8) at 01-null.c:58
$1 = (void (*)()) 0x0
0x7fffffffeb58:	0x0000555555555356
ctrl_dispatch + 50 in section .text of /lab/01-null.plain
  1. Line 3: frame #0 is 0x0 in ?? (). There's no function at 0 to name.
  2. Line 6: p $pc confirms it: the program counter is 0.
  3. Line 7: x/gx $rsp. The word on top of the stack is the return address the call pushed.
  4. Line 8: info symbol on that word: ctrl_dispatch + 50, the instruction just after the call. That's the caller.
  5. Line 4: GDB's backtrace found the same frame. With no function at pc to describe the frame, its fallback assumes nothing was pushed yet and reads the return address from [$rsp]. When it can't (a stripped binary, a confused unwind), do it by hand as on lines 7–8.
  6. Line 4 also shows op=1: the slot that holds NULL.

p s

#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/01-null-field.gdb ./01-null.plain cores/01-null.plain-field.core

gdb/01-null-fnptr.gdb — full source
# 01-null-fnptr.gdb — pc is 0: the caller is the return address on top of the stack.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/01-null-fnptr.gdb ./01-null.plain cores/01-null.plain-fnptr.core
set pagination off
bt
p $pc
x/gx $rsp
info symbol *(long*)$rsp
Deep dive

NULL table or NULL entry?

  • A NULL entry in a valid table (this lab): the load of the function pointer works, the call jumps to 0, and pc is 0.
  • A NULL table, such as an ops-struct pointer that was never set: the load faults, at the slot's offset. pc is still inside the caller, and the fault address is small. That's a field fault, solved as in 14.3.

When the small-address rule breaks

  • Only addresses below vm.mmap_min_addr are guaranteed unmapped (Ch 1).
  • A NULL base plus a large offset, such as a big array index, can land anywhere. If it lands in a mapping, there's no fault at all, just wrong data.

The compiler and NULL checks

  • GCC's -fdelete-null-pointer-checks, on by default on most targets, lets the optimizer assume a pointer that was dereferenced isn't NULL. A check that comes after the dereference may be deleted.
  • So "I check for NULL right there" can be untrue in the optimized binary. Read the disassembly, not the source. Some projects, the Linux kernel among them, build with -fno-delete-null-pointer-checks.

Catching it before the fault

  • UBSan's null check (-fsanitize=null, part of -fsanitize=undefined, so in the labs' asan variant) reports a member access through NULL with file and line, before the access.
  • For a daemon, the real fix is a contract: can session_lookup return NULL? If yes, every caller checks. If no, it asserts.
→ Ch 1. Process Memory Layout → Ch 2. x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 15. Uninitialized Pointer / Stack Garbage → Decision Matrix

15. Uninitialized Pointer / Stack Garbage

What you'll learn: why an uninitialized variable can look fine in one build and break in the next, how Memcheck and MSan trace a bad value back to the variable that was never written, and how pattern init turns garbage into a value you can spot in a core.

Builds on: definedness tracking in Memcheck and MSan (Ch 4, Instrumentation Models), and running them (Ch 12, Valgrind, Ch 10, The ASan Family).

15.1 The bug: a flag that is only sometimes written

session_apply_opts builds a session's flags from config words. flags is only written when a word parses. Start pktd with no options and it is never written at all.

/* Parse one config word. Writes *out only on success. Kept out of line, as
 * it would be in its own file, so the optimizer can't see through it. */
__attribute__((noinline)) static int parse_opt(const char *word, uint32_t *out)
{
    if (strcmp(word, "passive") == 0) { *out = SESS_F_PASSIVE; return 0; }
    if (strcmp(word, "md5") == 0)     { *out = SESS_F_MD5;     return 0; }
    return -1;
}

static void session_apply_opts(struct session *s, int argc, char **argv)
{
    uint32_t flags;                 /* BUG: never initialised */

    for (int i = 1; i < argc; i++)
        parse_opt(argv[i], &flags); /* return value ignored, too */
    s->flags = flags;
}
  • The value is whatever the stack slot held before: left over from an earlier call. It changes with the compiler, the optimization level and the call path.
  • The compiler doesn't warn. &flags goes to an out-of-line function, so as far as it knows, parse_opt may write it.
  • The lab's garbage is a flags word, so the program just takes a wrong branch. An uninitialized pointer is the same bug, and it crashes wherever the garbage points.
02-uninit.c — full source
/* 02-uninit.c — a session's flags come from a stack variable that is only
 * written when a config word parses. With no options it is never written,
 * and the value is whatever the stack held before: it changes with the
 * build, the compiler and the caller.
 *
 *   make 02-uninit.plain   && valgrind --track-origins=yes ./02-uninit.plain
 *   make 02-uninit.msan    && ./02-uninit.msan
 *   make 02-uninit.pattern && ./02-uninit.pattern   (GCC fills it with 0xFE)
 */
#include "pktd.h"

#define SESS_F_PASSIVE 0x1u
#define SESS_F_MD5     0x2u

/* Parse one config word. Writes *out only on success. Kept out of line, as
 * it would be in its own file, so the optimizer can't see through it. */
__attribute__((noinline)) static int parse_opt(const char *word, uint32_t *out)
{
    if (strcmp(word, "passive") == 0) { *out = SESS_F_PASSIVE; return 0; }
    if (strcmp(word, "md5") == 0)     { *out = SESS_F_MD5;     return 0; }
    return -1;
}

static void session_apply_opts(struct session *s, int argc, char **argv)
{
    uint32_t flags;                 /* BUG: never initialised */

    for (int i = 1; i < argc; i++)
        parse_opt(argv[i], &flags); /* return value ignored, too */
    s->flags = flags;
}

int main(int argc, char **argv)
{
    static const struct peer p = { 0x0a000002, 179 };
    struct session *s = session_new(2, &p);

    session_apply_opts(s, argc, argv);   /* no options given: flags is junk */
    printf("session %u flags = %#x\n", s->id, s->flags);
    if (s->flags & SESS_F_PASSIVE)
        puts("passive: waiting for the peer to connect");
    else
        puts("active: connecting to the peer");
    session_put(s);
    return 0;
}

Run the unmodified plain build under Memcheck with --track-origins=yes.

session 2 flags = 0
