⋯
==193== ERROR SUMMARY: 6 errors from 6 contexts (suppressed: 0 from 0)
  1. Lines 1–6: the first report. printf's number formatting uses an uninitialized value. It came in from main at line 39, the printf of s->flags.
  2. Lines 7–8: the origin, thanks to --track-origins=yes: a stack allocation in session_apply_opts. Valgrind names the function's opening line (25), where its frame is set up, not the declaration on line 26.
  3. Lines 10–11: the use that decides behaviour: the branch on s->flags at line 40.
  4. Line 15: flags = 0. In this run the stack slot happened to hold zero, so the output looks right. Memcheck reports it anyway: it tracks whether bits were ever written, not what they are.
  5. Line 18: six errors. Five come from inside printf as it formats the number; the sixth is line 40.

There is no crash, so no core. And a core from a normal build can't help: it holds bytes, not definedness. Garbage in flags looks exactly like data. The 0 Memcheck's run printed would pass for a real value.

A fingerprint exists only if the shipped build already used -ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern. This capture is a rerun of such a build, to show what you would then see:

session 2 flags = 0xfefefefe
active: connecting to the peer
  1. Line 1: 0xfefefefe. GCC's -ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern filled flags with 0xFE bytes at its declaration. Nothing wrote it after that.
  2. Line 2: still "active". 0xfefefefe & 0x1 is 0. A pattern doesn't force a crash; it makes the value recognizable.

In a core from such a build, p/x *s would show flags = 0xfefefefe. No code path writes that value. Look for the variable it was copied from, in the function that set the field.

Symptom → class: a value that changes between builds, or reads 0xfefefefe / 0xaaaaaaaa → uninitialized memory

15.3 MSan: the same bug, found by a rebuild

Clang's MemorySanitizer tracks the same definedness as Memcheck, much faster, but needs a rebuild: make 02-uninit.msan. This capture also passes MSAN_OPTIONS=external_symbolizer_path=/usr/bin/addr2line, because the lab image has no llvm-symbolizer (Ch 10).

==205==WARNING: MemorySanitizer: use-of-uninitialized-value
    #0 0x5577b28526d2 in main /lab/02-uninit.c:39
⋯
  Uninitialized value was stored to memory at
    #0 0x5577b28526cb in main /lab/02-uninit.c:30
⋯
  Uninitialized value was created by an allocation of 'flags.i9' in the stack frame
    #0 0x5577b28524cd in main /lab/02-uninit.c:26
⋯
SUMMARY: MemorySanitizer: use-of-uninitialized-value /lab/02-uninit.c:39 in main
  1. Lines 1–2: reported at line 39, the printf call. Passing an uninitialized value as a function argument counts as a use. That's Clang's default since Clang 16 (-fsanitize-memory-param-retval); the labs use Clang 18.
  2. Lines 4–5: -fsanitize-memory-track-origins gives the trail. The value was stored into the session at line 30.
  3. Lines 7–8: it was created as the stack variable flags, line 26. The build is -O1, so session_apply_opts was inlined into main: every frame says main, and the variable is flags.i9, the inliner's renamed copy.
  4. Line 10: one report, then exit. MSan stops at the first error by default.
MemcheckMSan
RebuildNoYes: Clang only, and every linked library instrumented
Reports atA branch, an address, a syscall argument: here, deep inside printfAlso any call argument: here, the printf call in your code
OriginWith --track-origins=yes: the function whose frame held itWith track-origins: the variable, and the store on the way

15.4 Pattern init: GCC 0xFE, Clang 0xAA

-ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern makes the compiler fill every automatic variable at its declaration. Code that writes the variable overwrites the fill; code that forgets leaves it. A switch or goto that jumps past a declaration skips the fill too, and the variable keeps whatever the stack held.

CompilerFill byteA 32-bit int readsA 64-bit pointer reads
GCC0xFE0xfefefefe (captured above)0xfefefefefefefefe
Clang0xAA0xaaaaaaaa0xaaaaaaaaaaaaaaaa
  • Both pointer patterns are non-canonical on x86-64. Dereferencing one is a general-protection fault, so the kernel reports si_addr as 0 (Ch 1). x/i $pc and the base register show the real value.
  • =zero is the production-hardening choice. It makes forgotten variables 0, which is often harmless. It also hides the bug: zero looks like a real value.
  • It covers stack variables only. Heap memory from malloc is not filled; for that, see MALLOC_PERTURB_ and its tcache caveat (Ch 3).

An uninitialized pointer in a normal build's core

Without pattern init, a garbage pointer is usually not random. It's an old value: whatever an earlier call left in the same stack slot. Ask the core what that value used to be.

  • info symbol <value>: if it names function + offset, the slot held a return address, left by an earlier call's frame.
  • info files (or maint info sections): find the core load segment the value falls in. On a core, info proc mappings lists only file-backed mappings (NT_FILE), with no [heap] or [stack] lines. Compare with the threads' $rsp values (thread apply all p $rsp) and with mp_.sbrk_base: a segment holding a thread's stack pointer means a stale stack address; the segment holding the brk heap means a stale heap pointer; no segment at all means plain junk.
  • Either answer points back at the path: which function ran just before, in that thread, and used that slot. That's your candidate for the variable that was never written.
Deep dive

Why the garbage changes

  • A new frame reuses stack bytes the previous call left behind. Which call ran just before, and how big its frame was, decide what flags holds.
  • Changing the optimization level moves variables between slots and registers. That's why "it works in debug" so often means "it reads a lucky leftover in debug".
  • Under Valgrind the leftovers can differ again. In this capture the value was 0; the plain build run directly may print something else.

Why no warning

  • -Wuninitialized and -Wmaybe-uninitialized only report what the compiler can see. Once &flags goes to a function it doesn't look into, it has to assume the variable may be written.
  • The cheap fix is still the right one: initialize at the declaration, and check parse_opt's return value.

Copies are not uses

  • Memcheck doesn't report copying uninitialized bytes, only using them. A struct copied with a hole in it is fine until something branches on the hole. That's why its report lands at the printf, not at line 30.
  • MSan works the same way, except that a call argument counts as a use.
  • --track-origins=yes costs extra time and memory. Run without it first to see whether there's anything to chase.
→ Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 12. Valgrind → Ch 16. Heap Overflow, Off-by-One → Build-Flag Matrix

16. Heap Overflow, Off-by-One

What you'll learn: how one stray NUL byte rewrites the next chunk's size, why glibc only notices at free and then blames the pointer, and how to read the redzone that lets ASan stop at the write instead.

Builds on: chunk headers and the P bit (Ch 3, The glibc Allocator), shadow bytes and redzones (Ch 4, Instrumentation Models), and reading an ASan report (Ch 10, The ASan Family).

16.1 The bug: 73 bytes into 72

A peer's description is exactly 72 characters. The buffer is 72 bytes. strcpy also writes the terminating NUL.

#define DESC_MAX 0x48               /* 72 bytes "including" the NUL. It isn't. */
⋯
    static const char cfg_desc[] =
        "transit-peer AS64500 primary uplink to the provider edge, rack 7 slot 12";
    char *a = malloc(DESC_MAX);     /* description buffer */
    char *b = malloc(DESC_MAX);     /* the next session's scratch buffer */

    _Static_assert(sizeof cfg_desc - 1 == DESC_MAX, "description is 72 chars");
    strcpy(a, cfg_desc);            /* BUG: writes 73 bytes into 72 */
    printf("desc: %s\n", a);

    free(b);                        /* glibc reads b's size field: 0 */
    free(a);

malloc(0x48) gets a 0x50 chunk (Ch 3). a and b come back to back. The last 8 bytes of a's data are lent to it by b's header: they are b's prev_size field. The 73rd byte lands one past that.

Address (from the core)What it isAfter the strcpy
a+0x00 … a+0x3f (…92a0)a's data, inside a's chunk"transit-peer … rack 7"
a+0x40 … a+0x47 (…92e0)b's prev_size, lent to a while a is in use" slot 12": legal, a owns these bytes
a+0x48 (…92e8)Low byte of b's size: 0x51, a 0x50 chunk with P set0x00, the NUL. size is now 0
a+0x50 (…92f0)b's dataUntouched

x86-64 is little-endian, so the low byte of the 8-byte size sits at the lowest address, right where the NUL lands. The other seven bytes were already 0.

03-heap-overflow.c — full source
/* 03-heap-overflow.c — an off-by-one strcpy into a 72-byte heap buffer.
 * a and b are both malloc(0x48), so both live in 0x50-byte chunks, back to
 * back. The 72-char description fits a exactly; its terminating NUL lands at
 * a[0x48], the low byte of b's chunk size field: 0x51 becomes 0x00. Nothing
 * notices until free(b).
 *
 *   make 03-heap-overflow.asan && ./03-heap-overflow.asan
 *   make 03-heap-overflow.plain && ./03-heap-overflow.plain
 *   make core-03-heap-overflow
 */
#include "pktd.h"

#define DESC_MAX 0x48               /* 72 bytes "including" the NUL. It isn't. */

int main(void)
{
    /* The peer's description, 72 chars, straight from the config file. */
    static const char cfg_desc[] =
        "transit-peer AS64500 primary uplink to the provider edge, rack 7 slot 12";
    char *a = malloc(DESC_MAX);     /* description buffer */
    char *b = malloc(DESC_MAX);     /* the next session's scratch buffer */

    _Static_assert(sizeof cfg_desc - 1 == DESC_MAX, "description is 72 chars");
    strcpy(a, cfg_desc);            /* BUG: writes 73 bytes into 72 */
    printf("desc: %s\n", a);

    free(b);                        /* glibc reads b's size field: 0 */
    free(a);
    return 0;
}

16.2 Both paths

Both builds warn first. GCC's -Wstringop-overflow saw a 73-byte strcpy into a 72-byte malloc at compile time. The captures start with that warning; it's trimmed here. It only works when the compiler can see both sizes. Read your warnings.

make 03-heap-overflow.asan && ./03-heap-overflow.asan. ASan stops at the strcpy.

  6. Line 13: the shadow row. 16.3 reads it byte by byte.

The plain build prints one line and aborts:

Program terminated with signal SIGABRT, Aborted.

#8  0x00007ffff7e53e8e in __GI___libc_free (mem=0x5555555592f0) at ./malloc/malloc.c:3398

0x5555555592e0:	0x323120746f6c7320	0x0000000000000000

  1. Line 1: SIGABRT. Something called abort().
  2. Line 4: malloc_printerr carries the message, even if stderr was lost: free(): invalid pointer.
  3. Lines 6–7, 9: it's free(b), line 27. The bad write was three lines earlier; nothing noticed until free read b's header.
  4. Line 12: b's header at b-16. prev_size reads 0x323120746f6c7320: read the bytes right to left and it's " slot 12", a's last 8 characters. size is 0x0; a live 0x50 chunk should say 0x51.
  5. Line 13: b's data, untouched.
  6. Line 14: a's full 72-character string. Its NUL is the byte that zeroed b's size. The overflowing buffer is the chunk just below the damaged header.
Symptom → class: SIGABRT inside free() or malloc(): "invalid pointer", "invalid size", "corrupted size" → heap overflow onto the next chunk's header (or freeing a non-heap pointer)

16.3 Reading the redzone

ASan doesn't put a and b back to back. Each block gets redzones, poisoned in shadow memory (Ch 4). Here is line 13 of the ASan block, one shadow byte per 8 application bytes. The row's label is the application address of its first byte.

App bytesShadowMeans
0x507000000080–08ffa faLeft redzone of a. ASan's own chunk header lives here
0x507000000090–0d700 × 9a's 72 bytes, all addressable: 9 × 8 = 72. The report's region [0x…090, 0x…0d8)
0x5070000000d8–0df[fa]Redzone. The brackets mark the granule of the bad address: a[72], where the NUL would go
0x5070000000e0–0fffa fa fa faMore redzone, before the next block
  • 72 is a multiple of 8, so the block ends on a granule boundary. A 70-byte request would end in a partial granule, shadow 06: only its first 6 bytes addressable.
  • The 73rd byte hits poison instead of a neighbour's header. The report comes at the write, and b is never damaged.

16.4 Why glibc blames the pointer

free(b) is a valid call with a valid pointer. glibc says invalid pointer anyway. The order of its checks explains why.

  • free reads the chunk's size and goes into _int_free (frame #7 in the core). Its first check is, in effect, "p > -size as unsigned numbers, or p misaligned" → free(): invalid pointer. It's meant to catch a chunk whose size would run past the end of the address space.
  • With size 0, -size is 0 too. Every non-NULL p is greater than 0, so the check fires.
  • The next check, size below the minimum → free(): invalid size, would describe the problem better. It never runs.
Read glibc's message as "this chunk's header is wrong". The message names the check that failed, not the bug (Ch 3 has the full table). Dump the header with x/4gx ptr-16, then look one chunk down: an overflow almost always comes from the chunk just below the damaged header.
gdb/03-heap-overflow-core.gdb — full source
# 03-heap-overflow-core.gdb — free() aborted: look at the chunk header it read.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/03-heap-overflow-core.gdb ./03-heap-overflow.plain cores/03-heap-overflow.plain.core
set pagination off
bt
frame function main
echo \n--- b's chunk header: prev_size (a's last 8 chars), then size|flags ---\n
x/4gx b-16
x/s a
Deep dive

Where the abort lands

  • glibc checks a header only when it touches the chunk: this free, a later malloc that splits or merges it, a consolidation pass.
  • In a daemon that can be much later, in another thread. The thread that aborts is often innocent. The chunk below the damaged header points at the guilty code: what type lives there, and who writes it?
  • Other messages from the same class: free(): invalid next size (the overflow hit the next chunk), corrupted size vs. prev_size, malloc(): corrupted top size (the overflow ran off the last chunk into the top).

Why off-by-one is so common

  • The NUL terminator: a buffer sized for strlen instead of strlen + 1, as here.
  • <= instead of < in a loop bound.
  • A single zero byte over a size field is enough to break the heap's bookkeeping. Heap-exploitation write-ups call it the "poison null byte".

Memcheck catches it too

  • Memcheck replaces malloc with its own, which pads each block with a redzone (--redzone-size, 16 bytes by default). An invalid write into it is reported at the strcpy, with the block's allocation stack, on the unmodified plain build.
  • Its blind spots are stack and global arrays: no redzones there (Ch 12).
→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 17. Stack Smashing

17. Stack Smashing (memcpy into a Local Buffer)

What you'll learn: what the canary abort tells you and what it doesn't, how to read a smashed frame word by word, why info symbol on the saved return address finds nothing, and how to unwind past the damage by hand.

Builds on: frame layout, canaries and unwinding by hand (Ch 2, x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames), and stack redzones (Ch 10, The ASan Family).

17.1 The bug: an unbounded copy into a local

The control thread copies a peer's hostname into a 16-byte local. The lab uses strcpy; a memcpy with a length from the wire, or a sprintf, is the same bug.

static int ctrl_set_hostname(struct session *s, const char *hostname)
{
    char host[16];

    strcpy(host, hostname);         /* BUG: no bound on the copy */
    snprintf(s->name, sizeof s->name, "%s", host);
    return (int)strlen(host);
}                                   /* canary checked here: __stack_chk_fail */
  • The hostname is 46 characters, plus the NUL: 47 bytes into 16.
  • The copy writes up the stack, toward the canary, the saved rbp and the return address (Ch 2).
  • Nothing checks during the copy. The canary is checked at the closing brace, line 19, just before ret.
04-stack-smash.c — full source
/* 04-stack-smash.c — the control thread copies a peer's hostname into a
 * 16-byte stack buffer with no length check. The copy runs over the stack
 * canary, the saved rbp and the saved return address. The canary check at
 * the end of the function catches it before the bad return happens.
 *
 *   make 04-stack-smash.plain && ./04-stack-smash.plain
 *   make 04-stack-smash.asan  && ./04-stack-smash.asan
 *   make core-04-stack-smash
 */
#include "pktd.h"

static int ctrl_set_hostname(struct session *s, const char *hostname)
{
    char host[16];

    strcpy(host, hostname);         /* BUG: no bound on the copy */
    snprintf(s->name, sizeof s->name, "%s", host);
    return (int)strlen(host);
}                                   /* canary checked here: __stack_chk_fail */

int main(void)
{
    static const struct peer p = { 0x0a000004, 179 };
    struct session *s = session_new(4, &p);
    /* 46 chars: well past host[16], the canary and the saved registers. */
    const char *from_peer = "edge-router-07.pop-ams.example.net.backup-link";

    int n = ctrl_set_hostname(s, from_peer);
    printf("hostname set (%d chars): %s\n", n, s->name);
    session_put(s);
    return 0;
}

  1. Lines 3–4: __stack_chk_fail reports through __fortify_fail, with the message stack smashing detected.
  2. Line 5: frame #8 is still right: ctrl_set_hostname, at line 19, the closing brace where the canary is checked. Its arguments are intact.
  3. Line 6: frame #9's "return address" is 0x00006b6e696c2d70. Read right to left, that's p-link: the end of the hostname.
  4. Line 7: frame #10 is built on more of the same. Everything past frame #8 is noise.
  5. Lines 10–17: the frame, 16 words from $rsp. 17.3 decodes them.
  6. Line 20: info symbol on the saved return address finds nothing.
  7. Line 21: info symbol $pc works: ctrl_set_hostname + 126, just after its call to __stack_chk_fail.
Symptom → class: "stack smashing detected", or a backtrace of ?? frames at ASCII-looking addresses → stack buffer overflow

17.3 Reading the smashed frame

Where is rbp? GDB took frame #9's address from $rbp+8, and that word, 0x00006b6e696c2d70, sits at …ebc8. So in frame #8, rbp is …ebc0. Everything below follows from that and the layout in Ch 2.

AddressSlotWord nowReads as
…eb90hostname, spilled (rbp−0x30)0x0000555555556028Intact: matches frame #8
…eb98s, spilled (rbp−0x28)0x00005555555592a0Intact: matches frame #8
…eba0host[0..7] (rbp−0x20)0x756f722d65676465"edge-rou"
…eba8host[8..15]0x702e37302d726574"ter-07.p": host ends here
…ebb0Between host and the canary0x652e736d612d706f"op-ams.e"
…ebb8Canary (rbp−0x8)0x6e2e656c706d6178"xample.n": the check fails
…ebc0Saved rbp0x756b6361622e7465"et.backu"
…ebc8Saved rip (rbp+0x8)0x00006b6e696c2d70"p-link", the NUL, one old zero byte
…ebd0 and upThe caller's frame—Untouched
  • From …eba0 to the NUL at …ebce is 47 bytes: the same 47 ASan reported.
  • The words below host are intact. An overflow only goes up.
"No symbol matches" is the answer, not a tool failure. info symbol looks the address up in every loaded object. 0x6b6e696c2d70 isn't in any of them, because it's text, not a code address: the saved rip is garbage. Had the canary not been there, ret would have jumped to it and died with a SIGSEGV at an ASCII-looking pc.

17.4 Unwinding past the damage

Frame #8 is right, and the older frames above the damage survive. Find where they start and resume the chain from there. Ch 2's Deep dive has the method.

In the dump (lines 14–17 of the core block)

  • Line 15, …ebe0: 0x5555555592a0 and 0x555555556028. The same two pointers as frame #8's s and hostname. They are the caller's locals, s and from_peer: the caller is main.
  • Line 16, …ebf0: 0x7fffffffec90, a stack address a little higher up, then 0x7ffff7dd01ca, which falls between the libc addresses of abort and raise in the full backtrace. A stack address followed by a code address is the shape of a saved rbp and return address: main's own frame record.

Continue by hand

  • x/2gx 0x7fffffffebf0, then x/2gx at each saved rbp in turn, walks the rest of the chain.
  • info symbol each return address. A real one follows a call: check with x/2i <addr>-5, for the common 5-byte direct call.
  • Only the return address into main is lost, and the locals above already told you who the caller was.
What the core proves: ctrl_set_hostname, called from main, overran host by 31 bytes, with the bytes of the hostname it was given. That's the whole bug, without a rerun.
gdb/04-stack-smash-core.gdb — full source
# 04-stack-smash-core.gdb — the canary check fired: is the return address still sane?
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/04-stack-smash-core.gdb ./04-stack-smash.plain cores/04-stack-smash.plain.core
set pagination off
bt
frame function ctrl_set_hostname
echo \n--- the smashed frame: host[16], canary, saved rbp, saved rip ---\n
x/16gx $rsp
echo \n--- candidate return addresses ---\n
info symbol *(long*)($rbp+8)
info symbol $pc
Deep dive

Where the canary came from

  • The labs' plain flags don't ask for one. Ubuntu's GCC enables -fstack-protector-strong by default, and host[16] is a local array, so the function got a canary.
  • -fstack-protector guards functions that call alloca or have character buffers of 8 bytes or more; -strong adds any local array and any local whose address is taken; -all guards every function.
  • A canary only catches writes that pass through it. An indexed write that skips over it, such as host[40] = c, reaches the return address without touching the canary.

_FORTIFY_SOURCE

This extends the short note in Ch 2's Deep dive.

  • With -D_FORTIFY_SOURCE and optimization, glibc's headers turn calls like strcpy(host, hostname) into checked versions such as __strcpy_chk(host, hostname, 16). The compiler supplies the 16: it knows host's size.
  • The check runs before the copy. It aborts with *** buffer overflow detected ***: terminated, and no byte past host is written. In a core, the stack shows __chk_fail calling the same __fortify_fail you saw above.
  • Levels 1 and 2 use sizes known at compile time (__builtin_object_size). Level 3 also uses sizes computed at run time (__builtin_dynamic_object_size; GCC 12 and glibc 2.34 or later).
  • It does nothing at -O0. glibc's headers only enable it when optimizing, and warn otherwise. The labs' plain build is -O0, which is why this lab gets the canary abort at the return instead. Several distros define it by default when optimizing, so your release build may fail differently from your debug build.

Which defence fires when

DefenceFires atLeaves you
ASanThe copy (line 16)A report with the exact variable and frame layout
_FORTIFY_SOURCEThe copy, before any byte is writtenAn abort with intact frames and a full backtrace
CanaryThe return (line 19)Frame #8 right, everything above it to recover by hand
NoneThe retA SIGSEGV at a garbage pc, and no frame you can trust
→ Ch 2. x86-64 Calling Convention & Stack Frames → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 18. Double Free → Build-Flag Matrix

18. Double Free

What you'll learn: how glibc's tcache key spots a double free, what the abort tells you and what it can't, and how ASan's three stacks hand you the first free that the core never saw.

Builds on: the tcache, its key and glibc's abort messages (Ch 3, The glibc Allocator), and ASan's alloc/free/use stacks (Ch 10, The ASan Family).

18.1 The bug: an error path that frees but doesn't unlink

session_add stores the new session in session_table, then finds the peer invalid and frees it. The table still points at it. At shutdown, the table walk frees it again.

static int session_add(uint32_t id, const struct peer *p)
{
    struct session *s = session_new(id, p);

    session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS] = s;
    if (p->port == 0) {             /* invalid peer: undo */
        free(s);                    /* BUG: the table still points at s */
        return -1;
    }
    return 0;
}

static void pktd_shutdown(void)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_SESSIONS; i++) {
        free(session_table[i]);     /* frees id 5 a second time */
        session_table[i] = NULL;
    }
}
  • The first free is the bug (line 16). The second (line 25) is where it crashes.
  • Here nothing touches the freed session in between. In a daemon, a lot can happen in between.
05-double-free.c — full source
/* 05-double-free.c — an error path frees a session but leaves it in
 * session_table. Shutdown later walks the table and frees it again.
 *
 *   make 05-double-free.plain && ./05-double-free.plain
 *   make 05-double-free.asan  && ./05-double-free.asan
 *   make core-05-double-free
 */
#include "pktd.h"

static int session_add(uint32_t id, const struct peer *p)
{
    struct session *s = session_new(id, p);

    session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS] = s;
    if (p->port == 0) {             /* invalid peer: undo */
        free(s);                    /* BUG: the table still points at s */
        return -1;
    }
    return 0;
}

static void pktd_shutdown(void)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_SESSIONS; i++) {
        free(session_table[i]);     /* frees id 5 a second time */
        session_table[i] = NULL;
    }
}

int main(void)
{
    static const struct peer good = { 0x0a000003, 179 };
    static const struct peer bad  = { 0x0a000005, 0 };

    session_add(3, &good);
    if (session_add(5, &bad) < 0)
        fprintf(stderr, "session 5: invalid peer port, dropped\n");
    pktd_shutdown();
    puts("pktd: clean shutdown");
    return 0;
}

#9  0x0000555555555396 in pktd_shutdown () at 05-double-free.c:25
25	        free(session_table[i]);     /* frees id 5 a second time */
$2 = 5
$3 = (struct session *) 0x5555555592e0
⋯
--- the chunk: tcache next (safe-linked), then the key ---
0x5555555592e0:	0x0000000555555559	0x24456a9db6c4646a
$4 = 0x555555559
$5 = {magic = 0x55555559, refcnt = 0x5, id = 0xb6c4646a, flags = 0x24456a9d, created_ns = 0x1d31fd5573bab7, peer = 0x555555556058, name = {0x73, 0x65, 0x73, 0x73, 0x2d, 0x35, 0x0 <repeats 18 times>}}
  1. Line 3: malloc_printerr holds the message: free(): double free detected in tcache 2. The trailing 2 is part of glibc's fixed text, not a bin number.
  2. Lines 4–5: _int_free in the main arena raised it, freeing mem=0x5555555592e0.
  3. Lines 6–7: the second free, in pktd_shutdown. The core has no record of the first: that call returned long ago.
  4. Line 12: p mem in frame 8. libc's debug info names free's argument.
  5. Lines 15–18: frame 9, the lab code. i is 5, and session_table[5] still equals mem: the abort came before line 26 could clear the slot. The table still points at a chunk the allocator says is free.
  6. Lines 21–23: the chunk itself, read in 18.3.
Symptom → class: SIGABRT with "free(): double free detected in tcache 2" → double free

18.3 The tcache key

A 56-byte session lives in a 0x40 chunk, so free puts it in the thread's tcache (Ch 3). That writes two words into the freed object.

Offset in the objectSession fieldAfter free
+0x00magic, refcntnext, safe-linked
+0x08id, flagskey: the process's random tcache_key
+0x10 and upcreated_ns, peer, nameUnchanged

Reading it in the core (lines 21–23 of the core block)

  • Line 21, first word: 0x0000000555555559, the safe-linked next. Line 22 shows mem >> 12 is 0x555555559: the same value. Decoding gives (pos >> 12) ^ stored = 0, so next is NULL (Ch 3). Session 5 went into an empty bin: it was the first 0x40 chunk freed, back in session_add.
  • Line 21, second word: the key, tcache_key. Random per process; yours will differ.
  • Line 23: the same 16 bytes through the session type. magic and refcnt are the two halves of next, id and flags the two halves of the key. name is untouched: the bytes 0x73 0x65 0x73 0x73 0x2d 0x35 spell sess-5. The freed chunk still tells you which session it was.

What the second free did

  • It read the word at +0x08. It equalled tcache_key, so the chunk was probably already in a tcache.
  • "Probably": live data could hold that value by chance. So glibc walked this size's bin. Finding the chunk there is proof: free(): double free detected in tcache 2.
  • The bin held session 3, freed a moment earlier by the same loop, then session 5, whose next ends the list. The walk found session 5 second.
  • If the key matches by chance and the walk doesn't find the chunk, free goes ahead normally.
A freed session's magic and id read as junk. In a core, x/2gx on a session that should be live tells you at once whether it's on a free list: a safe-linked pointer (equal to its own address >> 12 when it is the last in its bin), then a random-looking key.

18.4 From the core to the first free

The core shows the second free. The fix belongs at the first. The script's commands bridge the gap with what's still in memory.

echo \n--- frame 8: the pointer free() was given ---\n
frame 8
p mem
echo \n--- frame 9: the lab code, mid-loop ---\n
frame 9
p i
p session_table[i]
echo \n--- the chunk: tcache next (safe-linked), then the key ---\n
x/2gx session_table[i]
p/x (unsigned long)session_table[i] >> 12
p/x *session_table[i]
  • Which slot? i = 5, and session_table[5] is the pointer being freed (core block, lines 17–18).
  • Was it already free? Yes: next and the key sit where magic and id should be, and name still says sess-5 (lines 21–23). It was freed while the table still held it.
  • The hypothesis: some path frees a session but leaves it in session_table. Search for every free of a session and every store into the table. session_add's error path matches, and the program's own log line, "session 5: invalid peer port, dropped", says that path ran for this id.
  • The fix: store into the table only after the checks pass, or clear the slot on the error path. Better still, give sessions one owner and one release function: the reference counts of Ch 6.
gdb/05-double-free-core.gdb — full source
# 05-double-free-core.gdb — glibc aborted inside free(): who called it, and what is in the chunk?
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/05-double-free-core.gdb ./05-double-free.plain cores/05-double-free.plain.core
set pagination off
bt
echo \n--- frame 8: the pointer free() was given ---\n
frame 8
p mem
echo \n--- frame 9: the lab code, mid-loop ---\n
frame 9
p i
p session_table[i]
echo \n--- the chunk: tcache next (safe-linked), then the key ---\n
x/2gx session_table[i]
p/x (unsigned long)session_table[i] >> 12
p/x *session_table[i]
Deep dive

When the tcache isn't in the way

  • The key is only written when a chunk goes into a tcache. In glibc 2.39 the key is checked on every free of a tcache-sized chunk, even when the bin is full, so a chunk whose first free went into a tcache is caught as long as it is still in the tcache.
  • What escapes is a chunk whose first free went to a fastbin, because its bin was already full or the tcache is off. No key was ever written, so the second free sees nothing.
  • If that second free also goes to a fastbin, the only check compares against the chunk at the top of the bin: double free or corruption (fasttop). Free A, then B, then A again, and nothing notices.
  • Then two later mallocs return the same chunk. Two owners, one object. The crash that follows looks like a use-after-free in unrelated code (Ch 23).
  • Larger chunks go through other checks, such as double free or corruption (!prev) (Ch 3).

Why ASan sees it every time

  • ASan's allocator records each block's state. A free of a block that is already freed is reported, whatever its bins hold.
  • Quarantine keeps a freed block from being handed out again for a long while, so the second free still finds it marked freed instead of reused by someone else.
  • It also records the first free's stack, which no core can give you.

Hardening habits

  • Set the pointer to NULL right after free, in the code that owns it. free(NULL) is a no-op, so a second free through that pointer becomes harmless.
  • That only helps the copy you cleared. A second copy, like the table slot here, is the real problem: one owner per object.
→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 19. Simple Use-After-Free → Decision Matrix

19. Simple Use-After-Free

What you'll learn: why a freed object's first word turns into a pointer that looks almost right, how to prove from a core alone that an object was freed, and why the same bug crashes, runs on, or gets reported depending on which allocator is underneath.

Builds on: tcache and safe-linking (Ch 3, The glibc Allocator), ASan's three stacks (Ch 10, The ASan Family), and Memcheck on the plain build (Ch 12, Valgrind).

19.1 The bug: a drop the caller never hears about

rx_policy frees an oversized BGP job and returns -1. main logs the drop, then processes the job anyway. Ch 10 showed the two functions; here are the layout and the caller.

struct rx_job {                     /* 40 bytes */
    struct pktbuf *buf;             /* 0x00  tcache 'next' after free */
    uint64_t enq_ns;                /* 0x08  tcache 'key' after free */
    uint64_t seq;                   /* 0x10 */
    uint32_t port;                  /* 0x18 */
    uint32_t prio;                  /* 0x1c */
    uint64_t flags;                 /* 0x20 */
};
_Static_assert(sizeof(struct rx_job) == 40, "rx_job is 40 bytes (0x30 chunk)");
⋯
    struct rx_job *job = rx_enqueue(b, 1);
    if (rx_policy(job) < 0)
        fprintf(stderr, "rx: job 1 dropped by policy\n");
    printf("rx: job 1, %u bytes\n", rx_process(job));   /* BUG: ignores the drop */
  • struct rx_job is 40 bytes, so it lives in a 0x30 chunk: 40 bytes of data plus the 8-byte size field, rounded up to 16.
  • Nothing else in the program allocates that size, and there is only one job. When it's freed, its tcache bin is empty.
  • free writes two words into the object (Ch 3): the safe-linked next at +0x00 and the tcache key at +0x08. Those offsets are buf and enq_ns.
  • rx_process reads job->buf->len. It loads the first of those two words and follows it.

The full source is in Ch 10.

19.2 Both paths

make 06-uaf.asan && ./06-uaf.asan. Ch 10 reads this report line by line; these are the lines that solve it.

  2. Lines 2–3: an 8-byte read in rx_process, line 49: the load of job->buf.
  3. Line 5: offset 0 of a 40-byte block. That's buf in an rx_job.
  4. Lines 6–8: freed by rx_policy, line 41. The free is the event the fix has to account for.
  5. Line 10: the summary names the use, not the free. Read past it.

The plain build, one core from make core-06-uaf, and three questions in gdb/06-uaf-core.gdb.

Program terminated with signal SIGSEGV, Segmentation fault.
#0  rx_process (job=0x5555555592e0) at 06-uaf.c:49

#0  rx_process (job=0x5555555592e0) at 06-uaf.c:49

  1. Line 1: SIGSEGV, nothing more.
  2. Lines 2–3: in rx_process, on job->buf->len. job itself, 0x5555555592e0, is a normal heap address.
  3. Line 5: the caller is main, line 62: the printf right after the drop was logged.
  4. Line 6: job->buf is 0x555555559. A pktbuf pointer would be a heap address like job's. This one is far too small, and nothing is mapped there.
  5. Lines 7–8: it equals job >> 12 exactly. That's the safe-linked next of a chunk freed into an empty tcache bin.

So job was freed before this read. The core can't say who freed it, but the question is now narrow: which code frees an rx_job? 19.3 takes it from there.

Symptom → class: SIGSEGV through a pointer field that equals its own object's address >> 12 → use-after-free
gdb/06-uaf-core.gdb — full source
# 06-uaf-core.gdb — the fault address is the freed job's own address >> 12.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/06-uaf-core.gdb ./06-uaf.plain cores/06-uaf.plain.core
set pagination off
bt
p/x job->buf
p/x (unsigned long)job >> 12
p (unsigned long)job->buf == ((unsigned long)job >> 12)

19.3 Reading a freed object in a core

The allocator only writes the start of a freed chunk. Everything else keeps the values the program left there.

Offsetrx_job fieldAfter free into the tcache
+0x00bufnext, safe-linked: (&job->buf >> 12) ^ next
+0x08enq_nsThe tcache key: random per process
+0x10 … +0x27seq, port, prio, flagsUnchanged

The three cases for the first word

  • Equal to &obj >> 12: freed into an empty bin. next is NULL, so the stored value is the mask itself. This lab.
  • Decodes to another chunk: p/x ((unsigned long)&obj >> 12) ^ *(unsigned long *)&obj gives a 16-byte-aligned heap address. That's the next free chunk of the same size. Just as damning.
  • Neither: the chunk may have been handed out again and rewritten by its new owner. Then the type check has to come from the other fields: that's the boss level (Ch 23).

From "freed" to "freed by whom"

  • List every free that can take this type. Here there are two calls: rx_policy's, and main's free(b), which frees the pktbuf, not the job.
  • The unchanged fields tell you which path the object took. p job->port on this core would still show the 179 that rx_policy tests.
  • The fix is an ownership rule. Either rx_policy consumes the job when it returns -1 and the caller must stop using it, or the policy check only decides and the caller frees.

19.4 One bug, three allocators

Which allocator is underneath decides what the bug does. All three runs are captured in this guide.

BuildWhat free does to the jobWhat happens
plain (glibc)Puts it in the tcache: writes next and the key over buf and enq_nsSIGSEGV following buf (19.2)
MemcheckIts own free: marks the block inaccessible, doesn't write itReports the invalid read, then prints 9000 bytes: the stale pointer still works (Ch 12)
ASanPoisons the block and holds it in quarantineStops at the read, with alloc, free and use stacks (19.2)
  • The plain build crashes only because buf sits at offset 0. Had rx_process read job->seq, it would have got the old value and carried on.
  • So "it doesn't crash in debug" or "it doesn't crash under Valgrind" says nothing about whether the bug is there.
Deep dive

Which bytes each free list writes

  • tcache: 16 bytes at the start of the user data, next and the key.
  • fastbins: 8 bytes, a safe-linked fd.
  • unsorted, small and large bins: fd and bk, plain pointers, at +0x00 and +0x08. Large bins also write fd_nextsize and bk_nextsize at +0x10 and +0x18. A chunk merged with a free neighbour can also have its header moved.
  • A plain pointer in a freed chunk that lands inside libc's data is usually a bin head in main_arena. info symbol on it says so.

Making stale reads loud

  • MALLOC_PERTURB_=N fills freed memory with the byte N, so a stale field reads as a pattern instead of a plausible value. It skips the tcache path, so add GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0 (Ch 3).
  • That changes the heap's behaviour too. Use it to confirm a hypothesis, not as a production setting.

A write after free is worse

  • This lab only reads. A write to the first 8 bytes of a freed tcache chunk corrupts next. A later malloc of that size decodes it and may abort with malloc(): unaligned tcache chunk detected. The tcache is per thread, so that abort comes in the thread that freed the chunk, at its next malloc of that size.
  • That abort is the allocator noticing, not the bug. Treat it like Ch 16's header damage: find who wrote the freed chunk.
→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 12. Valgrind → Ch 20. Leak & Memory Growth → Ch 23. Boss Level

20. Leak & Memory Growth

What you'll learn: how LSan, Memcheck and heaptrack each point at a leak, how to tell a true leak from unbounded growth and from fragmentation, and what to do when the only evidence is a daemon whose memory keeps climbing.

Builds on: arenas and the top chunk (Ch 3, The glibc Allocator), gcore (Ch 8, Getting a Usable Core & Symbols), LSan (Ch 10, The ASan Family), and Massif (Ch 12, Valgrind).

20.1 The bug: a close that forgets the table's reference

07-leak opens 2,000 sessions, receives four packets on each, and closes it. session_open stores the new session, which starts with one reference: the table's. session_close clears the slot and drops nothing.

static void session_open(uint32_t id, const struct peer *p)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS] = session_new(id, p);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
}
⋯
static void session_close(uint32_t id)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[id % MAX_SESSIONS] = NULL;    /* BUG: no session_put() */
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&table_lock);
}
  • After the slot is cleared, nothing points at the session. It can never be freed.
  • The packet buffers in session_rx are freed right away. They churn, but don't leak.
  • Nothing crashes. In a daemon, RSS climbs until the out-of-memory killer, or an allocation failure, ends it hours later.

Massif's graph of this run, and the full source, are in Ch 12.

20.2 Both paths

make 07-leak.asan && ./07-leak.asan. LSan is part of ASan and runs at exit.

==126==ERROR: LeakSanitizer: detected memory leaks

Direct leak of 112000 byte(s) in 2000 object(s) allocated from:
    #0 0x7fdab10979c7 in malloc ../../../../src/libsanitizer/asan/asan_malloc_linux.cpp:69
    #1 0x558b0d3054e9 in session_new /lab/pktd.h:128
    #2 0x558b0d3058c5 in session_open /lab/07-leak.c:17
    #3 0x558b0d305c88 in main /lab/07-leak.c:44
⋯
SUMMARY: AddressSanitizer: 112000 byte(s) leaked in 2000 allocation(s).
  1. Line 1: LeakSanitizer ran at exit and found something.
  2. Line 3: a direct leak: 112,000 bytes in 2,000 objects. 56 bytes each is a struct session, one per open/close cycle.
  3. Lines 4–7: where they were allocated: session_new, from session_open, line 17.
  4. The 8,000 packet buffers aren't listed. They were freed.
  5. Line 9: the total, for CI logs.

LSan tells you who allocated the memory. It can't tell you who should have freed it. That's still a code search: who holds this reference, and where should it be dropped?

A leak doesn't crash, so no core arrives by itself. On a daemon you take one with gcore (Ch 8). The lab's GDB stops 07-leak.plain at _exit, after every cycle has run, and writes the core there. gdb/07-leak-core.gdb then compares the table with the heap.

Core was generated by `/lab/07-leak.plain'.

#0  __GI__exit (status=0) at ../sysdeps/unix/sysv/linux/_exit.c:27
⋯

--- where the brk heap starts (libc's malloc state) ---

$2 = {magic = 1582633003, refcnt = 1, id = 1999, flags = 0, created_ns = 8219675758183903, peer = 0x555555556050 <p>, name = "sess-1999", '\000' <repeats 14 times>}
  1. Lines 2–3: SIGTRAP at _exit is GDB's breakpoint, not a crash. main has returned; the heap is as the leak left it.
  2. Line 6: pktd-sessions (Ch 9): the table is empty. Every session was closed.
  3. Line 8: where the brk heap starts, from libc's malloc state (mp_.sbrk_base; needs libc's debug info).
  4. Line 10: the core's own segment that holds it. info proc mappings on a core reads only the file-backed list (NT_FILE), so it has no [heap] line. info files lists the core's load segments, and the script picks the one containing sbrk_base.
  5. Lines 12–15: find /w over that segment for SESSION_MAGIC: 2,000 hits, 0x40 apart, one per chunk. The 1,998 in between are trimmed.
  6. Line 17: the last hit, read as a session: intact (magic = 1582633003 is 0x5E55102B in decimal), refcnt = 1 (the table's reference, never dropped), id = 1999, sess-1999: the last one opened and closed.

2,000 sessions in the heap, none in the table: closed without a put. On a live daemon, take two cores an hour apart; the count of orphans grows between them. A magic number at offset 0 is what makes this search possible, the first of the boss level's design rules (Ch 23).

gdb/07-leak-core.gdb — full source
# 07-leak-core.gdb — a core of the leaking process: sessions in the heap, none in the table.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/07-leak-core.gdb ./07-leak.plain cores/07-leak.exit.core
# "info proc mappings" on a core lists only file-backed mappings (NT_FILE), so there
# is no [heap] line. The heap is one of the core's own load segments: libc's debug
# info says where the brk heap starts, and the Python below picks the segment
# ("info files") that contains that address.
set pagination off
source pktd-gdb.py
echo --- the table: what pktd still references ---\n
pktd-sessions
echo --- where the brk heap starts (libc's malloc state) ---\n
p/x mp_.sbrk_base
echo --- the core segment that holds it ---\n
python
import re
base = int(gdb.parse_and_eval("(unsigned long) mp_.sbrk_base"))
for line in gdb.execute("info files", to_string=True).splitlines():
    m = re.match(r"\s*(0x[0-9a-f]+) - (0x[0-9a-f]+) is (load\w*)\s*$", line)
    if m and int(m.group(1), 16) <= base < int(m.group(2), 16):
        print(line.strip())
        gdb.set_convenience_variable("heap_lo", int(m.group(1), 16))
        gdb.set_convenience_variable("heap_hi", int(m.group(2), 16))
        break
end
echo --- every 4-byte word in it equal to SESSION_MAGIC ---\n
find /w $heap_lo, $heap_hi - 4, 0x5E55102B
echo --- the last hit, read as a session ---\n
p *(struct session *)$_
Symptom → class: heap or RSS grows steadily under steady load, with no crash → leak or unbounded growth

20.3 Leak, growth or fragmentation?

"Memory keeps going up" has three different causes. The tools only call one of them a leak. Memcheck's summary shows the categories:

==134==     in use at exit: 112,000 bytes in 2,000 blocks
==134==   total heap usage: 10,001 allocs, 8,001 frees, 564,096 bytes allocated
⋯
==134== 112,000 bytes in 2,000 blocks are definitely lost in loss record 1 of 1
==134==    at 0x4846828: malloc (in /usr/libexec/valgrind/vgpreload_memcheck-amd64-linux.so)
==134==    by 0x109291: session_new (pktd.h:128)
==134==    by 0x10934E: session_open (07-leak.c:17)
==134==    by 0x109477: main (07-leak.c:44)
⋯
==134==    definitely lost: 112,000 bytes in 2,000 blocks
⋯
==134==    still reachable: 0 bytes in 0 blocks
  1. Line 1: 112,000 bytes in 2,000 blocks still allocated at exit.
  2. Line 2: 10,001 allocations, 8,001 frees. The 8,000 packet buffers balance; the sessions don't.
  3. Lines 4–8: the same allocation stack LSan gave.
  4. Line 10: definitely lost: no pointer to the block remains anywhere.
  5. Line 12: still reachable: blocks some pointer still reaches at exit. 0 here. In a daemon, this is where unbounded growth hides.
CauseWhat's trueWhat finds it
True leakNothing points to the block any moreLSan, Memcheck definitely lost, heaptrack
Unbounded growthStill referenced: a cache that never evicts, a list nobody trims. LSan stays silentMassif or heaptrack over time; Memcheck still reachable with --show-leak-kinds=all; counting entries in a core
FragmentationThe program freed it, but the allocator can't give the pages back. Live bytes are flat, RSS isn'tmalloc_info or malloc_stats (Deep dive): lots of free bytes inside the arenas
  • Leak checkers run at exit. A daemon that never exits never reports: call __lsan_do_recoverable_leak_check() from a debug command (Ch 10), or stop it cleanly in a test.
  • Growth is a design bug, not a lost pointer. Ask "what bounds this structure?", and export its size in your stats.

20.4 heaptrack: allocations, leaks and temporaries

heaptrack records every malloc and free with its stack, much faster than Valgrind. It needs no rebuild, and it can also attach to a running process (heaptrack -p <pid>).

heaptrack stats:
	allocations:          	10002
	leaked allocations:   	2001
	temporary allocations:	8000
⋯
MEMORY LEAKS
112.00K leaked over 2000 calls from
session_new
  at /lab/pktd.h:128
  in /lab/07-leak.plain
112.00K leaked over 2000 calls from:
    session_open
      at /lab/07-leak.c:17
      in /lab/07-leak.plain
    main
      at /lab/07-leak.c:44
      in /lab/07-leak.plain
⋯
4.10K leaked over 1 calls from
__GI__IO_file_doallocate
⋯
peak heap memory consumption: 189.82K
⋯
total memory leaked: 116.10K
  1. Lines 2–3: 10,002 allocations, 2,001 not freed by exit.
  2. Line 4: 8,000 temporary allocations: freed right after they were made, with no other allocation in between. The packet buffers. Not a leak, but in a hot path, a cost.
  3. Lines 7–17: 2,000 of the unfreed blocks: session_new from session_open, as before.
  4. Lines 19–20: the 2,001st, 4.10K from stdio: stdout's buffer, made by main's printf (stack trimmed). LSan didn't count it, because libc's stdout still points to it; heaptrack counts anything unfreed at exit. It's allocated once, so it isn't growth.
  5. Lines 22, 24: the peak, and the total unfreed: 112.00K + 4.10K.
The question for a growing daemon is "which call site's live bytes keep rising?". heaptrack_gui or heaptrack_print answer it per call site over time, as Massif does (Ch 12), without Valgrind's slowdown.
Deep dive

malloc_info and fragmentation

  • malloc_info(0, fp) writes an XML report, one <heap> per arena. <total type="fast"> and <total type="rest"> give the count and bytes of free chunks; <system type="current"> is what the arena holds from the kernel.
  • Fragmentation looks like this: the system bytes stay high, the free bytes are a large share of them, and your own live-object count is flat.
  • malloc_stats() prints a shorter per-arena summary to stderr. Both are calls a debug command in the daemon can make. You can also call them from GDB on a live process, at your own risk: that runs code inside the daemon.

Why freed memory stays

  • A heap gives memory back to the kernel from its top. One long-lived object near the end pins everything below it.
  • Each thread arena (Ch 3) keeps its own free memory. A burst handled by many workers can leave free chunks in many arenas.
  • malloc_trim(0) also returns whole free pages from the middle of each arena, with madvise. M_ARENA_MAX (or GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.arena_max=N) caps the number of arenas. Both trade speed for footprint.

Direct and indirect

  • If a leaked session had owned a heap name buffer, LSan would list that buffer as an indirect leak: reachable only from the leaked session.
  • Fix the direct leaks first. The indirect ones usually disappear with them.
→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 8. Getting a Usable Core & Symbols → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Ch 12. Valgrind → Ch 21. Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag

21. Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag

What you'll learn: why ++ on a shared counter loses updates, how to read Helgrind's lockset view of the race TSan found in Ch 11, why Valgrind finds the race while the loss disappears, and what a race leaves behind when all you have is the wrong number.

Builds on: data races and happens-before (Ch 5, The C Memory Model), atomic read-modify-write (Ch 6, Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting), the TSan report (Ch 11, TSan), and DRD (Ch 12, Valgrind).

21.1 The bug: ++ is three steps

Four workers each count a million packets into stats.pkts. The rule says stats_lock; the loop doesn't take it.

    for (int i = 0; i < PKTS_PER_WORKER; i++) {
        if (use_atomic)
            atomic_fetch_add((_Atomic uint64_t *)&stats.pkts, 1);
        else
            stats.pkts++;           /* BUG: stats_lock not held */
    }
  • At -O0, stats.pkts++ is a load into a register, an add, and a store back. Each iteration reads memory again.
  • Two workers load the same value, both add 1, both store. Two increments, one counted.
  • A worker that's interrupted between its load and its store can erase much more: everything the others added in the meantime is overwritten by its stale value plus one.

The full source is in Ch 11.

21.2 Both paths

TSan's report is in Ch 11. Helgrind finds the same race on the unmodified plain build: valgrind --tool=helgrind ./08-race.plain.

==210== Possible data race during read of size 8 at 0x10C2C0 by thread #3
==210== Locks held: none
==210==    at 0x1091F3: worker (08-race.c:26)
⋯
==210== This conflicts with a previous write of size 8 by thread #2
==210== Locks held: none
==210==    at 0x1091FE: worker (08-race.c:26)
⋯
==210==  Address 0x10c2c0 is 0 bytes inside data symbol "stats"
⋯
expected=4000000 actual=4000000 lost=0
⋯
==210== ERROR SUMMARY: 6 errors from 2 contexts (suppressed: 4 from 2)
  1. Line 1: a read by thread #3. Valgrind numbers threads from 1, the main thread; the trimmed lines announce where each was created.
  2. Line 2: Locks held: none. This is Helgrind's most useful line. Had one side held stats_lock and the other not, it would tell you which path forgot.
  3. Line 3: worker, line 26.
  4. Lines 5–7: the conflicting earlier write, by thread #2, same line, also with no lock.
  5. Line 9: the address is stats, offset 0: pkts.
  6. Line 11: no updates lost. Valgrind runs one thread at a time, so the bad interleaving never happened. Helgrind reported the race anyway: it reasons about ordering, not outcomes.
  7. Line 13: two contexts, the read side and the write side of the same ++.

No crash, no report. Just a number that's wrong:

expected=4000000 actual=1458198 lost=2541802
  1. Line 1: 2.5 million of 4 million updates lost, on the plain build, with the threads running in parallel.

A core of this process would show stats.pkts with the same wrong value. It holds no history: which threads wrote, and in what order, is gone. What you can do from the core and the code:

  • Find a pair that should balance and doesn't. Packets counted vs. packets processed; sessions opened minus closed vs. sessions in the table. The field that's off is the one to suspect.
  • List every writer of that field. Search for the field name and check, for each write, which lock is held. A write without stats_lock is the hypothesis.
  • Turn the hypothesis into a test. A loop, more threads, a TSan build, or rr chaos mode (Ch 24).
Symptom → class: counts or state wrong by a different amount on each run → data race

21.3 The same race, five runs

Every number below comes from a capture in this guide. Expected is 4,000,000 every time.

RunLostRace reported?Why
plain2,541,802No toolThreads on separate cores can interleave between any two instructions
TSan build1,509,804Yes, 2 warningsTSan watches; the threads still run in parallel (Ch 11)
Helgrind0YesSerialized by Valgrind; detected from ordering
DRD0Yes, 6,000,000 errorsThe same; DRD counts every occurrence (Ch 12)
TSan, atomic0NoThe fix: one atomic RMW per increment (Ch 11)
  • The detectors found the race whether or not it did damage in that run. That's the point of happens-before (Ch 5): the access pair is unordered, so it's a bug.
  • The loss depends on scheduling, and so does whether a test notices. A test that checks the total under Valgrind passes.

21.4 Choosing a detector, and a fix

TSanHelgrindDRD
RebuildYes, every file that touches shared stateNoNo
ThreadsRun in parallelSerializedSerialized
Report shapeBoth stacks, plus where each thread was createdBoth stacks, plus the locks held at eachThe access, plus the other thread's code segment
Lock-order cyclesYesYesNo
Best forCI and soak testsFinding which path skipped the lockLower memory than Helgrind on big programs

Three fixes, by cost

  • The lock the rule names. Correct, and the right choice when the counter has to stay consistent with other fields under the same lock.
  • An atomic. atomic_fetch_add; relaxed is enough for a statistic (Ch 6). Declare the field _Atomic so no plain access can slip back in.
  • Per-thread counters, summed on read. No shared write at all, so no cache-line bouncing between cores.
Deep dive

A flag, not a counter

  • The same class covers flags: if (!s->closing) { s->closing = 1; teardown(s); } run by two threads can tear down twice. The symptom is an action that happened twice, or never.
  • A volatile flag doesn't help (Ch 5). Use an atomic exchange or compare-and-swap, and act only if your thread is the one that changed it.

"It's only a statistic"

  • In C11 a data race is undefined behaviour, not just a wrong count. The optimizer may keep the value in a register across a whole loop, or split a store in two.
  • Racy statistics also bury real races in TSan output. Fix them, or make them atomic, so every report you see is worth reading.

How much can one stale store erase?

  • Everything other threads added between that thread's load and its store. In parallel on real cores that window is a few instructions, so losses are small and many.
  • When threads run one at a time with long timeslices, as under rr, one switch in the wrong place can erase a whole thread's work. Ch 24 shows a run that lost exactly one worker's million.
→ Ch 5. The C Memory Model → Ch 6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting → Ch 11. TSan → Ch 12. Valgrind → Ch 22. Lock-Order Deadlock → Ch 24. From a One-Shot Core to a Reproducer

22. Lock-Order Deadlock

What you'll learn: why the detectors run a program that never hangs while the core comes from one that does, how to read Helgrind's lock-order report, and how to turn a hung process into a proven cycle: mutex __owner → LWP → thread → the lock it waits for.

Builds on: mutex internals and lock ordering (Ch 6, Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting), gcore (Ch 8, Getting a Usable Core & Symbols), and TSan's inversion report (Ch 11, TSan).

22.1 The bug, in two modes

The stats thread takes stats_lock, then table_lock: the reverse of pktd's order. The lab runs it two ways.

 *   inversion  control finishes table -> stats, a barrier, then stats takes
 *              stats -> table. Never hangs, but both lock edges are recorded,
 *              so TSan and Helgrind report the cycle.
 *   hang       both threads sleep while holding their first lock: a real
 *              deadlock. Use it with gcore.
⋯
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);
    session_table[1] = session_new(1, &p);
    if (hang)
        usleep(100000);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
⋯
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    if (hang)
        usleep(100000);
    pthread_mutex_lock(&table_lock);    /* BUG: stats_lock -> table_lock */
  • inversion: a barrier makes the control thread finish table → stats before the stats thread starts stats → table. Both orders happen, never at the same time. It never hangs, and exits cleanly.
  • hang: each thread sleeps 100 ms holding its first lock, so both hold one lock when they ask for the other. A real deadlock, every run.

Why the detectors use inversion and the core uses hang

  • TSan and Helgrind record an edge each time a lock is taken while another is held (Ch 6). The inversion run records both edges, so the cycle is in the graph, and the run still finishes and prints its report. In the hang run, TSan checks before the blocking lock call, so it prints the cycle and then hangs anyway; Helgrind checks after a lock is acquired, so it has nothing to report. Either way, a run that really deadlocks still hangs CI.
  • A core is a snapshot. A core of the inversion run would show threads that aren't stuck: there the deadlock was only possible. The hang run is the state you'd find in production, so that's what gcore captures.

The full source is in Ch 11.

22.2 Both paths

TSan's report is in Ch 11. Helgrind, on the plain build: valgrind --tool=helgrind ./09-deadlock.plain inversion.

==253== Thread #3: lock order "0x10C040 before 0x10C080" violated
==253==

==253==    at 0x48512DC: ??? (in /usr/libexec/valgrind/vgpreload_helgrind-amd64-linux.so)
==253==    by 0x10942F: control_thread (09-deadlock.c:29)
⋯
==253==  Address 0x10c040 is 0 bytes inside data symbol "table_lock"
⋯

stats: pkts=1 live=1
pktd: clean exit
  1. Line 1: thread #3 broke the order "0x10C040 before 0x10C080".
  2. Lines 3–9: what it did: took 0x10C080 at line 45, then 0x10C040 at line 48, in stats_thread. The ??? frames are Helgrind's own wrapper around pthread_mutex_lock.
  3. Lines 11–17: where the order came from: control_thread took 0x10C040 at line 25, then 0x10C080 at line 29.
  4. Lines 19, 21: the names. 0x10C040 is table_lock, 0x10C080 is stats_lock.
  5. Lines 23–24: the program finished. Nothing hung, and the cycle was reported anyway.

"Required order" only means "seen first". The barrier makes the control thread go first, so Helgrind blames the stats thread, which matches pktd's documented order. Check the report against your rule, not the other way round.

The hang run, captured live with gcore while it was stuck, then opened with gdb/09-deadlock-core.gdb. gcore's own output (22.4) and a missing-source warning are trimmed.

Core was generated by `./09-deadlock.plain'.

#4  0x00005634ba4c8430 in control_thread (arg=0x0) at 09-deadlock.c:29
⋯
Thread 2 (Thread 0x7f25ff75f6c0 (LWP 272)):
#0  futex_wait (private=0, expected=2, futex_word=0x5634ba4cb040 <table_lock>) at ../sysdeps/nptl/futex-internal.h:146

#4  0x00005634ba4c84e2 in stats_thread (arg=0x0) at 09-deadlock.c:48

$1 = 271
$2 = 272
  1. Lines 4–7: info threads. Thread 1 is main. Threads 2 and 3 are in futex_wait, and GDB names the futex word: table_lock and stats_lock. That's the lock each one is waiting for (Ch 6).
  2. Lines 9–12: LWP 271 is control_thread, stuck at line 29: waiting for stats_lock.
  3. Lines 14–17: LWP 272 is stats_thread, stuck at line 48: waiting for table_lock.
  4. Line 19: table_lock.__data.__owner is 271: the control thread, which is waiting for stats_lock.
  5. Line 20: stats_lock's owner is 272: the stats thread, which is waiting for table_lock.
  6. Each owner is the other lock's waiter. That's the cycle, proven from memory. Your LWP numbers will differ; the pairing is what matters.
Symptom → class: process alive but stuck, threads parked in futex_wait on mutexes → lock-order deadlock
gdb/09-deadlock-core.gdb — full source
# 09-deadlock-core.gdb — a gcore of the hung process: who waits, who owns.
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/09-deadlock-core.gdb ./09-deadlock.plain cores/09.<pid>
set pagination off
info threads
thread apply all bt
p table_lock.__data.__owner
p stats_lock.__data.__owner

22.3 From __owner to a cycle

The recipe works for any number of locks. Start at any waiting thread and follow the chain.

  1. In thread apply all bt, find each thread blocked in a lock call. Its frames name the mutex it wants.
  2. p <mutex>.__data.__owner: the LWP that holds it.
  3. Find that LWP in info threads. GDB's thread number and the LWP are different numbers; match on the LWP.
  4. What is that thread waiting for? Go to step 2 with its mutex.
  5. Back at a thread you've already visited: a cycle, a deadlock.
Lock__ownerOwner threadOwner waits for
table_lock271control_thread (GDB thread 3)stats_lock
stats_lock272stats_thread (GDB thread 2)table_lock

When the chain doesn't close

  • It ends at a thread that isn't waiting for a lock: not a deadlock. The holder is slow, or stuck somewhere else: a syscall, a poll, a loop. Read its stack.
  • The owner's LWP isn't in info threads: a thread exited while holding the lock. Nobody will ever release it (Ch 6).
  • __owner is 0 but threads wait: a lost wake-up, or a lock that isn't a plain mutex. Condition variables and reader-writer locks don't record a single owner the same way (Deep dive).

22.4 Taking the core without killing the process

0x00007f25ffffce31 in __futex_abstimed_wait_cancelable64 () from /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6
Saved corefile cores/09.269
[Inferior 1 (process 269) detached]
  1. Line 1: gcore attached and stopped the process. This is where its first thread was: main, in pthread_join's futex wait.
  2. Line 2: the core is written.
  3. Line 3: gcore detached. The process carries on, still hung, exactly as before. The lab kills it afterwards.
  • In production, take the core before you restart. A restart clears the symptom and destroys the evidence.
  • A hang is the one bug where any moment is the right moment for the core: the state doesn't change while you wait.
  • If you can only run commands, gdb -p <pid> -batch -ex "thread apply all bt" gives the stacks, and the same p commands give the owners. Take the core too, so you can look again later.
Deep dive

What main is waiting for

  • Thread 1's frame shows expected=271. pthread_join sleeps on the joined thread's TID field, which the kernel clears when that thread exits. 271 is the control thread, so main is joining it.
  • The same reading works for any join that never returns: the expected value is the TID of the thread that never finished.

Other locks

  • Reader-writer locks: glibc records the writer's TID in __data.__cur_writer. Readers aren't recorded one by one, so a reader that never unlocks has to be found from stacks.
  • Condition variables: no owner at all. A thread stuck in pthread_cond_wait is waiting for a signal that never came. Look for the code that should have sent it, and check that it changes the predicate under the same mutex.
  • Spinlocks: the waiters are running, not parked. The process burns CPU instead of sitting idle.

Preventing it

  • Write the order down, as pktd.h does, and keep TSan or Helgrind in CI: they catch the inversion in a test that passes.
  • pthread_mutex_trylock plus back-off breaks a cycle at run time, at the cost of retry logic. Fixing the order is almost always better.
  • Error-checking mutexes (PTHREAD_MUTEX_ERRORCHECK) return EDEADLK when a thread locks a mutex it already holds: the one-thread version of this bug.
→ Ch 6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting → Ch 8. Getting a Usable Core & Symbols → Ch 11. TSan → Ch 12. Valgrind → Ch 23. Boss Level

23. Boss Level: Refcount Use-After-Free, Crash Far Away

What you'll learn: how to solve a crash whose cause is in another thread, in another function, earlier in time, four ways: core forensics, ASan, a watchpoint or rr walking back from the crash, and the design changes that turn the next one into a one-line report.

Builds on: tcache reuse and safe-linking (Ch 3, The glibc Allocator), refcount rules (Ch 6, Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting), hardware watchpoints (Ch 7, GDB on a Live Process), reading a core (Ch 9, GDB on a Core), ASan (Ch 10, The ASan Family) and rr (Ch 13, rr).

23.1 The scenario

Worker 3's timeout path drops a reference to session 7 that it never took. Nothing goes wrong yet: worker 2 still holds one. When worker 2 drops its own, correctly, the count hits zero and the session is freed while the table still points to it. Worker 2's next allocation is a packet buffer of the same size, and the allocator hands it the same chunk. Later the control thread dumps the table and dies on the packet's bytes.

void handle_timeout(struct session *s)
{
    pthread_mutex_lock(&stats_lock);
    stats.drops++;
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&stats_lock);
    session_put(s);
}
⋯
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);   /* 0: sessions exist */
    if (me == 2) {
        s = lookup(7);
        session_get(s);             /* 1: our reference, 1 -> 2 */
    }
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    if (me == 3)
        handle_timeout(lookup(7));  /* 2: 2 -> 1 */
    pthread_barrier_wait(&phase);
    if (me == 2) {
        session_put(s);             /* 3: our put, correct, but 1 -> 0: free() */
        pb = malloc(sizeof *pb);    /* the next packet: same thread, same bin */
        pb->len = sizeof pb->data;
        pb->port = 179;
        pb->ts = now_ns();
        memcpy(pb->data, "ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklm", sizeof pb->data);
    }
PhaseThreadDoesrefcnt
0controlCreates sessions 1–8; session 7's peer is &peers[2]1
1worker 2session_get(7)1 → 2
2worker 3handle_timeout(7): a put with no get. The bug2 → 1
3worker 2session_put(7), correct, so free. Then malloc and fill a pktbuf: same chunk1 → 0
4controltable_dump() reads session 7's peer: SIGSEGV—
  • Barriers force this order on every run. A real daemon has no barriers: the gap between the bad put and the crash can be seconds or hours, and the crash may never come (Ch 24).
  • The thread that crashes did nothing wrong. Neither did the thread that freed the session. The guilty thread finished its work long before.

The full source is in Ch 7.

23.2 Both paths

./10-refcount-uaf.asan. 23.4 reads the rest of the report.

==186==ERROR: AddressSanitizer: heap-use-after-free on address 0x506000000278 at pc 0x5555c91ecdbc bp 0x7fac191fecd0 sp 0x7fac191fecc0
READ of size 8 at 0x506000000278 thread T1
    #0 0x5555c91ecdbb in table_dump /lab/pktd.h:191
    #1 0x5555c91ed9ac in control_main /lab/10-refcount-uaf.c:98

⋯
