    #2 0x5555c91ed55b in worker_main /lab/10-refcount-uaf.c:75
  1. Line 1: heap-use-after-free, not a wild pointer.
  2. Lines 2–4: a read in table_dump, called from control_main. The same place the plain build dies.
  3. Line 6: 24 bytes into a 56-byte block. Offset 0x18 of a struct session is peer.
  4. Lines 7–10: freed by thread T3, in session_put, from worker_main line 75. That's worker 2's own put, and it was correct. Why ASan names it is 23.4.

The plain build's core, from make core-10-refcount-uaf.

Program terminated with signal SIGSEGV, Segmentation fault.
#0  table_dump () at /lab/pktd.h:191
191	               (s->peer->addr >> 8) & 0xff, s->peer->addr & 0xff, s->peer->port);
⋯
$2 = 0x28
  1. Line 1: SIGSEGV.
  2. Lines 2–3: in table_dump, on line 191, part of the printf that follows s->peer. Printing a table is not where bugs usually live.
  3. Line 5: p/x s->magic is 0x28. A live session says 0x5e55102b. Whatever s points at, it isn't a session any more.

From here the question is "what is it, then?". 23.3 answers it from the bytes alone.

Symptom → class: a crash in innocent code, on an object whose magic doesn't match its type → use-after-free with reuse (often a refcount bug)

23.3 Technique 1: core forensics

First: is it the same crash every time?

run 1: rc=139
run 2: rc=139
run 3: rc=139
run 4: rc=139
run 5: rc=139
--- run 1 output:
[ 1] id=1 refcnt=1 name=sess-1 peer=10.0.0.1:179
[ 2] id=2 refcnt=1 name=sess-2 peer=10.0.0.2:179
[ 3] id=3 refcnt=1 name=sess-3 peer=10.0.0.3:179
[ 4] id=4 refcnt=1 name=sess-4 peer=10.0.0.4:179
[ 5] id=5 refcnt=1 name=sess-5 peer=10.0.0.1:179
[ 6] id=6 refcnt=1 name=sess-6 peer=10.0.0.2:179
⋯
unique=1
  1. Lines 1–5: five runs of the plain build, all exit status 139: 128 + 11, SIGSEGV.
  2. Lines 7–12: table_dump printed sessions 1–6. Session 7 never printed: the crash is on it.
  3. Line 14: unique=1. All five runs produced byte-identical output.

The barriers fix the order, and the tcache's per-thread LIFO makes the reuse certain (Ch 3). So every core of this lab tells the same story, and you can check each step below against your own.

Read the object as both types

gdb/10-core.gdb goes to table_dump's frame and prints *s as a session, then as a pktbuf:

$1 = {magic = 40, refcnt = 179, id = 3798382428, flags = 1912158, created_ns = 5208208757389214273, peer = 0x504f4e4d4c4b4a49, name = "QRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklm"}
$2 = 0x28
$3 = {len = 40, port = 179, ts = 8212659873167196, data = "ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklm"}
OffsetAs a session (line 1)As a pktbuf (line 3)Reads as
0x00magic = 40 (0x28)len = 40sizeof pb->data
0x04refcnt = 179port = 179The BGP port, not a count
0x08id, flagstsOne timestamp split in two: flags × 2³² + id = ts
0x10created_ns = 5208208757389214273data[0..7]0x4847464544434241: the bytes "ABCDEFGH"
0x18peer = 0x504f4e4d4c4b4a49data[8..15]The bytes "IJKLMNOP"
0x20name = "QRST…m"data[16..39]The rest of the payload
  • As a session, every field is nonsense. As a pktbuf, every field makes sense: a length that matches the buffer, a well-known port, a timestamp, ASCII data. The chunk belongs to a pktbuf now.
  • peer is eight ASCII bytes. Bits 63–47 of 0x504f… aren't all equal, so it's non-canonical: the CPU raises a general-protection fault, and the kernel can't report the address (Ch 1).
  • This crashed only because those eight bytes were non-canonical. A payload that happened to look like a valid pointer would have printed garbage, or read some other object, and gone on.

Reason backwards

  1. Session 7 was freed while the table still pointed at it. The table holds a reference, so the count reached 0 one put too early. Somebody put a reference they didn't own.
  2. Who reused the chunk? Search for code that fills a pktbuf with len = sizeof data, port 179 and that payload: worker_main, lines 76–80.
  3. The tcache is per thread and LIFO. A freed 0x40 chunk goes to the freeing thread's tcache, and that thread's next 0x40 malloc gets it back. So the thread that allocated the pktbuf is very likely the one that freed the session, right before line 76.
  4. That worker's put, at line 75, is matched by its get at line 68. It's legitimate, and it was the last put. The extra one came earlier.
  5. List every session_put call site without a matching get in the same thread. handle_timeout is one. p stats.drops on the core is a cheap check: handle_timeout counts a drop just before its put, so a nonzero value says that path ran.

The core alone can't prove step 5; it gives you a short list of suspects. Techniques 3 and 4 turn the suspect into a proof.

gdb/10-core.gdb — full source
# 10-core.gdb — the boss crash, read from its core: whose bytes are in session 7?
#   gdb -q -batch -nx -x gdb/10-core.gdb ./10-refcount-uaf.plain cores/10-refcount-uaf.plain.core
set pagination off
source pktd-gdb.py
bt
frame function table_dump
p *s
p/x s->magic
p *(struct pktbuf *)s
pktd-sessions

23.4 Technique 2: ASan, and why it names the wrong put

0x506000000278 is located 24 bytes inside of 56-byte region [0x506000000260,0x506000000298)
freed by thread T3 here:
    #0 0x7fac1cdaa4d8 in free ../../../../src/libsanitizer/asan/asan_malloc_linux.cpp:52
    #1 0x5555c91ecbdf in session_put /lab/pktd.h:163
    #2 0x5555c91ed55b in worker_main /lab/10-refcount-uaf.c:75
⋯
previously allocated by thread T1 here:
    #0 0x7fac1cdab9c7 in malloc ../../../../src/libsanitizer/asan/asan_malloc_linux.cpp:69
    #1 0x5555c91ec6a9 in session_new /lab/pktd.h:128
    #2 0x5555c91ed84d in control_main /lab/10-refcount-uaf.c:93
⋯
Thread T3 created by T0 here:
    #0 0x7fac1cda31f9 in pthread_create ../../../../src/libsanitizer/asan/asan_interceptors.cpp:245
    #1 0x5555c91edc57 in main /lab/10-refcount-uaf.c:122
⋯
=>0x506000000200: 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 fa fa fa fa fa fd fd fd[fd]
  1. Line 1: the bad read is at offset 24 of a 56-byte block: peer, the same field.
  2. Lines 2–5: the free: thread T3, in session_put at pktd.h:163, called from worker_main line 75.
  3. Lines 7–10: the allocation: thread T1, session_new from control_main line 93. One of sessions 1–8.
  4. Lines 12–14: T3 was created at line 122, the worker loop. ASan numbers threads in creation order: T0 is main, T1 the control thread (created at line 120), then the workers in order. T3 is worker 2.
  5. Line 16: the shadow row ends in four fd granules; the next row, trimmed, holds three more: 56 freed bytes. The bracketed one, the fourth, is bytes 24–31: peer.

What quarantine changed

  • Worker 2 still did its malloc, but ASan's allocator didn't hand back the freed chunk: it sits in quarantine, poisoned. So table_dump reads freed memory, and ASan flags the first such read, whatever the bytes would have been.
  • Both builds stop in table_dump. The plain build stops on packet bytes, and only because they're non-canonical. ASan stops on any access to the dead session and names the bug class, the block and its history.
"Freed by" names who called free, not who is to blame. A refcounted object is freed by whichever put takes it to 0. Here that was worker 2's own, correct put. The guilty put, in handle_timeout, only moved the count from 2 to 1: no free, nothing for ASan to record. For a refcounted object, read ASan's free stack as "the count reached 0 here, one put too early", then go looking for the extra one.

23.5 Technique 3: watchpoint and rr

Every put changes refcnt, so a watchpoint on it sees the guilty put too. Ch 7 showed the mechanics and stopped at the 1 → 0 put. Here are the summary lines for all five writes, in the order they happened, with the detail of the two Ch 7 left for this chapter.

HIT 1->2 handle_timeout=0
⋯
HIT 2->1 handle_timeout=1
⋯
HIT 1->0 handle_timeout=0
⋯
Old value = 0
New value = 7
tcache_put (tc_idx=2, chunk=0x7ffff0000ce0) at ./malloc/malloc.c:3166
⋯
HIT 0->7 handle_timeout=0
⋯
Old value = 7
New value = 179
worker_main (arg=0x2) at 10-refcount-uaf.c:79
⋯
HIT 7->179 handle_timeout=0
  1. Lines 1, 3, 5: the three refcount writes: worker 2's get, the put from handle_timeout, worker 2's put to 0. Only the second has handle_timeout=1: $_caller_is says that put was called from handle_timeout. That's the bug.
  2. Lines 7–11: the fourth write is inside free: tcache_put, bin 2 (the 0x40 chunks), with chunk at the session's address − 0x10. It stores the safe-linked next over the first 8 bytes: magic is its low half, refcnt its high half (Ch 3).
  3. Why 7? With an empty bin, next is NULL, so the stored word is &s >> 12. For 0x7ffff0000cf0 that's 0x7ffff0000, whose high 32 bits are 7. The same signature as Ch 19.
  4. Lines 13–17: the fifth write. refcnt becomes 179, the pktbuf's port, in worker_main (arg=0x2): worker 2, the same thread. It's reported at line 79 because a hardware watchpoint traps after the store; the store is line 78, pb->port = 179.
  5. The len store, which made magic 40, isn't here. The watchpoint covers only refcnt's 4 bytes.

That worked because the script found session 7 through session_table at control_ready, before anything went wrong, and because the next run behaved the same. A forward watchpoint needs both: you must know what to watch, and the bug must happen again.

rr: start from the crash

rr removes both conditions. Record once. In the replay, run to the SIGSEGV, take s from table_dump's frame, watch its refcnt, and reverse-continue. Ch 13 showed the first and last stops; here are all four writes, with the detail of the two in the middle that Ch 13 elided.

HIT 179->7 handle_timeout=0
⋯
Old value = 7
New value = 0
0x00007f74749c7425 in tcache_put (tc_idx=2, chunk=0x7f746c000ce0) at ./malloc/malloc.c:3165
⋯
HIT 7->0 handle_timeout=0
⋯
Old value = 0
New value = 1
0x000056498a8e8454 in session_put (s=0x7f746c000cf0) at /lab/pktd.h:151
⋯
HIT 0->1 handle_timeout=0
⋯
#1  0x000056498a8e86f8 in worker_main (arg=0x2) at 10-refcount-uaf.c:75
⋯
HIT 1->2 handle_timeout=1
  1. Read top to bottom, this goes back in time, with Old/New swapped, as Ch 13 explained. The first reverse-continue, not shown, re-hits the SIGSEGV.
  2. Line 1: the port store, 179 → 7 read backwards.
  3. Lines 3–7: back into free: tcache_put, at malloc.c:3165. Going backwards, rr stops on the writing statement itself; going forwards, the trap was reported one line later, at 3166.
  4. Lines 9–15: worker 2's put, the one ASan named, called from worker_main line 75.
  5. Line 17: the fifth reverse-continue: the 2 → 1 write, handle_timeout=1. The bug.
The watch target is chosen after the crash. With rr you don't need to know in advance which object will die, or wait for it to happen again. The crashing frame names the object, and the recording already holds every write to it.

23.6 Technique 4: design so the next one is cheap

The first three techniques took a core, a rebuild, a debugger and some reasoning. A debug build can do most of that work for you, every time. pktd.h's PKTD_REFTRACE build, make 10-refcount-uaf.reftrace, adds three things.

static inline void ref_record(uint32_t id, char op, int after, void *caller)
{
    struct ref_ring *r = &rings[id % MAX_SESSIONS];
    unsigned slot = __atomic_fetch_add(&r->head, 1, __ATOMIC_RELAXED) % REF_RING;
    r->ev[slot] = (struct ref_event){ op, after, caller, gettid() };
}
⋯
#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
    /* Poison, don't free: keep refcnt and id, smash created_ns/peer/name
     * (0x10..0x37), and leave the chunk quarantined forever. */
    s->magic = SESSION_DEAD;
    memset((char *)s + offsetof(struct session, created_ns), 0x5A,
           sizeof *s - offsetof(struct session, created_ns));
#else
    free(s);
#endif
⋯
#ifdef PKTD_REFTRACE
static inline void session_check(struct session *s)
{
    if (s->magic != SESSION_DEAD)
        return;
    fprintf(stderr, "session %u: use after final put\n", s->id);
    ref_dump(s->id);
    abort();
}
  • A ring per session id, in a side table outside struct session: each get and put records its op, the count after it, the caller's return address and the thread ID. The struct's layout is the same in both builds.
  • Poison instead of free on the final put: magic becomes SESSION_DEAD, everything from created_ns on is filled with 0x5A, and the chunk is never reused. refcnt and id are kept for the report.
  • A check before use. table_dump calls session_check before it reads any field. A dead session aborts, with its history.
session 7: use after final put
  #0  N refcnt=1 tid=267  ./10-refcount-uaf.reftrace(control_main+0x52)[0x55d1d788fb0d]
  #1  G refcnt=2 tid=269  ./10-refcount-uaf.reftrace(worker_main+0x56)[0x55d1d788f9f3]
  #2  P refcnt=1 tid=270  ./10-refcount-uaf.reftrace(handle_timeout+0x4c)[0x55d1d788f99a]
  #3  P refcnt=0 tid=269  ./10-refcount-uaf.reftrace(worker_main+0x9e)[0x55d1d788fa3b]
bash: line 1:   266 Aborted                 ./10-refcount-uaf.reftrace
rc=134
  1. Line 1: table_dump found SESSION_DEAD on session 7 and stopped, before touching peer.
  2. Line 2: N, created by the control thread: the table's reference.
  3. Line 3: G by tid 269 from worker_main: worker 2's get.
  4. Line 4: P by tid 270 from handle_timeout. Tid 270 has no G. A put with no get in the same thread: that's the bug, in one line.
  5. Line 5: the final P, tid 269 again: worker 2's own, balanced put.
  6. Lines 6–7: abort(), exit status 134: 128 + 6, SIGABRT. A core taken here still has the ring in memory: p rings[7].
  • -rdynamic puts the program's function names in its dynamic symbol table, so backtrace_symbols_fd can print handle_timeout+0x4c without debug info.
  • The underflow assert (after >= 0) didn't fire here: the count went 2 → 1 → 0, never below. It catches an extra put that comes after the final one. With poison instead of free, refcnt stays at 0 in a live chunk, so such a put takes it to −1 and trips the assert.
Design checklist for refcounted objects
  1. Magic/type field at offset 0, checked on every use. A wrong value means freed or reused, and it lets you find every object of the type in a core.
  2. Poison on free in debug builds: a dead magic plus a fill pattern, and ideally no reuse, so a stale access fails at once and recognizably.
  3. A ref ring buffer: every get and put with its caller and thread, in a side table, dumped when a check fails and readable from a core.
  4. Underflow asserts after every put, so an extra put after the final one aborts on the spot.
Deep dive

Which technique when

TechniqueNeedsGives you
Core forensicsOne core and the exact binaryThe chunk's current owner and a short list of suspect paths
ASanA rebuild and a run that hits the bugThe class, the block, its alloc and free stacks. For refcounts, the last put, not the bad one
Forward watchpointA rerun that behaves the same, and the object known in advanceEvery write to the field, with stacks
rrOne recording of a failing run, and perf countersEvery write, walked back from the crash
Reftrace buildThe design work, done before the bugThe guilty call site, printed at the first stale use

If nothing had reused the chunk

  • The chunk sat in worker 2's tcache. While it stays there (the bin not full, the thread still alive), another thread's 56-byte malloc is served from its own tcache or arena, so only worker 2's next allocation of that size can get it.
  • Had nothing reused it, table_dump would have found the tcache's next and key in magic, refcnt, id and flags, and an intact peer and name. It would have printed a strange line and carried on. A use-after-free that doesn't crash is still there.

Watching more than the count

  • watch -l *(long *)s covers magic and refcnt with one 8-byte debug register. You'd also see the len store, at the cost of more hits on a busy object.
  • Four debug registers is the limit (Ch 7). Watch the one field every bad path must touch: for a refcount bug, the count.
→ Ch 3. The glibc Allocator → Ch 6. Atomics, Locks, Lock Ordering & Refcounting → Ch 7. GDB on a Live Process → Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 13. rr → Ch 24. From a One-Shot Core to a Reproducer → Cheat Sheet

24. From a One-Shot Core to a Reproducer

What you'll learn: how to turn one core into a hypothesis you can test, how to build a harness that makes a rare bug come back, and why rr chaos mode is best at keeping a bad run rather than finding one.

Builds on: reading a core (Ch 9, GDB on a Core), rr and chaos mode (Ch 13, rr), the race (Ch 21, Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag), and the boss level's forensics (Ch 23, Boss Level).

24.1 From a core to a hypothesis

A core answers "what state was the program in?". It never answers "how did it get there?". The bridge is a hypothesis: one path or interleaving that would produce exactly the state you see.

  1. What died, and on what? The frame, the faulting instruction, the object it was using.
  2. What is impossible? A magic that's wrong, a count that's not a count, two totals that should balance and don't, a lock owned by a thread that's waiting for another.
  3. Who can write that? Every writer of the field, every free of the type, every taker of the lock. Search the code, not the core.
  4. One hypothesis. A specific path or interleaving, small enough to test.
  5. A cheap check. Something a test can see long before a crash: an assert, a magic check, a counter.
Core evidenceHypothesisCheap check
A pointer field equal to &obj >> 12 (Ch 19)The object was freed, then readASan, or MALLOC_PERTURB_ with the tcache off
stats.pkts below what was processed (Ch 21)An unlocked read-modify-writeA loop that checks the total; TSan
Two mutex owners, each waiting for the other's lock (Ch 22)Two paths take the locks in opposite ordersTSan or Helgrind on a run that takes both paths
A session's bytes are a pktbuf (Ch 23)An extra put on that sessionThe reftrace build: magic check and ring

24.2 From a hypothesis to a harness

A harness is the smallest program, or test mode, that drives the suspected path over and over and checks after each pass. Then you widen the window the bug needs.

Widen the window

  • Repeat. Thousands of iterations, many processes in parallel. Count distinct outcomes, not just failures.
  • Thread jitter. A random yield, or a random short sleep, at the points the hypothesis names: before the put, between the lookup and the get. It shifts which thread gets there first.
  • sched_yield() injection. A yield inside the suspected window, such as between a load and its store, invites the scheduler to switch threads exactly there.
  • Change the CPU picture. More threads than cores, or everything pinned to fewer cores with taskset. Each gives a different mix of interleavings.
  • rr chaos mode. Randomized priorities and timeslices on a recorded run (24.4).

A jitter hook costs nothing when it's compiled out. An illustrative version, not from the labs: it yields on about one call in eight, with a seed per thread so threads don't all yield in step.

#ifdef STRESS
static __thread unsigned jit_seed;              /* 0 until this thread's first JITTER() */
#define JITTER() do {                                       \
        if (jit_seed == 0)                                  \
            jit_seed = (unsigned)gettid() | 1;              \
        if ((rand_r(&jit_seed) & 7) == 0)                   \
            sched_yield();                                  \
    } while (0)
#else
#define JITTER() ((void)0)
#endif

Make the failure loud and early

  • Assertions at the impossible state: magic checks, count checks. A harness that waits for a SIGSEGV may wait forever: Ch 23's crash needed non-canonical bytes in exactly the right place.
  • MALLOC_PERTURB_: freed memory reads as a fill pattern, not as plausible data. Pair it with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0, or the tcache path skips it (Ch 3).
  • The sanitizer builds: ASan for lifetimes, TSan for races. They turn a silent bad run into a report.
For the boss bug without its barriers: run the reftrace build in a loop, with jitter before every put. The first stale use aborts with the history, whichever thread wins the race that time.

24.3 Watch it: a stress loop

The race lab's plain build, run 200 times. The script counts how often each total appears.

runs=200
      1 actual=2555002
      1 actual=2517622
      1 actual=2505098
      1 actual=2435327
      1 actual=2417413
distinct=200
  1. Line 1: 200 runs.
  2. Lines 2–6: the top of the count list, as uniq -c prints it. Every total was seen once.
  3. Line 7: 200 distinct totals in 200 runs. Every run lost updates, and no two lost the same amount.
  • For this race the harness is just a loop. Four threads in parallel hit the window millions of times per run.
  • A real daemon's window is usually a few instructions on a path that runs a few times a second. That's what the jitter in 24.2 is for.

24.4 Watch it: keeping one bad run

The same lab recorded with rr record --chaos until one recording loses updates, then replayed. rr needed --cap-add SYS_ADMIN on the capture host (Ch 13), which also shows the first tries.

try 27: actual=4000000 lost=0
try 28: actual=4000000 lost=0
try 29: actual=3000000 lost=1000000
⋯
expected=4000000 actual=3000000 lost=1000000
⋯
expected=4000000 actual=3000000 lost=1000000
replay matches lossy recording: actual=3000000
  1. Lines 1–2: tries 27 and 28 came out exact, like the 26 before them. rr runs one thread at a time, so the race that hit every plain run in 24.3 is rare under recording.
  2. Line 3: try 29 lost exactly 1,000,000: one worker's whole count.
  3. The simplest schedule that loses exactly that much: worker A loads the counter, say X, and is switched out before its store. Worker B runs its entire loop: the counter goes to X + 1,000,000. A resumes and stores X + 1. All of B's million is gone; A's increment survives.
  4. Line 5: the recording's own output.
  5. Lines 7–8: rr replay -a on that trace printed the same total. Four rr warnings about the dynamic loader's metadata are trimmed; the replay still matched.
Chaos mode is designed to raise the odds; here it still took 29 tries. What the wait buys is a file. rr pack makes the trace self-contained (Ch 13), so it can sit next to the bug report, move to another machine, and be debugged backwards as often as you need, with the same addresses every time (Ch 23). The harness finds the bug; the recording keeps it.
Deep dive

Inputs or schedules?

  • If the hypothesis is about input, such as a malformed packet or an odd config, fuzz the input: a fuzzer such as libFuzzer or AFL++, with an ASan build.
  • If it's about timing, fuzz the schedule: jitter, yields, chaos mode. Many daemon bugs need both: one odd input, and one bad interleaving.

When it won't come back

  • Keep every core with its exact binary and debug info (Ch 8). Two cores from the same bug often differ in detail, and the details narrow the hypothesis.
  • Ship the cheap checks. A magic check, a ring like PKTD_REFTRACE's, a counter for the impossible state: they make the next production core carry its own history.
  • Record in a test environment that runs the real workload. A single rr recording of a failure is worth any number of guesses.

Proving the fix

  • The harness that made it fail is the test for the fix. Run it several times longer than it took to fail before, and keep it in CI.
  • For races, also run the TSan build of the harness. A fix that only makes the window smaller passes the loop and still gets reported.
→ Ch 9. GDB on a Core → Ch 13. rr → Ch 21. Data Race on a Shared Counter or Flag → Ch 23. Boss Level → Decision Matrix

Decision Matrix

What you'll learn: how to go from what you see (a signal, an abort string, a hang, a number that's wrong) to the likely bug class, the first tool to reach for when it reproduces, and the first move when all you have is a core.

Symptom → class → first move

Each row's first cell is the "Symptom → class" line from its ladder chapter, word for word. Find the line that matches what you see, then take the column for your path.

Symptom → classChPath A: it reproducesPath B: only a core
Symptom → class: SIGSEGV at a small address (< 0x1000), or rip = 0 → NULL dereference14The asan build: UBSan names the NULL member access, ASan the zero-page readbt, p $_siginfo._sifields._sigfault.si_addr, x/i $pc; map the offset to a field with ptype /o
Symptom → class: a value that changes between builds, or reads 0xfefefefe / 0xaaaaaaaa → uninitialized memory15Memcheck --track-origins=yes on the plain build, or the msan buildUsually no core. If the shipped build used -ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern, p/x *s and look for 0xfe (GCC) or 0xaa (Clang) bytes
Symptom → class: SIGABRT inside free() or malloc(): "invalid pointer", "invalid size", "corrupted size" → heap overflow onto the next chunk's header (or freeing a non-heap pointer)16The asan build: heap redzones stop the write itselfmalloc_printerr's str in bt; x/8gx ptr-16 on the freed pointer; read the chunk just below the damaged header
Symptom → class: "stack smashing detected", or a backtrace of ?? frames at ASCII-looking addresses → stack buffer overflow17The asan build: stack redzones catch the copyThe last sane frame in bt; x/16gx $rsp; info symbol on each saved return address
Symptom → class: SIGABRT with "free(): double free detected in tcache 2" → double free18The asan build: the second free, the first free and the allocation, as three stacksThe frame of the second free; who else still holds the pointer; the chunk's tcache key word
Symptom → class: SIGSEGV through a pointer field that equals its own object's address >> 12 → use-after-free19The asan build (alloc, free and use stacks), or Memcheck on the plain buildp/x (unsigned long)obj >> 12 against the field; then list every free of that type
Symptom → class: heap or RSS grows steadily under steady load, with no crash → leak or unbounded growth20LSan (part of the asan build), Memcheck --leak-check=full, Massifgcore the live process; find /w the heap for the type's magic and compare with the table
Symptom → class: counts or state wrong by a different amount on each run → data race21The tsan build; Helgrind or DRD on the plain buildFind a pair of values that should balance and doesn't; list every writer and the lock it holds; build a stress test (Ch 24)
Symptom → class: process alive but stuck, threads parked in futex_wait on mutexes → lock-order deadlock22The tsan build or Helgrind: a lock-order report even on runs that don't hanggcore the hang; thread apply all bt; each mutex's __data.__owner against the waiters' LWPs
Symptom → class: a crash in innocent code, on an object whose magic doesn't match its type → use-after-free with reuse (often a refcount bug)23The asan build, a watch -l on the count, or rr reverse execution from the crashPrint the object as every same-sized type; find who owns the chunk now; list puts without a matching get

Two more patterns

You seeLikely classPath APath B
SIGSEGV where si_addr reads 0, but the pointer isn't NULL: it's eight printable bytesA non-canonical pointer made of data: an overflow, or a freed object reused by another type (Ch 1, Ch 23)The asan buildx/gx the pointer and read it as ASCII, bytes right to left; then read the object as each same-sized type
A TSan or Helgrind lock-order report, but nothing ever hungA latent lock-order deadlock (Ch 22)Check both stacks against your documented lock order; fix the path that breaks itNothing to read until it hangs. Then as above: gcore and mutex owners
No match? Start from the signal. SIGSEGV is a bad access: classify the address with Ch 1's table. SIGABRT means something called abort(): find the message, in malloc_printerr's frame if stderr was lost, and decode it with Ch 3. No signal at all is a hang or a wrong number: Ch 22 or Ch 21.

Sanitizers

Valgrind

Explained in Ch 12. Always on the plain build.

TaskCommand
Memory errorsvalgrind --track-origins=yes --leak-check=full --error-exitcode=1 --num-callers=30 ./prog
Races and lock ordervalgrind --tool=helgrind ./prog
Races, lightervalgrind --tool=drd ./prog
Heap over timevalgrind --tool=massif ./prog, then ms_print massif.out.<pid>
Suppressions--gen-suppressions=all, --suppressions=file
Debug at each errorvalgrind --vgdb-error=0 ./prog; in GDB, target remote | vgdb
glibc's own knobs (no Valgrind)MALLOC_PERTURB_=N with GLIBC_TUNABLES=glibc.malloc.tcache_count=0; MALLOC_CHECK_=3 with LD_PRELOAD=libc_malloc_debug.so.0

rr

Explained in Ch 13. It needs perf counters.

TaskCommand
Recordrr record ./prog args; rr record --chaos ./prog to shake the schedule
Replayrr replay (under GDB), rr replay -a (no debugger), rr replay -p <pid>, rr ps
Move a tracerr pack
Backwardsreverse-continue (rc), reverse-step, reverse-next, reverse-finish, reverse-stepi
Find the last writecontinue to the crash, watch -l s->refcnt, reverse-continue. Going backwards, Old and New swap
Navigatewhen, checkpoint, restart N

Design checklist for refcounted objects

From Ch 23. pktd.h's PKTD_REFTRACE build (make 10-refcount-uaf.reftrace) does all four.

  1. Magic/type field at offset 0, checked on every use. A wrong value means freed or reused, and it lets you find every object of the type in a core.
  2. Poison on free in debug builds: a dead magic plus a fill pattern, and ideally no reuse, so a stale access fails at once and recognizably.
  3. A ref ring buffer: every get and put with its caller and thread, in a side table, dumped when a check fails and readable from a core.
  4. Underflow asserts after every put, so an extra put after the final one aborts on the spot.
→ GDB ↔ LLDB Table → Build-Flag Matrix → Ch 23. Boss Level

Build-Flag Matrix

What you'll learn: the exact build line behind every lab variant, and which tools can share one binary or one run.

Make variants

make <lab>.<variant>. Each line below is what the Makefile's rule runs.

VariantBuildUsed for
plaingcc -O0 -g -fno-omit-frame-pointer -pthreadGDB, cores, Valgrind, rr
asangcc -O0 -g -fno-omit-frame-pointer -pthread -fsanitize=address,undefinedASan, UBSan, LSan (Ch 10)
tsangcc -O1 -g -fsanitize=thread -pthreadTSan (Ch 11)
msanclang -O1 -g -fsanitize=memory -fsanitize-memory-track-origins -pthreadMSan; GCC has none (Ch 15)
patterngcc -O0 -g -fno-omit-frame-pointer -pthread -ftrivial-auto-var-init=patternRecognizable uninitialized locals: GCC fills 0xFE bytes, Clang 0xAA
strippedplain, then objcopy --only-keep-debug → <lab>.debug, strip, objcopy --add-gnu-debuglinkSymbols from a split debug file (Ch 8)
reftracegcc -O0 -g -fno-omit-frame-pointer -pthread -DPKTD_REFTRACE -rdynamicBoss lab only: magic check, poison and ref ring (Ch 23)

The rules themselves:

%.plain: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -o $@ $<

%.asan: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -fsanitize=address,undefined -o $@ $<

%.tsan: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) -O1 -g -fsanitize=thread -pthread -o $@ $<

%.msan: %.c $(HDRS)
	clang -O1 -g -fsanitize=memory -fsanitize-memory-track-origins -pthread -o $@ $<

%.pattern: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern -o $@ $<

%.stripped: %.plain
	cp $< $@
	objcopy --only-keep-debug $@ $*.debug
	strip $@
	objcopy --add-gnu-debuglink=$*.debug $@

%.reftrace: %.c $(HDRS)
	$(CC) $(PLAIN) -DPKTD_REFTRACE -rdynamic -o $@ $<

What combines

One build per tool. The reasons are in Ch 4: each sanitizer reserves its own shadow ranges and brings its own malloc; Valgrind brings its own malloc and manages the whole address space.

ASan (+UBSan, LSan)TSanMSanValgrindrr
ASan—NoNoNoNot tested here
TSanNo—NoNoNot tested here
MSanNoNo—NoNot tested here
ValgrindNoNoNo—Not tested here
rrNot tested hereNot tested hereNot tested hereNot tested here—
  • ASan + UBSan: yes. The labs' asan variant is exactly that. LSan is built into ASan.
  • ASan, TSan, MSan: pick one. Their shadow layouts and allocators collide, so the compiler driver rejects the combination.
  • Sanitizers + Valgrind: no. Two allocators, and a shadow layout Valgrind doesn't expect. Run Valgrind on plain.
  • rr + plain: yes. Every rr capture in this guide recorded a plain build. rr checks nothing itself; it makes a run repeatable. The labs never recorded a sanitized or Valgrind run, so this guide makes no claim either way: try it on your toolchain before you rely on it.
  • GDB works with every variant. To make GDB stop at a sanitizer report, have the runtime raise SIGABRT: ASAN_OPTIONS=abort_on_error=1, or TSAN_OPTIONS=halt_on_error=1:abort_on_error=1. TSan's halt_on_error=1 alone exits with code 66, so GDB only sees the process exit. Or break __sanitizer::Die before run.
→ Ch 4. Instrumentation Models → Ch 10. The ASan Family → Lab Kit

Getting started

How to build the image, run a lab and get a core, and why each docker run flag is there.

README.md — full source
# Memory-debugging lab kit

Small C programs, one per bug class, plus the container and scripts that produced every
terminal capture in the guide. Everything runs on Linux x86-64 inside the `mdg-lab` container.

## Build the image

```sh
docker build -t mdg-lab - < Dockerfile
```

The Dockerfile copies nothing in, so it needs no build context. The base image is pinned by
digest: gcc 13, clang 18 (with its sanitizer runtimes), gdb, valgrind, rr and heaptrack.

## Start a shell in it

From this directory:

```sh
docker run -it --rm --hostname pktd-lab -v "$PWD":/lab -w /lab \
  --ulimit core=0 --cap-add SYS_PTRACE --security-opt seccomp=unconfined \
  mdg-lab bash
```

Add `--cap-add PERFMON` (or `--cap-add SYS_ADMIN`, or `--privileged`) if you want to use rr.
See the flag table below.

## Run a lab

```sh
make 06-uaf.asan && ./06-uaf.asan
```

`make <lab>.<variant>` builds one lab in one variant: `plain`, `asan`, `tsan`, `msan`,
`pattern`, `stripped` or `reftrace`. The `Makefile` header lists the exact flags.

## Get a core

```sh
make core-01-null ARGS=field
```

This runs the lab under GDB and has GDB write `cores/01-null.plain-field.core` at the fault.
Pick another build with `V=<variant>`. The target fails if the program exits normally.
Open the core with `gdb ./01-null.plain cores/01-null.plain-field.core`.

### Why GDB writes the core, not the kernel

- **A container can't change `core_pattern`.** `/proc/sys/kernel/core_pattern` is global to
  the host kernel, not namespaced. Inside a container it is read-only. On a host where it pipes
  to a handler (systemd-coredump, apport and the like), a kernel core from the container goes to
  the *host's* handler. It never lands in your working directory.
- **So the container runs with `--ulimit core=0`.** With a file `core_pattern` the kernel then
  writes no core. With a pipe the kernel ignores the limit: it still starts the host's handler and
  passes it the limit (`%c`). A limit-honouring handler such as systemd-coredump keeps nothing;
  other handlers may not. Either way, no stray core lands in your working directory.
  `generate-core-file` is GDB writing a file through ptrace, so the limit doesn't apply to it.

## `docker run` flags

| Flag | Why |
|---|---|
| `--rm` | Throw the container away after each run; state lives only in `/lab`. |
| `--hostname pktd-lab` | Fixed, neutral hostname, so captures never show the real machine's name. |
| `-v "$PWD":/lab -w /lab` | Work on this directory in place; paths in captures read `/lab/...`. |
| `--ulimit core=0` | No file cores; a limit-honouring pipe handler keeps nothing, though it still runs (see above). GDB-written cores still work. |
| `--cap-add SYS_PTRACE` | Lets gdb attach to running processes and lets ASan/LSan inspect threads. |
| `--security-opt seccomp=unconfined` | Allows `personality()` (gdb disables ASLR) and the perf/ptrace calls rr needs. |
| `--cap-add PERFMON` / `SYS_ADMIN` / `--privileged` | Only for rr: it needs `perf_event_open`. Use the weakest one that works on your host. |

## Captures (maintainers)

`captures.tsv` lists each capture: `id`, the source files it depends on, and the shell command.
`run-capture.sh [id …]` runs rows in the container and writes `captures/<id>.txt`. Each file
starts with `# src <file> <sha256>` and `# cmd <command>` header lines. `check.sh [id …]` matches
each capture against its regexes in `expect.tsv`.

`remote.sh [id …]` drives all of it on a remote Docker host from your workstation. Set
`LAB_HOST=user@host` first. It syncs this directory to `~/mdg-lab/`, builds the image, captures,
checks, syncs `captures/` back and runs the repo's leak gate over them.
`remote.sh --rr-probe` finds the weakest flag that lets `rr record` work on that host. It records
the result in `.rr-caps`, a local file that is not committed.

<!-- cspell:words apport asan coredump heaptrack msan perfmon pktd reftrace seccomp tsan valgrind -->

The capture pipeline

For maintainers, or for anyone who wants to regenerate every terminal block. captures.tsv says what to run. run-capture.sh runs it inside the container and stamps each source's SHA-256 at the top of the capture. check.sh matches the result against expect.tsv. remote.sh does all of it on a remote Docker host named by LAB_HOST.

run-capture.sh — full source
#!/bin/bash
# run-capture.sh — run rows of captures.tsv and write captures/<id>.txt (inside the mdg-lab container).
# usage: ./run-capture.sh [id ...]        (no ids = every row)
#
# Each capture file is:
#   # src <file> <sha256>     one line per source listed in the row ("-" = none)
#   # cmd <command>
#   <combined stdout+stderr of the command>
# C0 control bytes other than \t \n \r are stripped, so ANSI colour and stray
# binary bytes never reach the guide. The command's exit status is ignored:
# check.sh decides whether a capture is good.
set -u
cd "$(dirname "$0")" || exit 2

TSV=captures.tsv
TIMEOUT=${CAP_TIMEOUT:-300}   # seconds per capture, so a hung lab cannot stall a run

rows() { grep -v -e '^#' -e '^[[:space:]]*$' "$TSV"; }

# Reject unknown ids before running anything.
for want in "$@"; do
  rows | cut -f1 | grep -qxF -- "$want" || { echo "run-capture: unknown id: $want" >&2; exit 2; }
done

mkdir -p captures
while IFS=$'\t' read -r id srcs cmd; do
  if [ $# -gt 0 ]; then
    hit=0; for want in "$@"; do [ "$want" = "$id" ] && hit=1; done
    [ $hit -eq 1 ] || continue
  fi
  out=captures/$id.txt
  {
    if [ "$srcs" != "-" ]; then
      IFS=',' read -ra files <<< "$srcs"
      for f in "${files[@]}"; do
        [ -f "$f" ] || { echo "run-capture: $id: source not found: $f" >&2; exit 2; }
        echo "# src $f $(sha256sum "$f" | cut -d' ' -f1)"
      done
    fi
    echo "# cmd $cmd"
    timeout -k 5 "$TIMEOUT" bash -c "$cmd" < /dev/null 2>&1 | tr -d '\000-\010\013\014\016-\037'
  } > "$out" || exit 2
  echo "captured $id"
done < <(rows)

check.sh — full source
#!/bin/bash
# check.sh — match captures/<id>.txt against expect.tsv (inside the mdg-lab container).
# usage: ./check.sh [id ...]              (no ids = every id in expect.tsv)
# Prints PASS <id> or FAIL <id>: <reason> per id; exits 1 on any miss.
# Regexes are POSIX ERE and see only the capture body, never the # src / # cmd header.
set -u
cd "$(dirname "$0")" || exit 2

TSV=expect.tsv
exp() { grep -v -e '^#' -e '^[[:space:]]*$' "$TSV"; }

if [ $# -eq 0 ]; then
  mapfile -t ids < <(exp | cut -f1 | awk '!seen[$0]++')
else
  ids=("$@")
fi

rc=0
for id in "${ids[@]}"; do
  f=captures/$id.txt
  if [ ! -f "$f" ]; then echo "FAIL $id: no captures/$id.txt"; rc=1; continue; fi
  body=$(awk 'h && /^# (src|cmd) /{next} {h=0; print}' h=1 "$f")
  n=0; miss=0
  while IFS=$'\t' read -r eid re; do
    [ "$eid" = "$id" ] || continue
    n=$((n + 1))
    printf '%s\n' "$body" | grep -Eq -- "$re" || { echo "FAIL $id: missing /$re/"; miss=1; }
  done < <(exp)
  if [ $n -eq 0 ]; then echo "FAIL $id: no expectations in $TSV"; miss=1; fi
  if [ $miss -eq 0 ]; then echo "PASS $id"; else rc=1; fi
done
exit $rc
remote.sh — full source
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# remote.sh — run lab captures on a remote x86-64 Linux Docker host. Runs on your workstation.
# usage: LAB_HOST=user@host ./remote.sh [capture-id ...]   (no ids = every row of captures.tsv)
#        CAP_TIMEOUT=<s> raises run-capture.sh's per-capture timeout (default 300 s)
#        LAB_HOST=user@host ./remote.sh --rr-probe         (decide RR_CAPS, write .rr-caps)
#
# Capture run, in order:
#   1. rsync this directory to ~/mdg-lab/ on the host
#   2. docker build -t mdg-lab (the Dockerfile copies nothing, so no build context is sent)
#   3. run-capture.sh <ids> && check.sh <ids> in the container ($RR_CAPS from .rr-caps)
#   4. chown /lab back to the host user (uid/gid expanded by the host's shell)
#   5. rsync captures/ back
#   6. leak-gate.sh over every capture, here (the container has no repo, git or term list)
# Only the host's sudo-free Docker is used; nothing on the host is reconfigured.
set -euo pipefail
: "${LAB_HOST:?set LAB_HOST=user@host}"
cd "$(dirname "$0")"

SSH=(ssh -o BatchMode=yes)
RSH="ssh -o BatchMode=yes"
RDIR=mdg-lab
# Expanded by the host's shell after "cd mdg-lab", so $PWD is the host's lab copy.
BASE='--rm --hostname pktd-lab -v "$PWD":/lab -w /lab --ulimit core=0 --cap-add SYS_PTRACE --security-opt seccomp=unconfined'
# Built binaries and cores live only on the host: never uploaded, never deleted by --delete.
KEEP=(--exclude 'cores/' --exclude '*.plain' --exclude '*.asan' --exclude '*.tsan' --exclude '*.msan'
      --exclude '*.pattern' --exclude '*.stripped' --exclude '*.reftrace' --exclude '*.debug')

up()    { rsync -az --delete -e "$RSH" "${KEEP[@]}" ./ "$LAB_HOST:$RDIR/"; }
build() { "${SSH[@]}" "$LAB_HOST" "cd $RDIR && docker build -q -t mdg-lab - < Dockerfile"; }

rr_probe() {
  up; build
  local label flags
  for pair in 'perfmon|--cap-add PERFMON' 'sys_admin|--cap-add SYS_ADMIN' 'privileged|--privileged'; do
    label=${pair%%|*}; flags=${pair#*|}
    echo "== rr probe: $label ($flags)"
    if "${SSH[@]}" "$LAB_HOST" "cd $RDIR && docker run $BASE $flags mdg-lab rr record -n /bin/true"; then
      printf '%s\n%s\n' "$label" "$flags" > .rr-caps
      echo "rr-smoke($label)"; return 0
    fi
  done
  printf 'none\n\n' > .rr-caps
  echo "rr-smoke(none)"
}

if [ "${1:-}" = "--rr-probe" ]; then rr_probe; exit 0; fi

for id in "$@"; do
  [[ $id =~ ^[A-Za-z0-9._-]+$ ]] || { echo "remote.sh: bad capture id: $id" >&2; exit 2; }
done
ids="$*"
caps=$(sed -n 2p .rr-caps 2>/dev/null || true)

up; build
set +e
"${SSH[@]}" "$LAB_HOST" "cd $RDIR && docker run $BASE $caps ${CAP_TIMEOUT:+-e CAP_TIMEOUT=$CAP_TIMEOUT} mdg-lab bash -c './run-capture.sh $ids && ./check.sh $ids'"
run_rc=$?
"${SSH[@]}" "$LAB_HOST" 'cd mdg-lab && docker run --rm -v "$PWD":/lab mdg-lab chown -R "$(id -u):$(id -g)" /lab'
set -e
mkdir -p captures
rsync -az -e "$RSH" "$LAB_HOST:$RDIR/captures/" captures/

gate=$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/scripts/leak-gate.sh
set +e
"$gate" captures/*.txt
leak_rc=$?
set -e
[ $leak_rc -eq 0 ] || { echo "remote.sh: leak gate failed (exit $leak_rc)" >&2; exit 1; }
exit $run_rc
→ Welcome → Build-Flag Matrix

Glossary A–Z

What you'll learn: one or two sentences on every term the guide leans on, with the chapter that explains it properly.

A–F

TermMeaning
A bits / V bitsMemcheck's shadow state: an A bit says whether a byte is addressable, a V bit whether a bit is defined (Ch 4).
All-stop modeGDB's default: when one thread stops, every thread stops, and they all resume together (Ch 7).
ArenaA malloc heap with its own bins. The main arena uses the brk heap; other threads get mmap'd arenas, whose chunks carry the A bit (Ch 3).
ASanAddressSanitizer: compile-time instrumentation with shadow memory, redzones and quarantine. Finds overflows, use-after-free and double free (Ch 10).
ASLRAddress-space layout randomization: the heap, stacks, libraries and a PIE binary move on every run. GDB turns it off for programs it starts (Ch 1).
BinsThe allocator's free lists: tcache, fastbins, the unsorted bin, small bins and large bins (Ch 3).
Build IDA hash in the .note.gnu.build-id note. It ties a core, a binary and a debug file to one exact build (Ch 8).
CanaryA random value the stack protector places between a frame's locals and its saved registers. A changed canary at return aborts with "stack smashing detected" (Ch 17).
CFACanonical frame address: the value of rsp just before the call that made the frame. Unwind rules describe every saved register relative to it (Ch 2).
Chaos moderr record --chaos: randomized thread priorities and timeslices, so rare schedules come up more often under recording (Ch 13).
Chunkmalloc's unit of memory: a 16-byte header (prev_size, then size with flag bits), then user data (Ch 3).
Core fileAn ELF file of type CORE: each thread's registers, the fatal signal, and the process's writable memory at the moment it was taken (Ch 9).
core_patternThe host-wide kernel setting that says where cores go: a file template, or a | pipe to a handler. Containers can't change it (Ch 8).
Data raceTwo accesses to the same memory from different threads, at least one a write and not both atomic, with no happens-before between them. Undefined behaviour in C (Ch 5).
debuginfodAn HTTP service that serves debug files, binaries and sources by build ID. GDB queries it when DEBUGINFOD_URLS is set (Ch 8).
DebuglinkA .gnu_debuglink section naming a separate debug file, with its CRC (Ch 8).
Direct / indirect leakLSan's terms: a direct leak is a block nothing points to; an indirect one is reachable only from leaked blocks (Ch 10).
DRDA Valgrind tool for data races and pthread misuse, lighter than Helgrind, without lock-order checking (Ch 12).
DWARFThe debug-info format: types, variable locations, line tables and unwind rules (Ch 2).
FastbinA per-arena LIFO list of small free chunks, singly linked and safe-linked like the tcache (Ch 3).
Frame pointerrbp, kept as a chain of saved frame bases. Fast to unwind; -fno-omit-frame-pointer keeps it (Ch 2).
FutexThe kernel wait primitive under a glibc mutex. A thread blocked on a contended lock waits in futex_wait (Ch 6).

G–P

TermMeaning
gcoreWrites a core of a running process, which then carries on. GDB's generate-core-file does the same from inside a session (Ch 8).
General-protection faultWhat the CPU raises for a non-canonical address. The kernel can't report the address, so si_addr reads 0 (Ch 1).
Guard pageAn inaccessible page below a thread's stack. A stack overflow hits it and becomes a SIGSEGV (Ch 1).
Happens-beforeThe order the C memory model guarantees: program order within a thread, plus synchronizes-with edges (unlock to lock, release to acquire) between threads, chained transitively (Ch 5).
Hardware watchpointA watchpoint held in one of x86-64's four debug registers, each covering up to 8 aligned bytes. It traps after the writing instruction, in any thread, at full speed (Ch 7).
HelgrindA Valgrind tool for data races, pthread misuse and lock-order inversions. Its "Locks held" line shows which side forgot the lock (Ch 12).
HWASanHardware-assisted ASan, mainly on AArch64: pointer tags and memory tags compared on every access. Less memory than ASan; detection is probabilistic (Ch 10).
Lock-order inversionTwo paths that take the same two locks in opposite orders. It can deadlock; TSan and Helgrind report it even on runs that don't (Ch 6).
LSanLeakSanitizer: at exit, scans memory for pointers into the heap and reports blocks nothing points to. Built into ASan (Ch 10).
LWPLight-weight process: the kernel's thread ID, as GDB shows it. A glibc mutex's __owner holds one (Ch 22).
Magic numberA known constant at offset 0 of an object, such as SESSION_MAGIC. A wrong value means freed or reused; it also lets you find every object of the type in a core (Ch 23).
MassifA Valgrind tool that profiles heap size over time, and which call sites hold it (Ch 12).
MemcheckValgrind's default tool: invalid accesses, use of uninitialized values, bad frees and leaks, on an unmodified binary (Ch 12).
MSanMemorySanitizer, Clang only: one shadow bit per bit, reporting when an uninitialized value decides a branch, an address or a syscall argument (Ch 15).
Non-canonical addressAn x86-64 address whose bits 63–47 aren't all equal. Eight ASCII bytes used as a pointer usually are (Ch 1).
Non-stop modeset non-stop on: GDB stops only the thread that hit an event; the others keep running (Ch 7).
Path A / Path BThis guide's two modes. A: it reproduces, so rebuild with a tool and rerun. B: only a core, so do forensics in GDB (The Map).
Pattern init-ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern: fill locals at declaration so uninitialized reads are recognizable. GCC uses 0xFE bytes, Clang 0xAA (Ch 15).
PIEPosition-independent executable: the binary itself can load at a random base, so its addresses are offsets until it runs (Ch 1).
Poison on freeOverwrite a dead object with a dead magic and a fill pattern, and ideally never reuse it, so a stale access fails at once (Ch 23).

Q–Z

TermMeaning
QuarantineASan's FIFO of freed blocks, held poisoned before reuse, 256 MB by default on 64-bit. A stale pointer hits poison long after the free (Ch 4).
Race conditionA bug that depends on the order of correctly synchronized operations, such as check, unlock, re-lock, use. Not a data race, so TSan can't see it (Ch 5).
Red zone (ABI)The 128 bytes below rsp that a leaf function may use without moving rsp. Not the same thing as an ASan redzone (Ch 2).
Redzone (ASan)Poisoned padding around each heap block, stack array and global. An overflow lands in it and is reported (Ch 4).
RefcountA count of references to a shared object. Each get pairs with exactly one put; the put that reaches 0 frees it (Ch 6).
Ref ring bufferA side table recording each get and put with its caller and thread, dumped when a check fails. The boss lab's PKTD_REFTRACE build has one per session (Ch 23).
Reverse executionRunning backwards to the previous breakpoint or watchpoint hit. rr does it by restoring a checkpoint and replaying forward (Ch 13).
rrA record-and-replay debugger: record once, then replay the identical run under GDB, forwards and backwards. It needs perf counters (Ch 13).
Safe-linkingSince glibc 2.32, tcache and fastbin next pointers are stored as (pos >> 12) ^ next. In a bin that was empty, the stored word is the field's own address >> 12 (Ch 3).
Scheduler-lockingGDB's setting for which threads run when you resume: off, step, on or replay (Ch 7).
Shadow memoryA sanitizer's metadata about application memory. For ASan, one byte per 8 bytes, at (addr >> 3) + 0x7fff8000 on x86-64 Linux (Ch 4).
SIGABRTSignal 6: something called abort(), usually glibc's allocator checks, the stack protector or an assert. A shell shows exit status 134 (Ch 9).
SIGSEGVSignal 11: a bad memory access. A shell shows exit status 139 (Ch 9).
Split debug infoDebug info moved into a separate .debug file so the shipped binary can be stripped (Ch 8).
Stripped binaryA binary without its symbol table and DWARF. Only .dynsym remains, so internal functions show as ?? (Ch 8).
Tcacheglibc's per-thread cache of free chunks: 64 bins, LIFO, 7 chunks per bin by default, no lock. A thread's next same-size malloc gets back the chunk it just freed (Ch 3).
Tcache keyA random per-process value glibc writes into the second word of a chunk's user data when it enters the tcache. Finding it again on free flags a double free (Ch 18).
Top chunkThe free space at the end of an arena's heap, split when no bin fits (Ch 3).
TSanThreadSanitizer: vector clocks and shadow cells that report data races and lock-order inversions, even when the race didn't bite (Ch 11).
UBSanUndefinedBehaviorSanitizer: checks language rules such as NULL member access, signed overflow and bad shifts. Cheap, and combines with ASan (Ch 10).
Use-after-freeReading or writing an object after it was freed. It may crash at once, much later, or never, depending on who reuses the chunk (Ch 19).
Vector clockOne logical time per thread, carried by each thread and lock. TSan compares them to decide whether two accesses are ordered (Ch 4).
watch -lA watchpoint that evaluates the address once and watches that memory in any frame or thread. What you want for a heap field (Ch 7).
→ The Map → Further Reading
